## Scene 2: the road to Trafalgar

Facts for scene 2, from the invasion camps at Boulogne to the fleets' positions at dawn on 21 October: Villeneuve's run to the West Indies and back with Nelson in pursuit, Calder's action off Cape Finisterre, Ferrol and Cádiz, Napoleon's turn east to Ulm, Nelson's return to the fleet in September and his watch on Cádiz, the sortie, the signals and the names on the chart. They come from the research notes in `research/scene-02/` (`h1-campaign.md`, `h2-boulogne-cadiz.md`, `h3-signals.md`, `h4-names.md` and `h5-names.md` for the names, `h6-september.md` for Nelson's passage of September, the notes in `session-17/` for the bay of Cádiz on 19 October and the small craft with the fleets, `session-21/strengths-by-date.md` for the strengths lettered by date, and `session-32/army-march.md` for the army's march to Ulm), their independent checks (`v-*.md`) and the adjudications of every contested verdict (`a-*.md`), which are the final word on the points they rule on; the checks of this section as a whole and the rulings on them are `v-merged-*.md` and `a-v-merged-*.md`. `research/scene-02/README.md` says what each file is. The confidence scale is the one at the top of this file. Boulogne itself in August 1805 (the roads and the moored line, the flotilla's kinds, the drying harbour, the camps' huts and plan, the town and its defences, Napoleon's pavilion, the Royal Navy's watch) is in the section "Scene 2: Boulogne from the sea (beat 1)", called "Boulogne from the sea" below; this section points to its subsections and does not repeat them.

Dates are civil, times are ship's times as logged, and bearings are by compass unless marked true; "Dates and conventions" below gives the rules. Positions are in degrees and minutes, distances in nautical miles (nm), with leagues (3 nm) where a source uses them; kilometres measure the drawing's own offsets and sizes on the chart (ships eased apart, the flotilla's lines, glyphs kept off the shore) and the army's road on land. NRS II is *Logs of the Great Sea Fights* vol. II (Navy Records Society, 1900), printed by civil day.

### Where the sources changed the brief

| Brief | Sources | Scene | Confidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Villeneuve breaks out of Toulon on 29 or 30 March | He weighed at about 4 p.m. on 30 March (Reille, the Atlas's log, his letter of 9 germinal; Nelson: "in the night of Saturday, March 30th"). 29 March is James's slip in converting the Republican date, copied by Clowes | 30 March 1805 | High |
| He collects Spanish ships at Cádiz and crosses to Martinique | A stop of about six hours on the night of 9–10 April (Corbett: under four hours). Of 18 ships of the line nominally together, 14 crossed in company; three Spaniards met him at Martinique on 14 May and the San Rafael on the 16th | 14 glyphs on the crossing, 17 and then 18 at Martinique | High |
| He sails back for Europe around 9 to 11 June | He left Fort-de-France on 5 June, took a convoy off Barbuda on the 8th and turned for Europe on the 10th; his letter is dated at sea on the 11th; James has "the 9th or 10th" | The route goes north through the islands; a day circle lettered "10" at the turn (after "6 June" off Dominica), and 10 June on the date line | High for 5 and 8 June, medium for 10 June |
| Beat 2: "races back for the Channel"; Nelson "follows him across the Atlantic and back" | Decrès's dispatch of 29 April, with the Emperor's orders of 14 April (both brought by the Didon), and Villeneuve's decision of 10 June sent him back to Ferrol first, to gather the ships there before Brest and the Channel (Desbrière 1907, pp. 30–34, 41–42). Homeward the tracks parted: Villeneuve went north of the whole Azores group for Ferrol; Nelson went south of the western and central Azores and through the group, north of São Miguel, to Cape St Vincent and Gibraltar (19 July), then north to Ushant (15 August) and Spithead (18 August), and never caught up. He sailed again from St Helens on 15 September and joined the fleet off Cádiz on the 28th | Villeneuve's line heads for Ferrol; Nelson's runs south of it to Gibraltar, and on in beat 3 to Ushant and Spithead and back to Cádiz in September (the owner's decision, 5 November 2026); the captions can keep the plan's aim, and "across the Atlantic and back" holds only loosely, since Nelson came home by another track | High |
| Calder intercepts Villeneuve off Cape Finisterre, 22 July | The action was 95–130 nm WNW of the cape; Calder's noon was 43°30′ N 11°17′ W | Smoke at 43°35′ N 11°40′ W, 113 nm WNW of the cape (planned, not drawn yet: see "How the chart shows the signals") | Medium |
| He reaches A Coruña and Ferrol around 1 August | He put into Vigo first (28 July), doubled Cape Finisterre on the evening of 1 August and entered the bay of A Coruña on the 2nd; James, Mahan and Corbett's narrative have the 1st, Corbett's dating of the junction the 2nd | The route goes by Vigo; a day circle lettered "2" at A Coruña (after "31 July" at Vigo), and 2 August on the date line | High for Vigo, medium for 2 August |
| He sails south to Cádiz, arriving around 20 August | He left Ferrol on 13 August with 29 of the line and stood WNW for two days, turning south only at nightfall on 15 August; Cádiz on the evening of 20 August. His orders of 16 July allowed him Cádiz after a defeat, a separation or a change in the situation, and his letter of 22 August rests the turn on them (Desbrière 1907, pp. 81, 112 n.) | The line runs out WNW and bends south at about 43°45′ N 12°20′ W; no day circles from Ferrol to Cádiz: the date line reads 15 August at the bend and 20 August at Cádiz | High for the dates, low for the turning point |
| The army at Boulogne in rows of tiny tents | From the autumn of 1803 the troops lived in huts built for the winter, in long lines parallel to the sea, cut by cross streets between battalions; tents were campaign kit ("Boulogne from the sea", "The camps") | Rows of small huts along camp streets | High |
| A flotilla of invasion barges in the harbour | Rigged gun craft, with fishing and merchant craft as transports ("Boulogne from the sea", "Rigged gun craft, not barges"); 1,104 at Boulogne on 20 July 1805 (its "How many"). On the chart's coastline the Liane basin is land | Twenty rigged craft in the roads off Boulogne, Wimereux and Ambleteuse, each standing for a division of the flotilla (a chart's convention: "Assumptions and licences", 6); the caption says "invasion craft" (the owner's decision, 5 November 2026) | High for the designs, medium for the rigs as built and for the count (right for 20 July 1805), low for the layout in August 1805 |
| Napoleon abandons the invasion; beat 3: "gives up on England" | On 23 August he had Marmont told that if the fleet were held at Cádiz he would put the crossing off "à une autre année" (Corr. XI No. 9120), and he left troops and the flotilla at Boulogne as a standing threat. He decided on 23–26 August, before he knew Villeneuve was in Cádiz | The caption may stand ("puts off" is nearer his words); Cádiz may come before the turn east, never as its cause | High |
| On 20 October an Austrian army under Mack surrenders at Ulm | The capitulation was signed on 17 October; on 20 October the garrison, about 25,000, marched out and laid down its arms before Napoleon | 20 October on the date line; "lays down its arms" is exact, "surrenders" defensible; never 19 October | High for the dates, medium for the number |
| Beat 4: the combined fleet "in the harbour" | From 9–10 October it lay in the roadstead at the harbour mouth, out of range of the city's guns, with its five frigates and three brigs; the Fougueux is the one ship recorded still in the narrows, until the evening of the 19th | 32 of the line at anchor north-east of the entrance shoals and the Fougueux alone in the narrows, with 5 frigates and 3 brigs (see "Positions on the chart") | High for the roadstead and the narrows, medium for the layout, low for the coordinates |
| Beat 4 caption: "linked to the coast by a chain of frigates" | Frigates held the Cádiz end: the Euryalus and Sirius off the port, the Phoebe and Naiad sent out to repeat; the links beyond them were ships of the line (Defence, Colossus, Mars, with the Agamemnon stationed by the Defence) (NRS II 145–146; diary of 14 October, Hudson 1917, p. 21; Beatty 1807, p. 6) | Caption: "a chain of frigates and ships of the line" (the owner's decision, 1 November 2026); frigate glyphs inshore, ships of the line beyond | High |
| The chain "lights up ship by ship when the combined fleet sails" | The relay of 19 October ran in daylight, 7 to about 9.30, with flags and guns (the Phoebe's three-minute guns; a ship "firing guns" seen from the fleet); Popham's telegraph carried the Sirius's first report at 6.04 and later messages along the chain, from 10.20; counted guns with each hoist are logged only on the 20th. Only seven of the line got out that day. Lights, rockets and blue lights belong to the nights of 19–20 and 20–21 October, and the night chain was a different, short chain between the closing fleets | Planned, not drawn yet (see "How the chart shows the signals"): by day each link shows a hoist and white gun puffs, in pulses, never instant; "by half past nine" or "some two and a half hours later", never "in minutes" or "within two hours"; light effects only in the night beat | High for the sequence, medium for the duration |

### Dates and conventions

- **Civil and nautical days.** Dates here are civil, midnight to midnight. British logs of 1805 kept the nautical day, which began at noon of the civil day before, so a log's afternoon of "the 10th" is the civil 9th. Nicolas prints the Victory's log in civil order and corrects it where he noticed ("March 27th [i.e. 26th]", Nicolas VI 374); the Sally's "Sunday 16th June, P.M. 7" is civil Saturday 15 June (Nicolas VI 474). James and Clowes give some British events by the log: Gibraltar 7 May (civil 6), Lagos 10 May (9), Knight's arrival 12 May (11), Antigua 13 June (12), Tetuan "24th at noon" (23). High.
- **The Republican calendar.** French documents are dated by it. Year XIII began on 23 September 1804 and year XIV on 23 September 1805. 1 germinal XIII is 22 March 1805, 1 floréal 21 April, 1 prairial 21 May, 1 messidor 20 June, 1 thermidor 20 July and 1 fructidor 19 August, so 9 germinal is 30 March, 24 floréal 14 May, 16 prairial 5 June, 14 thermidor 2 August and 27 thermidor 15 August; 29 vendémiaire XIV is 21 October. High (computed; Desbrière's own glosses agree).
- **James's one-day shift.** James converted Republican dates himself and came out a day early. Only the dates he took from French documents are affected: Toulon 29 March (for 30), Cartagena 6 April (7), Gibraltar and Cádiz 8 April (9), under way "on the 9th" (10), Martinique sighted 12 May and reached 13 May (13, 14), Martinique left 4 June (5), Vigo left 30 July (31). Clowes copies them. Other dates in his French narrative are right: the San Rafael at Martinique on 16 May, Diamond Rock 29 May to 2 June (the expedition sailed on the 29th and opened the attack on the 31st, III 349), Basse-Terre 6 June, the convoy 8 June, the five frigates rejoining on 30 June (the place he gives, about 20 leagues NE of Corvo, is doubtful; see "Open doubts"); his 9 July for the gale is his conversion of the Moniteur letter's "21 messidor" (10 July) and matches Reille's 20 messidor (9 July) only by chance (James III 351, 353). Two are two days early (Vigo reached 26 July for 28, out of Ferrol 11 August for 13), and his A Coruña on 1 August is tied to the British being blown off station that morning, so it is not simply his slip. No James date can be corrected mechanically (`research/scene-02/a-h1ac.md`, H1-02b). High.
- **The Atlas's tables.** Desbrière prints the 74 Atlas's noon positions in two tables. In the first (Toulon to Cádiz, p. 9) a row labelled N germinal gives the noon position of N+1 germinal (the label is the day on which that noon-to-noon run began), so "18 germinal", the Strait, is 9 April; the frigates' sightings and Orde's letters confirm it. Whether the second (the crossing, p. 26 n.) follows the same rule is disputed, so the crossing gets no day marks after the noon of 10 April, table A's last row (`research/scene-02/a-h1ac.md`, H1-07). His 7.45 in the morning for the anchoring off Cádiz is a slip for the evening. High for the first table, low for the second.
- **Positions, leagues and times.** French longitudes, reckoned from Paris (2°20′14″ E of Greenwich), are converted to Greenwich. A sea league is 3 nm. Mid-ocean positions are dead reckoning: at the Martinique landfall the French reckoning was 36 leagues too far east, at Finisterre about 2° too far west. Times are ship's times as logged (local apparent time). High.
- **Bearings.** Logs and letters give bearings by compass unless they say "true". The variation in 1805 (NOAA's gufm1 model) was about 20° W off Toulon, 18½° W south of Sardinia, 21° W off Cádiz, Cape St Vincent and São Miguel, 22° W off the Rock of Lisbon, 24° W at the Finisterre action, 25° W off Ushant and 24–25° W in the Channel, but 2½–3° E in the Lesser Antilles; with west variation, true = magnetic − variation. Positions worked from bearings say which reading they use. The Victory's own observations of mid-August 1805, 22°35′–23°15′ W between longitudes 11° and 18° W (Corbett 1910, p. 261 n.), lie 1–3½° below the model there. High for the model, medium for each worked position.

### Toulon to the West Indies, 30 March to 11 June

- **The plan.** Napoleon's instructions of 2 March 1805 sent Villeneuve, with his 11 of the line, from Toulon to Cádiz to collect the Aigle and the Spanish ships ready there, and on to Martinique to wait 40 days for Ganteaume. Ganteaume was to come out of Brest with 21 of the line, attack the seven or eight British ships off Ferrol, collect Gourdon's four and the Spanish ships there and cross to the West Indies; joined there by Villeneuve and Missiessy, about 40 of the line were to come back by Ushant and make straight for Boulogne. If Ganteaume did not come, Villeneuve was to land 1,300 troops, cruise off the Canaries for the India convoys and go back to Cádiz (Desbrière 1907, pp. 4–5). Ganteaume never got out: he anchored in Bertheaume roads on 27 March, the British appeared in strength, and under orders not to attack he went back into Brest, on the day, Desbrière writes, that Villeneuve sailed (p. 5). The Emperor's new orders of 13–14 April, sent on the 17th with Magon, told Villeneuve to wait about a month (35 days) after Magon's arrival, taking what British islands he could, then return to Ferrol, collect the ships there, free Ganteaume at Brest and enter the Channel with more than 50 of the line; Decrès's dispatch of 29 April, with a postscript of 1 May, pressed the attack on the islands and the return to Ferrol in any case. The Didon carried both and reached Martinique first, on 30 May (pp. 30–34; the other dates of her arrival are under "At Fort-de-France"). High.
- **Toulon, 30 March.** Villeneuve weighed at about 4 p.m. on 30 March 1805 and was a league south-east of Cap Sépet by 7 p.m. (Reille and the Atlas's log, Desbrière 1907, pp. 7, 9; his letter of 9 germinal). Nelson's frigates Active and Phoebe saw him at 8 a.m. on the 31st (10 a.m. in his note to Ball, Nicolas VI 400; James, III 339, and Clowes have the afternoon), ten leagues south of Cape Sicié, steering SSW (Nicolas VI 396–398). Nelson dated the sailing "in the night of Saturday, March 30th"; the "31st" of two other letters is the sighting. "29 March" is James's slip (III 339), copied by Clowes. The scene uses 30 March. High.
- **His force.** 11 of the line, four 80s and seven 74s (Bucentaure, Formidable, Neptune, Indomptable; Pluton, Mont-Blanc, Berwick, Atlas, Swiftsure, Scipion, Intrépide), six frigates and the brigs Furet and Naïade (Reille's "Pléiade" is an error), with about 3,070 troops under Lauriston (Desbrière 1907, pp. 6–7, 27). The British frigates counted seven frigates; James has 3,500 troops. High for the ships, medium for the troops.
- **Inside the Balearics.** He meant to pass east of the Balearics, but on 1 April a merchant ship, Ragusan by James and Corbett, told him Nelson was south of Sardinia, and he went between the islands and Spain. He lay becalmed off Cartagena on the morning of 7 April; Salcedo's six Spanish ships there did not join. A fresh easterly took him past Cabo de Gata that night (Desbrière 1907, p. 8; Corbett 1910, pp. 56, 61); a neutral's report to the Leviathan, and Nelson's order to Capel of 18 April, have him off the cape on the 7th (James III 341; Nicolas VI 408). High.
- **The Strait, 9 April.** The Rock and Ape's Hill were in sight in the morning, and at about 9 a.m. the fleet entered the Strait in two columns, frigates ahead (Desbrière 1907, p. 9). Every British document written at or near the scene gives the 9th: Orde's two letters of 10 April, Gardner's of 22 April, the Admiralty's order of 25 April and the Beagle's sighting off Cape Spartel; so do the American consuls at Cádiz and Lisbon. "8 April" goes back to a Ragusan's report that reached Nelson off Toro on 18 April; he passed it to the Admiralty that day, and his order to Capel of the same day has the enemy off Cabo de Gata on the 7th and through the Strait the day after (Nicolas VI 408). Nicolas's chronology, Mahan (for Gibraltar) and Adkin carry it; James gives it both as the news that reached Nelson (III 341) and in his French narrative, where it is his one-day slip (III 342), and Clowes copies him. The scene uses 9 April. High.
- **Orde.** Sir John Orde lay off Cádiz with five of the line: Glory 98, Defence 74 and the 64s Polyphemus, Agamemnon and Ruby. Strachan's Renown, which had seen the French at daybreak off Tarifa, joined him about midday with the warning, and the six drew off towards Lagos; the French frigates signalled them about 4 p.m. (Corbett 1910, pp. 62–63; Desbrière 1907, p. 17 n.). High.
- **Cádiz, 9–10 April.** The French anchored outside the bay at nightfall on 9 April, about 7.45–8 p.m., and were under sail again by about 2 a.m. on the 10th, a stop of about six hours (Desbrière 1907, pp. 9–11; Corbett 1910, p. 68, has under four hours). Out to them came the French Aigle 74, the corvette Torche and the brig Argus, and Gravina with Argonauta 80, San Rafael 80, Firme 74, Terrible 74, América 64, España 64 and the frigate Santa Magdalena, with 1,930 Spanish troops (Decrès's return; James: 1,600). The scene anchors the French in open water 3.2 nm west of Las Puercas, an estimate, where `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` gives 12–16 m of water today (see "Routes on the chart"). High for the times, low for the position.
- **Fourteen crossed together.** The San Rafael grounded coming out. At dawn on 10 April only the Argonauta was with the 12 French, and the América rejoined on the 12th, so 14 crossed in company. The Firme, Terrible and España, with the Magdalena, crossed on their own and were found off Fort-de-France on 14 May; the San Rafael came in on the 16th (Reille, in Desbrière 1907, pp. 11, 26; Troude III 342; `research/scene-02/a-h1ac.md`, GLYPH-crossing). High.
- **The crossing.** South along the Moroccan coast to Cape Cantin, west from about 15 April, north of the Canaries and close to the Salvages, then south-west; westerlies forced a run SSW from about 26 April to 2 May, then west along 18–14½° N (Desbrière 1907, p. 25; a Spanish log captured later, Nicolas VI 468). Formidable, Intrépide and above all Atlas held the fleet back. At 1 p.m. on 13 May a frigate signalled land seven leagues to the west, 36 leagues before the reckoning expected it (Desbrière 1907, p. 26 n.). High for the route, medium for the daily positions.
- **Martinique, 14 May.** At daybreak the fleet entered the St Lucia Channel and during the day anchored off Fort-de-France (Fort Royal), fired on in passing by the British post on Diamond Rock: 17 of the line that day, 18 when San Rafael came in on the 16th (Villeneuve's report, Desbrière 1907, pp. 25–26). James's 13 May is his slip; Clowes gives both. High.
- **At Fort-de-France.** The Didon brought Decrès's dispatch of 29 April with a copy of the Emperor's orders of 14 April: take British islands, wait 35 days after Magon's arrival, then return to Ferrol (see "The plan"). She arrived on 29 May (Mahan), 30 May (Reille), 31 May (Corbett) or 1 June (James). Diamond Rock was attacked from 31 May; its garrison, 107 (James) or 128 (Desbrière), surrendered on 2 June (Corbett: 3 June). On 4 June Rear-Admiral Magon joined with Algésiras and Achille and 861 soldiers (James: 840), making 20 of the line, 14 French and 6 Spanish (Desbrière 1907, p. 36); James has the junction at sea, on the day the fleet sailed or the next (III 351). High; low for the Didon's date, medium for where Magon joined.
- **North through the islands.** The fleet sailed at 5 a.m. on 5 June (James: the 4th), passed under the lee of Dominica on the 6th, embarked troops off Basse-Terre, Guadeloupe, that afternoon and spent the night of the 7th under the lee of Antigua (Desbrière 1907, pp. 40–41). At 10 a.m. on 8 June, to leeward of Barbuda, it sighted a convoy of 15 sail from St John's and after a five-hour chase took 14 (James: all 15). The escort escaped: the schooner Netley under Lieutenant William Carr by Corbett (p. 166); the frigate Barbadoes (Captain Joseph Nourse) and the Netley (Lieutenant Richard Harward) by James (III 351), for which Corbett finds no authority (p. 166 n.). High.
- **The turn, 10 June.** The prisoners said Nelson was at Barbados with 12 or 14 of the line. By 10 June the borrowed troops were in four frigates for Guadeloupe and the fleet stood NNE for the Azores and Ferrol (Corbett 1910, p. 167; Desbrière 1907, pp. 41–43). Villeneuve's letter is dated at sea on 11 June, and Desbrière once has him sailing that day; James has "the 9th or 10th". The scene turns the line on 10 June, with a day circle lettered "10", about 19°14′ N 61°44′ W on Corbett's chart. Medium.

### Nelson's pursuit

- **Where he was.** After showing himself off Barcelona, Nelson anchored in the Gulf of Palmas, south-west Sardinia, at 4.40 p.m. on 26 March (the log's "March 27th [i.e. 26th]", Nicolas VI 374; James and Nicolas's chronology give the log date, 27 March). He weighed at 6.15 p.m. on 30 March, about two hours after Villeneuve, anchored in Pula Roads at 5.30 p.m. on the 31st and sailed again at 5.30 a.m. on 3 April (Nicolas VI 390, 392). High.
- **The news.** The Phoebe rejoined at about 10 a.m. on 4 April, some six leagues W¾S of Toro (the log's "Toro bore E. ¾ N.", Nicolas VI 393; Desbrière 1907, p. 11, after Mahan, has 10.20 a.m.). Nelson had 11 of the line, four frigates and two corvettes (Nicolas VI 398 n.); the eleven names, his ten later West Indies ships and the Royal Sovereign, are inferred. High for the count, medium for the names.
- **East first.** Fearing for Sicily, Naples or Egypt, he waited 48 hours between Sardinia and Galita (4–6 April), bore up for Palermo from the south end of Sardinia on the 7th, and was off Palermo on 9–10 April. He turned west on the 9th by his letter of 18 April, or the 10th by one dated off Palermo that day (Nicolas VI 401–408); Corbett's arrival at Ustica on the 7th is wrong (`research/scene-02/a-h1b.md`, S3-PALERMO). High; medium for the 9th or 10th.
- **Against the wind.** Beating back took nine days to regain Sardinia. Off Toro on 18 April he learned the French had passed the Strait, and on the 19th he was still ten leagues west of Toro with the wind dead against him (Corbett 1910, pp. 93–95). He sighted the Rock on 30 April, watered at Tetuan from 10.30 a.m. on 4 May and lay in Gibraltar Bay from 2 to 6 p.m. on 6 May (James: the 7th, a log date). There, by Campbell's own later complaint, Rear-Admiral Donald Campbell of the Portuguese navy came aboard as Nelson "passed the Gut" and told him where the enemy had gone (Nicolas VI 431 n.); Mahan and Desbrière put the visit at Lagos. High; medium for Campbell.
- **Lagos and the bear-away.** He anchored in Lagos Bay at 9 p.m. on 9 May to clear Orde's storeships, weighed at 9.10 a.m. on 11 May (Corbett's move off the cape on the 10th is not in the log), met Rear-Admiral Knight with Craig's troop convoy at 4 p.m., gave him the Royal Sovereign, and at 6.50 p.m. bore up for the West Indies with Cape St Vincent NW by N 7 leagues: about 36°43′ N 8°45′ W if the bearing is true, 36°49′–50′ N 8°37′–38′ W by compass, so about 36°47′ N 8°41′ W, ±5 nm (Nicolas VI 433; `research/scene-02/a-h1b.md`, S3-11MAY). The sloop Martin, which Nelson had written on the 10th was going "this day", parted ahead that afternoon by the log. High for the times, medium for the position.
- **The squadron.** Ten of the line: Victory, Canopus, Superb, Spencer, Donegal, Tigre, Leviathan, Belleisle, Conqueror and Swiftsure, with the frigates Decade, Amphion and Amazon (Nicolas VI 432 n.; Corbett 1910, p. 108). James and Clowes print nine names. High.
- **Barbados, 4 June.** He was 38 leagues from Madeira on 14 May and saw it on the 15th. Barbados was in sight at 6 a.m. on 4 June, and the squadron anchored in Carlisle Bay at 4.50 p.m. (Nicolas VI 436, 445; Desbrière too prints 4.50). He had run 3,227 miles from Cape St Vincent and gained about ten days on Villeneuve (Nicolas VI 471; Corbett 1910, p. 160). No source gives a date other than 4 June. Cochrane's Northumberland and the Spartiate made 12 of the line; Dacres had kept four of Cochrane's ships at Jamaica (James, Clowes) or five of his six (Corbett). High; medium for Cochrane's ships.
- **Trinidad.** On a report from St Lucia that 28 sail had passed Gros Islet on the night of 28 May, Nelson embarked about 2,000 troops and sailed south at 9.30 a.m. on 5 June. Off Tobago at noon on the 6th he heard of an American ship, arrived the day before, that had been boarded by a French ship to windward of St Vincent three days earlier, the enemy standing south (Nelson to Marsden, 11 June, Nicolas VI 451; Corbett 1910, p. 162). Clarke and M'Arthur tell it differently: an American brig spoken by the Curieux had been boarded a few days before by the French fleet off Grenada, standing for the Bocas of Trinidad (Nicolas VI 448 n.). At 6.10 p.m. a schooner made, by chance, the signal for the enemy at Trinidad. On the 7th he went through the Bocas into the Gulf of Paria ready for battle and found nothing. At daylight on the 8th he learned the enemy had been at Martinique on the 4th. Maurice, late of Diamond Rock, also passed on the French commodore's word that a "Ferrol Squadron" of six French and eight Spanish ships had reached Fort Royal on 4 June; Nelson gave it little credit, and only Magon's two 74s had come (Nicolas VI 446–451; diary, VI 449). High; medium for the American's report.
- **Antigua.** Off Grenada on 9 June he learned, from the Jason at 1 p.m., that the enemy had passed Dominica on the 6th, steering north. He was off Guadeloupe and Montserrat on the 11th, anchored in St John's Road, Antigua, at 7 p.m. on the 12th, sent the brig Curieux home with dispatches, and sailed for Europe at 11.50 a.m. on 13 June with 11 of the line, the Northumberland staying (Nicolas VI 451–456). High.
- **Homeward.** Thinking the allies bound for Cádiz or Toulon, he kept south of their track: south of the western and central Azores, then through the archipelago between Terceira and São Miguel, passing north of São Miguel on 8–9 July (log, Nicolas VI 468; Mahan). Villeneuve had gone north of the whole group, by Corvo. His frigates left on the way: the Decade and the sloop Martin on 19 June, the Amazon on 13 July, the Amphion on a day not recorded. He made Cape St Vincent on 17 July and anchored in Rosia Bay, Gibraltar, at 7.30 a.m. on 19 July (Mahan once has the 20th); ashore on the 20th, he noted it was his first time since June 1803 (Nicolas VI 471–475). High.
- **North.** He watered at Tetuan on 22–23 July. Off Tarifa at about 3.30–4 a.m. on 25 July (3.30 by Mahan; "four o'clock" in Nelson's letter to Collingwood) the sloop Termagant brought a Lisbon paper with the Curieux's news, and he went north at once against northerly winds; his diary has 39°1′ N 16° W on 3 August and 41°8′ N 18°14′ W on the 7th (Desbrière 1907, pp. 74–75). On 15 August he joined Cornwallis off Ushant and left him nine ships, then anchored with the Victory and the Superb at Spithead at 8.30 a.m. on 18 August and struck his flag on the 19th (Nicolas VII 4–8). The Victory counted 24 of the Channel fleet's ships of the line in sight at noon on the 15th (Nicolas VII 4). With Calder's ships, just rejoined, and Nelson's eleven, Cornwallis briefly had about 40 (Desbrière 1907, p. 88); once Nelson's flagship and the ships needing a refit had gone in, he had some 36 with his flag or on their way to it, besides the Dragon and Goliath, away to the south (Corbett 1910, p. 240). High; medium for the Channel fleet's strength.

### Cape Finisterre, 22 and 23 July

- **Villeneuve's run home.** From the turn the fleet stood NNE (Desbrière 1907, p. 43) to about 33–35° N on 21–24 June (Corbett's chart, after Desbrière's), then ENE, north of the whole Azores group, sighting Corvo on 2 July (Reille, in Desbrière 1907, p. 57). The American schooner Sally saw "about 22" large ships at 7 p.m. on 15 June near her noon position, 27°28′ N 60°58′ W (Nicolas VI 463; her log has the nautical "16th"). High for the route, medium for the positions.
- **The Curieux.** Bettesworth, carrying Nelson's dispatches, saw the allies on 19 June, by his report as far north as 33°12′ N, in 58° W (Corbett 1910, p. 181; James III 354), but Corbett's own chart, after Desbrière's, marks the sighting near 30°13′ N 58°06′ W. He reached Plymouth on 7 July and the Admiralty late on the 8th (Corbett) or by daylight on the 9th (Mahan, Desbrière), and Barham at once sent Stirling's Rochefort squadron to join Calder off Ferrol and both to cruise 30–40 leagues west of Finisterre. High for the date, low for the position.
- **Corvo to Finisterre.** Five frigates rejoined about 20 leagues NE of Corvo on 30 June (James); Reille has Corvo sighted on 2 July. On 3 July the fleet took the Liverpool privateer Mars and her prize, the Spanish galleon Matilda. A north-east gale on 9 July cost the Indomptable her main topmast; on the 12th the fleet was within about 300 miles of the coast west of Oporto (Corbett; about 365 nm on his chart), was then driven back west for four days, and ran east from the 19th by Reille (Desbrière 1907, p. 57; from the 17th by Corbett 1910, p. 195), in fog on the 21st and 22nd. Villeneuve wrote of twenty days against north-easterly winds. High for the events, medium for the 30 June and 2 July positions.
- **Calder's squadron.** After Stirling's five joined on 15 July, Vice-Admiral Sir Robert Calder had 15 of the line, four 98s, one 80, eight 74s and two 64s, with the frigates Egyptienne and Sirius, the lugger Nile and the cutter Frisk (James III 356–357; Calder's dispatch). High.
- **Villeneuve's fleet.** 20 of the line, 14 French and 6 Spanish, with 7 frigates, 2 brigs and the Matilda (James III 357; Troude III 352). Calder reported 20 of the line, three large ships armed en flûte, five frigates and three brigs. High.
- **Fog.** On the morning of 22 July the allies steered ESE by compass (about east true) for Ferrol in three columns, the Spaniards on the right, in thick fog and a light WNW breeze. It lifted a little about noon, and the Defiance reported 24, then 27 sail to the SSW (Corbett 1910, p. 196; James: SW). High.
- **Where.** There are three British noon positions: 43°30′ N 11°17′ W in Calder's dispatch (the primary one), 43°54′ N 11°38′ W in James, and 44°17′ N 11°52′ W at 11.30 a.m. in Desbrière; and a fourth with no hour: the Windsor Castle's minutes of the action are headed 44°10′ N 11°22′ W, "Cape Finisterre S. E. 112 Miles" (*Naval Chronicle* XIV, p. 168). The allies were about 16 miles SSW of the British; from the Windsor Castle's position that puts them about 43°55′ N 11°31′ W, on the northern edge of the range below, which stands. The French reckoning (13°53′ W) ran about 2° too far west, and Corbett's chart (12°20′ W) and his figure of 300 miles from Ferrol are also too far west. The fight lay within about 43°20′–43°55′ N, 11°15′–12°00′ W, 95–130 nm WNW of Cape Finisterre; the scene is to put its smoke at 43°35′ N 11°40′ W, 113 nm from the cape (`research/scene-02/a-h1ac.md`, H1-26), only while the firing went on (not drawn yet: see "How the chart shows the signals"). Medium.
- **The action.** At about 5.15 p.m. the Hero tacked and the Argonauta opened fire (Desbrière: about 5). The lines fought on the starboard tack heading about WSW, in fog and smoke so thick that a ship could see little beyond her own length (James III 361). The firing stopped at about 7.30 in the dark (Desbrière), at nine (Calder at his trial), or at 9.30, an hour after the signal to discontinue (James). High for the start, medium for the end.
- **Two prizes.** The San Rafael 80 (84 in the Gazette) and the Firme 74 drifted dismasted into the British line and struck: soon after 8 p.m. by the British account, at daybreak on the 23rd by the French (James III 361; Desbrière 1907, p. 64). Their captains were Montes and Villavicencio. High for the ships, medium for the hour.
- **Losses.** British 41 killed and 158 wounded, ship by ship in Calder's return; James's 39 and 159 is his own count. Allied: 155 killed and 341 wounded in Corbett's table (p. 202), 476 killed and wounded in James, about 580 with Desbrière's footnote for the prizes. A caption can say "about 500". High for the British, medium for the allies.
- **23 and 24 July.** At dawn the fleets were about 17 miles apart. Calder drew together round the crippled Windsor Castle and his prizes and stood NE; Villeneuve, now in the frigate Hortense, bore down between noon and 3 p.m., then hauled his wind three or four leagues off. On the 24th the wind went round to the north and Calder, now to windward, did not attack; they lost sight of each other that evening (James III 363–365) or the next morning (Corbett 1910, p. 205); Mahan has the 26th. High.
- **Calder's fate.** At his own request he was tried in Portsmouth harbour on 23–26 December 1805 and cleared of cowardice and disaffection, but for an error in judgment in not renewing the action he was "severely reprimanded" (Naval Chronicle XV 174–175). Nelson let him go home in his own flagship, the Prince of Wales, which left the fleet on the evening of 13 October (Nelson to Marsden that day, Nicolas VII 120; Corbett 1910, p. 330), so he missed Trafalgar. The Victory's log enters the parting under the nautical "October 14th" (VII 121 n.), and James (III 383) and Clowes give the 14th. High.

### Ferrol, the turn south and Cádiz

- **Vigo.** On 25 July the Didon sighted Cape Finisterre ESE. With the north-east wind rising and sickness spreading, Villeneuve chose Cádiz, then Vigo, tried for Ferrol again on the 26th, and on the 27th wrote from 40 leagues WSW of Finisterre. The fleet anchored in Vigo Bay at nightfall on 28 July (Desbrière 1907, pp. 67–69, 85; Mahan); the Cíes came in sight at 6 p.m. on the 27th or the 28th. James's "evening of the 26th" is two days early. High for 28 July, low for the Cíes.
- **To A Coruña.** Leaving the Atlas (with about 800 sick), España and América at Vigo, he sailed on 31 July (James: the 30th) with 15 of the line, 13 French and 2 Spanish, into a north-west gale. The wind went south-west on 1 August, blew Calder off Ferrol and let Villeneuve double Cape Finisterre that evening; he entered the bay on 2 August (Desbrière 1907, p. 86; Corbett 1910, p. 219, dating the junction). Napoleon's order of 16 July kept the French out of Ferrol: the 13 French anchored at A Coruña and the two Spaniards, the Argonauta and Terrible, went on into Ferrol. James and Mahan, and Corbett's narrative (p. 218), have him in on 1 August; James ties his date to Calder's being blown off that morning, so it is not simply his slip. The scene uses 2 August. Medium.
- **Twenty-nine of the line.** With Gourdon's five French and nine Spanish from Ferrol, the combined fleet had 29 of the line, 18 French and 11 Spanish, with 6 frigates and 4 corvettes: one three-decker, the Príncipe de Asturias (112 guns by Desbrière, 120 by Corbett), and six 80s; the San Julián stayed behind (Desbrière 1907, pp. 98–99; Corbett 1910, p. 239). The ships came out between 8 and 10 August to the outer bay off Ares, the last three joined before noon on 13 August, and by 2 p.m. all were under sail (Desbrière 1907, pp. 97–98; Villeneuve's letter, "25 thermidor (13 août)"). James has the fleet out at sea on the 11th, two days early. On the 10th Villeneuve had written that he would steer for Brest or Cádiz as circumstances allowed. High.
- **West-north-west.** The fleet ran west in a fresh north-easterly, stood NNE towards Cape Prior for some hours on sighting a British ship, and steered NW again from 2 a.m. on the 14th. The frigate Iris watched it, on a course W by N ½ N at 44°17′ N 9°26′ W, until about 2 p.m. (Corbett 1910, p. 259 n.). At sunset the Dragon saw 26 sail; a Danish merchantman she had boarded, told she belonged to a fleet of 25 of the line, passed the story to a French frigate (Corbett 1910, p. 262; James III 374). High.
- **His orders.** The Emperor's letter and Decrès's dispatch of 16 July, with Decrès's private letter of the 17th (received at Ferrol only on 6 August), told Villeneuve, once he had Gourdon's ships, to free the Rochefort squadron and make France master of the Strait of Dover: by joining the Brest and Rochefort squadrons, or the Brest squadron alone, or by taking the Rochefort squadron round Ireland and Scotland. After a defeat, a separation or any change in the situation, he was allowed to go to Cádiz (Desbrière 1907, p. 81). The Emperor's letter also kept the French out of Ferrol itself (p. 86). His letter from Cádiz of 22 August rests the turn on the dispatch of 16 July, and Decrès told Napoleon on the 23rd that it was his only ground (p. 112 and n.). High.
- **The turn, 15 August.** Still standing WNW against the north-east gale, Villeneuve turned for Cádiz "à l'entrée de la nuit" on 15 August, the third day out (his letter of 22 August, Desbrière 1907, p. 112 n.). Desbrière's narrative once calls the 16th the decisive day (p. 102), and his print of the same letter on p. 103 reads 13 August; both are slips, since the days described are the 14th and 15th. Reille's noon put the fleet 40 leagues WNW of Finisterre (Corbett 1910, p. 263 n.): about 43°39′ N 11°48′ W if the bearing is true, as Corbett reads it (p. 261 n.), about 42°50′ N 12°00′ W if by compass; the Iris's fix of the 14th favours the first. Villeneuve wrote 80 leagues, which Corbett shows to be impossible; Desbrière's staff map has the fleet near 44°40′ N 12°25′ W, which Corbett rejects as too far north for a fleet that was off Cádiz eighty hours after the turn (p. 261 n.); James implies the evening of the 14th. The scene bends the line south at about 43°45′ N 12°20′ W. High for the date, low for the position.
- **South.** The fleet ran south before the north-easterly, out of sight of land, and was off Cape St Vincent on 17 August (Desbrière's text) or 18 August (James; Desbrière's map), where it burned three merchantmen of a convoy whose escort, the brig Halcyon, escaped; from the 18th Blackwood's Euryalus shadowed it. At 10 a.m. on 20 August, with Cádiz NE about nine leagues, it sighted Collingwood's three ships right ahead, chased them towards the Strait (Magon led, says Desbrière; Gravina, says Corbett), bore up for the harbour at 3 p.m. and anchored in Cádiz road that evening (James III 375; Desbrière 1907, p. 107). Medium for St Vincent, high for Cádiz.
- **20 August.** The date is in James (from the ships' muster rolls), Desbrière, Corbett, Escaño's day-by-day relation and Collingwood's letter of the 21st, which says "yesterday". 21 August is in the Précis des événements militaires, which James rejects, in Escaño's later letter to MacDonnell, and in Francis Austen's log of the 24th, where the Euryalus reports the combined fleet "anchored in Cadiz on the 21st", probably the nautical date (Hubback 1906, p. 142); Troude's 17 August is impossible, since the turn came only at nightfall on the 15th, at least 500 nm away (about 514 nm in a straight line from the bend drawn, about 565 nm by Cape St Vincent; Corbett reckons 550 miles, p. 261 n.). Desbrière's staff map "Situation au 15 août" prints "19 Août" beside the six Spanish ships in Cádiz, while the fleet's track ends in an arrow off the port; if the label dates the arrival, it is a day earlier than his own text (p. 107). The scene uses 20 August. High.
- **Collingwood.** He had the Dreadnought 98, Colossus 74 and Achille 74 with the frigate Endymion and the bomb Thunder, a force he described next day as a poor one (Corbett 1910, p. 270). He drew off about 21 miles towards the Strait; the Mars rejoined from Tangier about midnight, and he was back off the port before daylight (James III 375; Desbrière: back the same evening, with four). High.
- **After.** 29 of the line came in; with the six Spaniards already in the harbour James counts 35, and Collingwood's "36 sail of men-of-war" counts frigates too. Bickerton's four joined Collingwood on 22 August, and Calder came south from Cornwallis's fleet with 18, joining on 30 August (James III 379; Corbett 1910, p. 267, give the parts); Desbrière gives the sum, 26 of the line (p. 145), and the news Allemand had from a captured brig on 11 September also gave 26 (p. 108). Desbrière's first count, 32 (p. 109), counted the Minotaur, Mars and Bellerophon twice and had Calder bring 21, the Dragon and Goliath joining him on the way, as Cornwallis's orders intended (p. 103 n.; Corbett p. 247); he corrects it himself: the Dragon went home escorting the captured Didon and the Goliath back to Ushant, neither joining, and the total was 26 (pp. 143, 145). The Dragon was off Vigo again on 10 October (p. 108 n.). Lower counts of the moment: 24 or 25 of the line seen from the San Sebastián tower (p. 145), and Francis Austen's log, which has Calder's ships joining "Vice-Admiral Collingwood's squadron of five sail of the line" (Hubback 1906, p. 142). Calder's ships included eight of the eleven Nelson had brought back from the West Indies (Desbrière 1907, p. 109 n.) (`research/scene-02/a-h1b.md`, S6-BRIT-CADIZ; `a-h1ac.md`, S5-reinf). Where the fleet lay in the bay is under "Positions on the chart". High for 29 in, medium for 35 and 26.

### Boulogne and the invasion army

The army, the flotilla and the plan in the campaign, and the camps as the chart marks them. Boulogne itself in August 1805 is in "Boulogne from the sea".

- **The camps.** Napoleon ordered six camps on 14 June 1803; Desbrière calls that order the origin of the Grande Armée (Desbrière, *Projets* III 184). By 1805 the army was the armée des côtes de l'Océan, set out in Napoleon's note of 15 May 1805 and the war minister's letter of 30 May: Lannes's advance guard (Oudinot's grenadiers), Davout's corps (the camp of Bruges), Soult's (the camp of Saint-Omer, at Boulogne), Ney's (the camp of Montreuil), corps at Utrecht and Brest, a reserve and the Guard (*Projets* IV 459–461). English writers called it the Army of England. High.
- **The ports.** Seven: Étaples, Boulogne, Wimereux, Ambleteuse, Calais, Dunkirk and Ostend. Wimereux and Ambleteuse were made flotilla ports in 1803–04: Napoleon called both new ports he had built (Corr. IX No. 7832, 2 July 1804); James has Wimereux formed into a port and Ambleteuse's existing harbour deepened and enlarged (III 217). High; the two accounts stand side by side.
- **Which troops lay where.** In August 1805 the right corps, Davout's (the camp of Bruges), was camped at Ambleteuse, the centre, Soult's, at Boulogne and the left, Ney's (the camp of Montreuil), at Étaples: Napoleon's order of 26 August sends each corps' third battalions back to those three camps (Corr. XI No. 9137, pp. 141–144). Oudinot's grenadiers marched from Wimereux (Berthier's order of 25 August, Alombert and Colin I 277). James gives the flotilla's grand divisions and whom each was to carry: from Étaples the troops of the camp of Montreuil, from Boulogne Soult's two camps right and left of the town, from Wimereux Lannes's corps with the grenadiers, from Ambleteuse (the Batavian flotilla) Davout's, and the reserve division at Calais the Italian division and the dragoons (III 325–326). High.
- **Boulogne.** Soult's corps lay in two camps either side of the harbour: the right camp on the cliff plateau north of the Liane by the Tour d'Ordre, where Napoleon had his pavilion, and the left camp on the Outreau plateau south of the river; headquarters inland at Pont-de-Briques (Corr. IX 77, 488; XI No. 9137). Where on the heights they stood, which divisions held them and the ground are in "Boulogne from the sea", "The camps" ("Where they stood", "The ground"); the pavilion is in its "Napoleon's pavilion". High: the sites are documented there (Desbrière III 428 for 1804; Bertrand I 334 for the extent).
- **Huts, not tents.** The evidence, the huts' build and look, and the camps' plan are in "Boulogne from the sea", "The camps" ("Huts, not tents", "The hut", "The camp's plan"), and what is known of the hut counts in its "Where they stood". The chart draws each camp as rows of huts along its plateau (see "Positions on the chart", "Boulogne").
- **The army.** The embarkation return of spring 1805 counted 147,662 men (142,601 combatants) and 15,764 horses: Montreuil 27,050, Saint-Omer (Soult, at Boulogne) 45,907, Bruges 33,012, the reserve 41,693. Its 2,054 craft had room for only 127,500 men and 9,975 horses (*Projets* IV 433). Napoleon's own round figure was 150,000 men and 10,000 horses (Corr. XI No. 9209). Other figures are right for their own dates: the craft at Étaples, Boulogne, Wimereux and Ambleteuse could carry 120,000 men and 10,000 horses by Napoleon's count of 2 July 1804 (Corr. IX No. 7832), and the table of July 1805 was to carry 163,645 men, 16,783 of them seamen, and 9,059 horses (James III, App. 27, from Dumas; `research/scene-02/h2-boulogne-cadiz.md`, H2-03). High. The modern figures of about 60,000 at Boulogne and over 175,000 in the camps have no source and are not used (`research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R12–R13).
- **The flotilla.** The count of 20 July 1805 found 2,293 craft in the seven ports, 1,339 armed and 954 transports (James III 217, 325; Clowes V 62); Boulogne's own count of that day is in "Boulogne from the sea", "The flotilla in the roads and in the harbour" ("How many"). Medium: other counts are right for their own dates (1,301 built in 1803–04, 2,054 in the spring of 1805; `research/scene-02/h2-boulogne-cadiz.md`, H2-05). The gun craft were organised in divisions of eighteen: Napoleon's note of 10 March 1805 gave the flotilla 14 divisions of chaloupes (252 craft), 19 of bateaux (342; 18 divisions and 341 craft in the war minister's version) and 16 of péniches (288), 881–882 gun craft (No. 8410, Desbrière IV 429–434; `research/scene-02/v-h2a.md`, H2-05). High. The chart draws one craft for a division (see "Assumptions and licences", 6).
- **The craft.** Rigged gun craft, not barges: their kinds, sizes, rigs, guns and look, and the transports, are in "Boulogne from the sea", "The flotilla in the roads and in the harbour" ("Rigged gun craft, not barges", "Other craft", "Colours"). The chart draws its craft as four kinds: prames, chaloupes canonnières, bateaux canonniers and péniches.
- **The harbour and the roads.** The Liane harbour, its quays, basin and dam, and how it dried, are in "Boulogne from the sea", "The tide, the strand and the drying harbour"; the moored line in the roads, in 1804 and 1805, in its "The flotilla in the roads and in the harbour" ("The moored line", "August 1805", "Where the line lay", "Spacing"). James's account of 1804 agrees: a line of heavy gun-vessels moored across the road against bombardment, and 45 brigs and 43 luggers left at anchor in the road in the north-north-easterly of 19 July 1804 (III 217, 227). After he left, Napoleon meant to keep gun-vessels "in the basin and road" (James III 332).
- **What the crossing needed.** Command of the Strait (*détroit*, Pas-de-Calais) for a short time, and the span changed often: eight hours of night (November 1803), six hours (2 July 1804; April and June 1805), four or five days (16 July 1805), three days (20 and 26 July, 13 August), twenty-four hours (13 and 22 August) and, looking back, fifteen days (13 September, a date the editors supply) (Corr. IX–XI; `research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R10). High. Beat 1's "command of the Channel" stands. The six-hours line is No. 7832 (2 July 1804); the Tower of London sentence is genuine but from a letter to Brune of 27 July 1804 (No. 7874), and popular versions splice the two. High.
- **Napoleon at Boulogne.** From 3 August 1805 (his datelines; James III 326; Corbett 1910, p. 240). He had the army embarked twice in his presence. High for the date; medium for the two embarkations, which rest on James alone (III 326).

### The turn east and Ulm

- **The decision, 22–26 August.** Word that Villeneuve had left Ferrol reached Boulogne on 22 August (Desbrière 1907, p. 110). On 23 August Napoleon told Talleyrand "Mon parti est pris", and had Marmont told that if the fleet were held at Cádiz he would put the crossing off "à une autre année" (Corr. XI Nos. 9117, 9120; No. 9120 is misprinted "23 mai"). The same day he ordered a force of about 10,000 under Bernadotte to gather at Göttingen (No. 9119). James (III 332) dates Berthier's letter to Marmont the 22nd and quotes it, from Dumas, with another condition: if the fleets could not reach the Channel. Cavalry orders followed on the 24th, "Mon mouvement est commencé" on the 25th, and the general order of march on the 26th, when the Grande Armée was named; it was announced on the 29th (Nos. 9128, 9130, 9137; Alombert and Colin I 216–218). High.
- **Why east.** Austria had joined Russia in a new coalition. Russia first approached her in October 1803; the courier with Austria's definite adherence left Vienna on 7 July 1805, and the two signed a treaty on 9 August (Maude 1912, pp. 76, 90). On 23 August Napoleon told Talleyrand that Austria was playing for time, and that by spring he would face Russians in Poland paid by England. So he would raise his camps, be in Germany with 200,000 men by 1 vendémiaire (23 September) and march on Vienna (Corr. XI No. 9117). On the 25th he wrote that Austria's preparations had suspended his embarkation (No. 9130). His figures for the army he would bring varied with the reader: 200,000 in Germany (to Talleyrand, 23 and 25 August), 300,000 (for Marmont, 23 August) and "plus de 100,000" in Bavaria (to the King of Prussia, 23 August); none is a return (`research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R20). Mack's columns were to cross the Inn into Bavaria on 8 September (Maude 1912, p. 98). The brief's "against Austria (the Third Coalition)" stands. High for Napoleon's letters; medium for the coalition's dates, which rest on Maude alone (the treaties, and Britain's part, were not researched).
- **Not because of Cádiz.** Napoleon knew Villeneuve was in Cádiz by 31 August at the latest, from a postscript dated 13 fructidor (Corr. XI 157). Corbett's 1 September (p. 278) reads the same postscript; Desbrière's 1–2 September (pp. 115–116) is the arrival of the full dispatches. All of it came after the orders of 23–26 August, and he had weighed the Cádiz case on 22–23 August. James (III 332) has the news arriving about four days after the 22nd, and the Daru story of the Emperor turning east in fury at it (James III, App. 28) does not fit the dates (`research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R9). High. Beat 3 may put Cádiz before the turn east, but must not make it the cause.
- **Put off, not abandoned.** He left nineteen battalions at Ambleteuse, Boulogne and Étaples, concentrated the flotilla at Boulogne and armed its seamen as infantry (No. 9137). On 13 September (a date the editors supply) he set out a plan to keep an army of 60,000–80,000 men on the heights of Boulogne and 500 craft able to carry 40,000–50,000 of them, as a standing threat (No. 9209; Desbrière 1907, p. 120, rounds it to 500 craft and 80,000 men). James (III 332) has 30,000–40,000 men kept encamped on the heights, with the gun-vessels in the basin and road. High. "Gives up on England" (beat 3) is acceptable; "puts off" is nearer his words.
- **The army leaves.** Oudinot's grenadiers marched from Wimereux on the morning of 26 August (Alombert and Colin I 277, 283–284). The first divisions of Davout's, Soult's and Ney's corps left on 28 August (10 fructidor), in the morning ("demain matin" in Berthier's letter of the 27th; no hour is given), the second on 30 and the third on 31 August, the rest from 1 September; by Berthier's order nobody marched on the 29th, though Napoleon wrote on the 28th that the second divisions would go "demain" (No. 9137; Alombert and Colin I 337, 373; No. 9155). Soult's corps left by divisions: Saint-Hilaire's 1st first, on the 28th, and Suchet's 4th at 5 a.m. on 2 September (Berjaud 2009). Napoleon left Boulogne on 2 September and was at Montreuil that evening (No. 9177; Desbrière 1907, p. 116); James (III 332) has 4 September. High. Date line: 28 August 1805.
- **To the Rhine.** About 400 miles from the coast to the Rhine (Maude 1912, p. 123), planned as 24 days' march by three roads (No. 9137). Soult's corps, from the Boulogne camps, took the centre road (Alombert and Colin I 236, 373; II 4–5, 70–77). It did not run straight across Picardy and Champagne. It went east to Saint-Omer, then south-east by Aire, Béthune, Lens and Douai to Cambrai, well north of the straight line to Metz, east by Landrecies, Avesnes, Hirson and Maubert-Fontaine to Mézières, then up the Meuse by Sedan, Stenay and Dun to Verdun, and on to Metz. Klein's dragoons had taken the same road a few days before (Alombert and Colin I 255, 284, 291; II 70–71). On 30 August Napoleon changed the destinations: Soult's four divisions were to stand between Germersheim, Lauterbourg, Bergzabern and Wissembourg instead of going to Strasbourg (Alombert and Colin I 220–221). So from Metz the corps took the Saarbrücken road as far as Saint-Avold and turned south-east across the Vosges by Sarreguemines, Bitche and Lembach to Wissembourg and Germersheim; the 1st division's itinerary is on Alombert and Colin I 415. Saint-Hilaire's 1st division, at the corps' head, was at Mézières from 9 to 11 September (the war ministry's digest of the march reports, Alombert and Colin II 74–77), was ordered to leave Metz on 18 September and was due at Germersheim on the 24th (the second order of movement, I 415, 421; the 4th division kept the same order to the day). The army crossed the Rhine between 25 and 30 September: Lannes and Murat at Kehl on the 25th, Davout at Mannheim on 26–29, Soult at Rheinhausen by Speyer on 26–30, Ney between Pforz and Durlach on the 27th (Alombert and Colin II 39–42). Soult's crossing was not at Speyer itself but at Rheinhausen, on the right bank a little upstream, which he chose because the river was narrower there and he had too few boats lower down. On 26 September the 1st division's advance guard (the 10th light infantry and the 26th chasseurs) went over by ferry and boats, at noon as Soult planned that morning (the narrative has only "dès le 26"). The bridge was laid in the night of 26–27 September; the 1st division crossed at 9 a.m. on the 27th, the 2nd at noon, the 3rd on the 28th and the 4th on the 30th (Soult to Murat, Speyer, 26 September, Alombert and Colin II 507–508; II 42). Nobody crossed on Soult's road on the 25th. So the corps' head took 29 days from Boulogne to the Rhine, not the planned 24. Napoleon's 1st Bulletin rounds the crossings to 25–26 September and has Soult cross on the bridge at Spire on the 26th (Corr. XI No. 9348). High for the road and the crossing's days; medium for the advance guard's hour; medium-high for Metz to the Rhine, the order's itinerary; medium for the head's dates between Boulogne and Metz, which are derived (see "Routes on the chart", "The army's march").
- **The Danube.** From the Rhine Soult's head went by Bruchsal and Eppingen to Heilbronn. The order of 27 September prescribed Wiesloch and Sinsheim, but Soult took the Bruchsal road to keep clear of Davout's posts. The first two divisions reached Heilbronn on 29 September and the whole corps by 1 October (Alombert and Colin II 43–44, 508). It left on 2 October, and from Öhringen Vandamme's 2nd division led: by Hall (3 October) and Rosenberg to Ellwangen, which its advance guard entered at 6 a.m. on 5 October, and on to Nördlingen on the 6th (Alombert and Colin II 48–49, 57–58, 61, 638, 685, 812–814). The 1st Bulletin's road for the corps, by Gaildorf, Abtsgmünd and Aalen (Corr. XI No. 9348), was the plan, and the 1st division's road. On the 6th Vandamme's advance guard took Harburg and Ebermergen, and in the night Donauwörth, which the Austrians had just left after cutting the bridge (Soult's report of 7 October, Alombert and Colin II 867–868); Maude puts the seizure at about 8 p.m. on the 6th (pp. 199–200). The Danube was crossed at Donauwörth on 7 October. Napoleon directed the crossing and slept in the town while his headquarters stayed at Nördlingen, which is why his papers of the 7th bear that name (Schuermans 1908, p. 180; Maude 1912, pp. 199–200). High for the date; medium for the hour (afternoon by Schuermans, morning by Maude).
- **The dashed line.** A composite, labelled as the army: no single corps marched the whole of it. From Boulogne to Donauwörth it follows the head of Soult's IV corps, from the Boulogne camps: Saint-Hilaire's 1st division as far as Heilbronn, Vandamme's 2nd from Öhringen. From Donauwörth Soult went south by Augsburg and Landsberg, then west to Memmingen and the Biberach road (`research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R6), so the line leaves his road there. Ney's VI corps came down from Heidenheim and Giengen to the Danube at Höchstädt, 17 km above Donauwörth, on the evening of 7 October (Maude 1912, p. 200; Alombert and Colin have him ordered that morning to stay at Giengen, II 61, and Berthier's order of the 8th was due at Höchstädt by 9 a.m., III/1 20). On the 8th it spread west along the left bank: the 59th held Gundelfingen, with companies at Lauingen and Dillingen (III/1 21). On the 9th Labassée's column marched from Gundelfingen on Günzburg, and Malher's division seized the bridge there in the evening, after a hard fight (III/1 34; Maude 1912, pp. 209–211). At 8 a.m. on 14 October Ney, with Loison's division leading, forced the bridge at Elchingen and drove the Austrians back on Ulm, in fighting that lasted ten hours. On the 15th, at about 3 p.m., Ney and part of Suchet's division took the Michelsberg and the Geisberg above the town (Maude 1912, pp. 176–177, 209–211, 231–233, 240–241). So Ney fought at Elchingen and closed on Ulm; the town itself was given up by capitulation (see "Ulm"). From Donauwörth the line runs up the Danube's left bank: to Höchstädt it joins the two corps' roads, where no corps marched; from there on it is Ney's road west by Dillingen, Lauingen and Gundelfingen to Günzburg. The line's waypoints, their dates and the dates lettered beside it are under "Routes on the chart", "The army's march" (`research/scene-02/session-32/army-march.md`). High for the roads and the dates from the Rhine on; medium-high for Metz to the Rhine, as ordered; medium for the head's dates between Boulogne and Metz.
- **Ulm.** Mack's capitulation was signed on 17 October (at noon by Alombert and Colin; the Austrian eyewitness has Berthier come at 2 p.m.), the garrison to become prisoners unless relieved by 25 October. Under its article 6 a brigade of Ney's corps, under General Labassée, took over the Stuttgart gate at 10 a.m. on 18 October (Alombert and Colin III/1 226; the Austrian eyewitness they print has the "porte Neuve" handed over at 9). A convention of 19 October brought the march-out forward. On 20 October, from 2 p.m. (it had been announced for 3), the garrison marched out by the Frauentor and laid down its arms before Napoleon, who watched from a rise at the foot of the Michelsberg until about 7 p.m. (Alombert and Colin III/1 225–228; 7th and 8th Bulletins, Corr. XI 339–342). High. The scene uses 20 October; "lays down its arms" is exact, "surrenders" defensible. The 19 October of some modern summaries has no period support.
- **How many.** About 25,000: 25,365 prisoners (Alombert and Colin III/1 229; Maude 1912, p. 250), "vingt-trois mille" by the eyewitness. Napoleon's figures grew from 15,000 and 20,000 on the 17th to 27,000 with 18 generals in the 7th Bulletin (the source of the modern 27,000) and 30,000 with 16 generals in the 8th (the Austrian eyewitness also has 16). Taken with them: 40 colours, 63 guns, 2 howitzers and 13,600 muskets (page image; the OCR's 43,600 is a misreading). Medium.

### Nelson off Cádiz

- **Recalled.** Early on 2 September Blackwood, posting from Lymington to the Admiralty with the news that the combined fleet was in Cádiz, called at Merton, and Nelson was ordered to resume the Mediterranean command (Corbett 1910, p. 278; Nicolas VII, Analysis, p. xxxi). Of the ships he chose at Portsmouth only the Victory and the frigate Euryalus were ready; the Royal Sovereign, Defiance and Agamemnon were left to follow, and the Ajax and Thunderer were ordered out of Plymouth to join him (Nicolas VII 36–37; Desbrière 1907, p. 146). High.
- **Portsmouth and St Helens, 13–15 September.** Nelson left Merton at 10.30 p.m. on Friday 13 September and reached Portsmouth at six the next morning (his diary, Hudson 1917, p. 15; to Marsden, Nicolas VII 36). The Victory lay at St Helens, where his flag was rehoisted at 11.30 a.m. by her log, "about noon" by his letter (Nicolas VII 36). He embarked from the beach by the bathing machines with Rose and Canning and was on board at 2 p.m., by his diary as Hudson reads the manuscript and as Beatty prints it (Hudson pp. 15–16; Beatty 1825, p. 95). Nicolas prints the "two" as the hour he left the beach (VII 35), and Corbett has him go off to the ship early in the morning (p. 292). She weighed at daylight on Sunday 15 September in light northerly airs, anchored again at 6 and weighed at 8, making sail to the SSE with the Euryalus (diary, Hudson p. 16; log, Nicolas VII 36). Beatty, James, Desbrière and Nicolas's chronology give the 15th (Beatty 1825, p. 1; James III 379; Desbrière 1907, p. 146; Nicolas VII, Analysis, p. xxxi). Corbett has the signal to weigh made on the 14th, after Rose and Canning had dined on board (p. 292), and the Royal Museums Greenwich gives the 14th for his leaving Portsmouth: that is the day he embarked. High. Date line: 15 September 1805.
- **Down Channel, 15–18 September.** Against light airs, fog and then a foul westerly he was off Christchurch at sunset on the 15th, off Portland on the 16th and four miles off Berry Head that evening. At 9 a.m. on the 17th, abreast of Plymouth, he sent the Euryalus in to call the Ajax and Thunderer out of Cawsand Bay; a letter of the same day is dated off the Eddystone (diary, Hudson 1917, p. 16; Nicolas VII 38–43). On the 18th he lay to with the Lizard north, and the two 74s joined: in the morning by his letter to Marsden, at noon by his diary (Nicolas VII 45; Hudson p. 16). Desbrière also has the Lizard (p. 146); James (III 379) and Beatty (1825, p. 1) say off Plymouth. High.
- **Across the Bay, 19–23 September.** A south-westerly gale on the 19th held him in the mouth of the Channel (diary, Hudson pp. 16–17; west-south-westerly in Desbrière 1907, p. 146). On the 20th he passed Rear-Admiral Stirling's five of the line at 11 a.m. and hove to from 1.30 to 2.20 p.m. for the frigate Decade, which was carrying Bickerton home sick (Victory's log, Nicolas VII 46). The diary has the frigate seen to windward at noon and spoken at 2; Beatty puts the meeting at noon (p. 1). Nelson placed it "27 leagues S.W. from Scilly" (to Collingwood, 25 September, Nicolas VII 49); Desbrière puts both meetings there (p. 146). Northerly and then easterly gales carried him south, and at 6 a.m. on the 23rd he was "abreast of Cape Finisterre 17 Lgs" (diary, Hudson p. 17). High for the dates, medium for the places.
- **Down the coast of Portugal, 23–27 September.** The diary's noon latitudes are 42°25′ N on the 23rd (printed "42°: 2′5 [sic.]", which could also be 42°02½′) and 40°05′ N on the 24th (Hudson 1917, p. 18). He made the Rock of Lisbon SSE ten leagues on the 25th, having arrived off it that morning, and lay becalmed there all day; he asked the consul and the Amphion at Lisbon to keep his approach secret (diary; Nicolas VII 48–50). On the 26th, with the Rock NNE 13–14 leagues, he sent the Euryalus ahead at 4 p.m. with a letter dated the 25th: Collingwood was to put himself under Nelson's command and, if in sight of Cádiz, to fire no salute and hoist no colours when Nelson joined (diary, Hudson p. 18; Nicolas VII 49). James and Beatty also give the 26th (James III 379; Beatty 1825, p. 2); Corbett has the Euryalus sent from off Cape St Vincent (p. 317). At daylight on the 27th Cape St Vincent bore SE by S by compass, six leagues. There he stopped the sloop Nautilus, homeward bound with Collingwood's dispatches, and left her off the cape to send his reinforcements to the rendezvous between Cape St Mary's and Cádiz; at noon he was abreast of Lagos Bay (diary, Hudson pp. 18–19; Nicolas VII 51–53). Nelson heads letters "off Cape St. Vincent" on the 26th and 27th, and Desbrière keeps him there both days (p. 147), but the cape came in sight only on the 27th. High for the dates, medium for the positions.
- **Joining.** After lying to from 1 a.m., he bore up at daylight on Saturday 28 September with a fresh NNW breeze. At noon, in 36°32′ N, he saw nine ships of war to the east, and eighteen by 1 p.m. (the diary from the manuscript, Hudson 1917, p. 19; Beatty and Nicolas print only "At noon saw eighteen sail"). In near calm, under studding sails, the Victory joined Collingwood at 6 p.m. (Victory's log, Nicolas VII 53 n.; Desbrière 1907, p. 147). At 8, the log's next entry, San Sebastián's light bore E by N, five or six leagues: about 36°23′ N 6°36′ W by compass, 16½ nm south-west by west of the light. Desbrière gives that bearing at 8 a.m., for the main body (p. 147). The joining was in sight of Cádiz: Nelson saw the combined fleet in the harbour, Beatty puts the city about fifteen miles off (p. 2), and the Cádiz signal stations reported a three-decker and two 74s joining Collingwood from the westward that evening (Corbett 1910, p. 322). He could not communicate until morning and took command on the 29th, his forty-seventh birthday (his letters, Nicolas VII 60, 62–63; Duff, VII 70 n.; Codrington 1873, I 51); his "got fairly into the Fleet yesterday", written to Ball on the 30th, means that day (Nicolas VII 54). The main body went out of sight, 16–18 leagues west, only after he took command (see "The station"). Nicolas heads both 28 and 29 September "Sunday", a slip; the manuscript and Beatty have "Saturday" for the 28th. Fraser has him arrive on the 29th (1906, p. 22), and some modern accounts give 27 September, or the 29th, the day he took command; the period record is the evening of the 28th (`research/scene-02/h2-boulogne-cadiz.md`, H2-15). High; medium for the position. Date line: 28 September 1805.
- **Strength on arrival.** 29 of the line, counting Victory, Ajax and Thunderer: 23 in the main body and 6 inshore under Rear-Admiral Louis (Collingwood's 26 are named ship by ship in Desbrière 1907, pp. 143–145). The sources, and James's and Beatty's 27, are under "Fleet sizes at each stage". High for 29 under his command; medium for all 29 in company (`research/scene-02/a-h2b.md`, H2-16).
- **Louis's detachment.** Ordered on 2 October to Gibraltar and Tetuan for water and stores, he sailed at 6 that evening with the Queen 98, Canopus 80, Spencer 74, Tigre 74 and Zealous 74, the frigate Endymion and five merchantmen (Nelson to Marsden, VII 63; the Victory's log, VII 63 n.; Francis Austen, Hubback 1906, p. 148). His brief return on 4–5 October, the Leviathan's absence and the dates the sources give are under "Fleet sizes at each stage" ("The British fleet off Cádiz, by date", "Louis's brief return", "The Leviathan"). Medium-high; the return date is disputed.
- **The count through October.** By date, 29 on Nelson's arrival, 23 by 5 October, 24, 26, 27 and 28, and 27 from the 17th to the battle; each change, its source and the versions that differ (James's and Clowes's peak of 29 among them) are under "Fleet sizes at each stage". High for 29, 23 by the 5th and 27; medium-high for the steps between.
- **At Trafalgar.** 27 of the line: 3 of 100 guns, 4 of 98, 1 of 80, 16 of 74 and 3 of 64; the frigates Euryalus, Naiad, Phoebe and Sirius; the schooner Pickle (joined 1 October) and the cutter Entreprenante (joined 4 October) (Nicolas VII 64, 74, 130). Collingwood's printed order of attack names the same six after the ships of the line (London Gazette, 6 November 1805; Nicolas VII 215), and at daylight the Conqueror logged the fleet as 27 of the line, four frigates, a schooner and a cutter (NRS II 257; the Leviathan agrees, II 267). Lieutenant John Richards Lapenotière commanded the Pickle and Lieutenant Robert Benjamin Young the Entreprenante (Nicolas VII 142); James and the NRS list print the cutter's commander as "John Puver", which Nicolas corrects (James III 384; NRS II 144; Nicolas VII 346 n.). No list or log names any other British warship below a frigate: the brig-sloop Weazle had gone for Gibraltar on the 19th (see "Blackwood's squadron"), and a third small sail some observers counted is under "Open doubts". Louis's five, the Prince of Wales and the Donegal had gone. In all, 34 different ships of the line served under Nelson off Cádiz. High.
- **Louis on 18–21 October.** He finished watering at Tetuan on the 15th; that evening a westerly wind forced him back there, and on the evening of the 17th a frigate brought the order to see the Malta convoy as far as Cartagena (Austen, Hubback 1906, p. 152; Corbett 1910, pp. 331 n., 335). On the 18th he crossed to Gibraltar and left eastward with the convoy, five of the line and three frigates (Austen, Hubback pp. 152–153); the telegraph report that reached Villeneuve that day spoke of four of the line with the convoy and two more in Gibraltar (Corbett 1910, p. 332). At dawn on the 20th he was some 200 miles east of the Strait (Corbett 1910, p. 335). On the 21st he parted from the convoy: off Cape Ténès, as the usual practice was, by Corbett (from the Queen's log), or earlier than planned, on news that the enemy was coming out, by Austen (Hubback p. 154) (`research/scene-02/a-h2b.md`, H2-17f). Medium. He is outside the Gulf frame.
- **The station.** Nelson kept the body of the fleet "from sixteen to eighteen leagues West of Cadiz" (to Blackwood, 4 October; to Barham, 5 October), fifteen to eighteen by his letter to Ball of 11 October and "15 lgs. to twenty" by his diary of 14 October (Hudson 1917, p. 21), on about the port's parallel. In fresh easterly breezes he would work up toward Cádiz, "never getting to the Northward of it", and on hearing that the enemy was coming out he would carry a press of sail south toward Cape Spartel (to Blackwood, 4 October; Nicolas VII 73, 75, 111, 122). Desbrière (p. 149) puts this as never standing north of the port's parallel. Nelson's reasons: not to be caught close in by a westerly gale and swept into the Strait, and to hide his strength and tempt the enemy out (to Barham, 5 October; Corbett 1910, pp. 326–327). High.
- **Out of sight.** Before Nelson came, Collingwood's fleet cruised in sight of the port: on 3 September Lauriston reported 5 British ships of the line and 2 frigates in view from the San Sebastián tower at 3 leagues, and 17 more ships at 6 leagues (Desbrière 1907, pp. 125, 145); when Nelson arrived, 22 were cruising about 15 miles off (James III 379; 15–20 miles by Mahan). From a watchtower about 45 m up (probably the Torre Tavira) a masthead shows to about 29 nm in clear air and a hull to about 18–19 nm, so the main body at 45–60 nm was invisible and the inner ships of the line at 21–30 nm were at the limit (computed; `research/scene-02/h2-boulogne-cadiz.md`, H2-24). Beurnonville wrote from Madrid on 20 October that Nelson's force was not in sight and kept only frigates on watch, which he put down to the weather (Desbrière 1907, annexes 70–71). Medium.
- **Blackwood's squadron.** The Euryalus and Hydra were off Cádiz from 29 September; Blackwood had "five Frigates, a Brig, and a Schooner" by 11 October (Nicolas VII 53, 111). The brig and the schooner were the Weazle and the Pickle, which Nelson sent to Blackwood on 8–9 October (Nicolas VII 87, 95–97, 110); the French saw five frigates, a brig and a schooner close off the port in those days (Desbrière 1907, pp. 141, 150). On 19 October: Euryalus (Blackwood), Sirius, Phoebe and Naiad, the Weazle and the Pickle (NRS II 146). By day they kept close off the harbour, usually 2–8 nm west or south-west of San Sebastián. On 8 October the Euryalus stood in until San Sebastián bore S½W, 2¼ miles, in the mouth of the bay (James III 380). At night a frigate might anchor off the port and the Pickle worked close inshore (Nicolas VII 76, 96). The Weazle and the Pickle kept with the Euryalus and Sirius until about 9 a.m. on the 19th: the Euryalus signalled the Weazle to part company at 8.40 (her signal log, NRS II 160–161) and ordered the Pickle off at 9, and the Sirius logs both parting at 9 (NRS II 146, 312). The Weazle, a Cruizer-class brig-sloop of 18 guns under Commander Peter Parker (Winfield 2008, via Wikipedia), went with the news for Gibraltar and Tetuan, to bring out Louis's ships (Blackwood to his wife that day, Nicolas VII 131 n.), and missed the battle. The Pickle was to tell every ship off Cape Spartel that the enemy was out, cruise there three days and come back; at noon both bore SSE of the Euryalus in a calm (NRS II 146), and the Pickle was with the fleet again by the early afternoon of the 20th (NRS II 317). High.
- **The chain.** From 4 October the Mars, Defence and Colossus (Duff's squadron, with the Ajax at first) stood about four leagues east of the fleet ("from three to four leagues" in the order to Duff, four in the letter to Blackwood), one pushed east toward Cádiz "as near as possible in the latitude", using distant signals and guns "by day or night" (Nicolas VII 70–73; `research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R1). From 14–15 October the Defence and Agamemnon lay 7–10 leagues west of Cádiz and the Mars and Colossus 5 leagues east of the fleet (diary, 14 October: Nicolas VII 122; Hudson 1917, p. 21). On 19 October the cordon was the Colossus, Mars, Defence and Agamemnon (Beatty, p. 6 in both editions; Codrington 1873, I 57). Frigates at the Cádiz end, ships of the line beyond: beat 4's caption says "a chain of frigates and ships of the line" (the owner's decision, 1 November 2026), not the brief's "chain of frigates". High. How the signal ran is under "The signal chain, 19 to 21 October".
- **The British track, 19–21 October.** After the general chase at 9.30 the fleet ran south-east for the Strait (the night's signals and lights are under "The signal chain, 19 to 21 October"). It hove to at 1 a.m. on the 20th (Corbett 1910, p. 335). At daybreak it lay "between Trafalgar and Cape Spartel", in sight of Gibraltar, with no enemy in sight, in rain and a fresh SSW wind (diary, Nicolas VII 137; Corbett 1910, pp. 335–336): about 36°00′ N 5°58′ W. It wore and stood north-west, and at 7 the Phoebe signalled the enemy to the north (James III 390). At noon Cádiz bore NE, 9 leagues (diary, Nicolas VII 137). That puts the fleet about 36°07′ N 6°33′ W if the bearing is by compass, 36°13′ N 6°43′ W if true. James has 8–9 leagues, Corbett about 20 miles, Desbrière about 25 (p. 177), Clowes about 28 (V 130). At 2 p.m. a shift of wind to WNW took the fleet aback. At 4 it wore and stood north on the larboard tack (James III 390; Desbrière has it tack at 4.40, p. 178). At 8 (8.40 by James) it wore to the south-west, and the Africa, missing the signal, held on north (Corbett 1910, p. 340 n.). At 4 a.m. on the 21st it wore again: N by E by the Victory's log, "to the N.E." by Nelson's diary (Corbett 1910, p. 340; James III 390). Desbrière's midnight estimate, about 20 miles south-west of Cádiz with the allies 5–6 miles off (p. 178), cannot be joined to the dawn position to better than 5–15 nm (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-32i). High for the sequence; low for the positions.

### Orders, Rosily and the sortie

- **The orders.** On 14 September Napoleon ordered Villeneuve to sail at the first chance with at least two and a half months' food, collect the Spanish squadron at Cartagena, land his troops at Naples, fight any enemy found in inferior force, and refit at Toulon (Corr. XI No. 9210; James III 378 and Clowes V 128 date the orders 17 September). The orders reached Cádiz on 27 September by Decrès's report (Desbrière 1907, annexes 34) or on the 28th (Desbrière p. 136; James; Clowes). The troops embarked from 30 September. High; the day of arrival is disputed by one.
- **Rosily.** On 15 September Napoleon told Decrès that Villeneuve's "excessive pusillanimité" would keep him in port and that Rosily should replace him (No. 9220; James III 378 dates this letter 17 September and puts the new orders in it); Rosily's instructions are of 17 September (No. 9230; Desbrière's narrative says the 18th). Decrès's letter of 20 September superseding Villeneuve had not reached him by 18 October: neither Decrès nor Beurnonville had told him (Desbrière p. 142). High.
- **Rosily's journey.** Eleven days from Bayonne to Madrid, arriving in the night of 10–11 October (Beurnonville; Desbrière p. 128 has the 10th; Corbett's 12 October is the date of Rosily's own letter). With the posts broken down and robbers on the road beyond Córdoba he left Madrid only on the 14th, and reached Cádiz on 23–25 October: expected on the 23rd, his first letter from Cádiz dated the 25th (Desbrière 1907, annexes 68–73, 117–118; Fraser 1906, p. 379). Medium-high.
- **What Villeneuve knew.** On 15 October private letters said Rosily had passed Bayonne. On 18 October he heard "par le bruit public" that Rosily was in Madrid to take command, and wrote that he would sail next day if the wind allowed (Desbrière pp. 141–142; Corbett 1910, pp. 331–332). Fraser (p. 63) puts the Madrid news within hours of the Bayonne letters, about 15–16 October. Medium.
- **The council of war, 8 October.** Gravina had asked for it on the 6th, and Villeneuve called it on board the Bucentaure; fourteen officers signed, the Spanish brigadier Hore among them, whom Desbrière's narrative leaves out. It resolved to wait for weather that drove the British off, or for them to divide (Desbrière 1907, annexes 97–100). The argument that the allied crews were unpractised was Escaño's, Gravina's chief of staff, not Prigny's as Corbett (p. 324) has it. High.
- **Into the roadstead.** The fleet entered Cádiz on 20 August (Escaño's relation; his later letter says the 21st) and lay at first in the inner harbour, the Argonauta between the Puntal and Matagorda forts (Desbrière 1907, annexes 8). Within ten days Villeneuve brought his fastest ships down to just within the harbour mouth (Fraser 1906, p. 29). Corbett has the fleet still packed in the harbour about 29 September (p. 318), and on 1 October the Euryalus saw it at anchor in the outer harbour, apparently ready for sea (James III 380).
