From 9–10 October it moved down to the roadstead at the harbour mouth (Fraser 1906, p. 53; James III 385; Nelson's diary, 14 October: "Enemy at the Harbours Mouth", Hudson 1917, p. 21), out of range of the city's guns and so moored with springs (Escaño, annexes 98), with two anchors, NW and SE (so the Montañés, annexes 372). Not every ship moved: the Fougueux, the one ship any source places in the narrows, lay between Puntal and Matagorda until she warped out on the evening of the 19th, and the ships furthest in, which the Thémis does not name, stayed at anchor that day (annexes 213, 216, 273). High.
- **The decision to sail.** Hard westerly gales blew from the 10th to the 17th; at midnight on the 17th the wind went east (James III 385). Coast signals reported four of the line gone from Gibraltar with a convoy, one there dismasted and another going in, so Nelson seemed six short (Desbrière p. 141). On the 18th Villeneuve ordered Magon out that night with four French and three Spanish of the line and the Hermione to catch the inshore frigates (his letter, annexes 101; Desbrière's narrative says three ships), then decided to sail next day. The signal to unmoor went up at 5.15 p.m., and the cables were hove short by 10.30 p.m. (Desbrière p. 160). High.
- **19 October.** The signal to weigh went up at first light (5–6 a.m. by the French; 7 by James), but the breeze died and only seven of the line got out: Algésiras, Achille, Argonaute, Neptune, Héros, Duguay-Trouin and the Spanish Bahama, with the frigates Rhin and Hermione, the Thémis following in the evening (Philibert's journal, Desbrière 1907, annexes 229–230; the Montañés, annexes 373). Other French counts: "six à sept" (the Rhin), "7 ou 8" (the Fougueux), eight French (the Observateur), a division of four at 4.30 p.m. (the Thémis) and nine (Prigny, writing from memory) (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-30c). British observers counted 9 sail at 4 p.m. and 12 at 6 p.m., frigates included, and that is where James's and Nicolas's "twelve ships" comes from (NRS II 146; `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-30c); the Naiad at noon logged eleven "line-of-battle ships" outside the San Sebastián light, from 11 or 12 miles off (NRS II 302; `research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R7). At first light all 33 of the line lay in the bay, with the five frigates and the brigs Argus, Furet and Observateur (Villeneuve's state of 16 September, annexes 85–86; Escaño's list of the 15 Spanish ships at anchor in the bay, headed 19 October but dated the 16th in the volume's index, Fernández Duro VIII 318, 487; all sailed on the 19th or the 20th but the Observateur, annexes 158, 273). The morning was busy. The Rhin weighed on the order at 6.15 and went out towed by her boats, and the Héros was under way by 6.30 with two boats towing ahead (annexes 164–165, 207–208); at 7 the Euryalus saw the northernmost ships under way, and by 8 she counted 19 of the enemy under way and all the rest but two ships of the line with their topsails at the mastheads (NRS II 145–146); at 9 the Argus counted 17 under sail. Then the air died, the flood ran in, and many anchored again where they were (the Hermione, the Argus, the Montañés, annexes 242, 268, 373). The Fougueux and the Montañés saw the outermost ships weigh first (annexes 213, 373). The seven and the Rhin went out through the passes between about 10 a.m. and 1 p.m. (Desbrière p. 161); Magon sent the Hermione out to reconnoitre at 9.30, and she too was towed (annexes 229, 242). At 4 p.m. the Swiftsure and all the French frigates were signalled to weigh (the Furet, annexes 174): the Thémis weighed at 4.30 and joined Magon at about 6.30 (annexes 230, 273), and the Cornélie and Hortense probably went out that evening (medium; see "Open doubts"). The brigs stayed in: at 8.30 a.m. the Observateur's captain brought the Argus an order to sail only with the last ship of the fleet (annexes 268). The Fougueux, hove short in the narrows, made no attempt to weigh; at 5.30 p.m. she warped out with her own boats and hawsers to a berth off the city from which she could weigh with any wind (annexes 213, 216). High.
- **The night of 19–20 October.** Magon stayed under sail off the port. After midnight the Bahama and Achille anchored north-west of the Cádiz light, near Rota; at daylight the Euryalus saw nine under sail and four at anchor (Desbrière p. 162; NRS II 147). Nelson was told of "near forty Sail" outside, an overcount (diary, Nicolas VII 136–137). High.
- **20 October.** The signal to weigh came at first light (6 a.m. by the Montañés and the Observateur, 6.30 by Desbrière). The whole fleet was out of the bay by about 8–8.30 a.m., the Rayo last at about 10, and the Euryalus counted 34 out at 8.50–9.10 (Desbrière 1907, annexes 158, 213, 216, 373; NRS II 147–148). The Fougueux weighed at first light with the general signal (annexes 213), and the Furet came out with the main body (annexes 174). The Argus weighed at 8.30 to wait for the Rayo, which told her she had had many anchors to weigh, and made all sail after the fleet once the Rayo was under way; at 11 she had the San Sebastián tower SE 5° E, 8 miles, about 36°34′ N 6°28′ W if the bearing is by compass, 36°37′ N 6°27′ W if true (annexes 268–269). Desbrière's time of about 11 (p. 163) and Corbett's of shortly before noon (p. 337) describe the fleet formed and standing off. High. The scene avoids "at noon".
- **At sea.** 33 of the line, 5 frigates and 2 brigs (the Thémis; the Pickle's log at 3 p.m. as Nicolas prints it, VII 135 n., where the NRS transcription, II 317, has 4 frigates). In rain and a fresh wind from SSW to SW they stood WNW, then wore onto the starboard tack in mid-afternoon: between 1 and 3 (Desbrière p. 164, adding that many reports say 3; the Sirius's master's log already has the van on the starboard tack after 1, NRS II 313), at 3.30 (the Sirius's log as Nicolas prints it, VII 134), or about 4 (Corbett; Prigny). James (III 388) keeps them on the larboard tack until they tacked toward the Strait at about 5. They formed in three columns (Prigny, Fougueux, Desbrière) or five (James; the Spanish official report, per Nicolas); the two can be reconciled, the main body in three columns with Gravina's squadron of observation "à gauche de l'armée" (Prigny; `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-31e). About 6 p.m. the Achille saw 18 British sail to the WSW. About 8 p.m. Villeneuve ordered the line of battle on the starboard tack, Gravina leading with a light, and a night course "S.-O. 1/4 S.", that is SW by S by compass, about S by W true (Desbrière p. 164). The brigs carried the admirals' word: at 8.30 p.m. Villeneuve sent the Furet along the whole line to tell the captains to close up and keep their wind; the Argus, back by Gravina from 5 p.m., took Gravina's report of the Achille's sighting to Villeneuve and was back by 9.15 with his answer, to form the line on the ship furthest to leeward, which she then carried down the line (annexes 174, 269, 379, 384). Whether the fleet wore in the night is disputed. James (III 388) has them wear and stand north-west after the Aigle signalled 18 British ships in line to the south at 7.30. Beatty (p. 10 in both editions) and Nicolas (VII 136) say they wore twice in the night. Desbrière (p. 178) says the two reports of their going about that reached Nelson were wrong, and the French reports keep them on the starboard tack (see "Which half of the code"). Corbett (p. 338) makes the mean course about south. At nightfall they were at most 8–10 miles south-west of Cádiz by the British frigates' logs (Desbrière p. 164): about 36°25′ N 6°27′ W, or 36°23.5′ N 6°23.5′ W if the bearing is by compass. High for the order and the sequence; the hour of the wear and the columns are disputed (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-31e, H2-31g); low for the night's track.
- **The combined fleet.** 18 French (4 of 80 guns, 14 of 74) and 15 Spanish: the Santísima Trinidad (130 guns in Nicolas VII 130, 136 in `research/dawn-setting.md`, 140 in Desbrière p. 134), Santa Ana and Príncipe de Asturias (112), Rayo (100), two of 80, eight of 74 and San Leandro (64). With them the French frigates Cornélie, Hermione, Hortense, Rhin and Thémis and the brigs Argus and Furet (Collingwood's dispatch; James III 386); the Spaniards had no frigates or small craft with them (Nicolas VII 130). At 8 a.m. on the 21st the Euryalus counted 33 of the line, five frigates and two brigs (NRS II 149); other British logs give two brigs, or one. In the line of battle of 6 October the Furet (lieutenant de vaisseau Demay), a brig-corvette of 16–18 guns, had her station abreast the Santísima Trinidad in Villeneuve's own squadron, and the Argus (lieutenant de vaisseau Taillard), a brig of 14–16 guns, abreast the French Achille in Gravina's division of the squadron of observation (annexes 86, 124, 173, 268; the guns as Winfield and Roberts 2015 and James III 238, 349 give them, the first via threedecks.org and Wikipedia). High; medium for the guns.
- **Left in port.** The bay had held 35 of the line: Villeneuve's 29 from Ferrol and 6 Spanish fitted out at Cádiz (Clowes V 121; Desbrière pp. 134–135). The Terrible 74 was disarmed, and the San Fulgencio 64 docked to survey her bottom; their crews manned the newly commissioned ships (Gravina to Decrès, 16 September, Desbrière 1907, annexes 84). Neither was with the fleet in October; no source gives their berths, and they lay most likely at the arsenal of La Carraca, which had the bay's only dry docks, about 6 nm south-east of the anchorage (medium; low for the place). Two more Spanish ships of the line, found in the roads when the fleet arrived, were judged worthless and replaced (Lauriston, 2 September, annexes 112; Desbrière p. 134); like the other ships laid up in the arsenal (Fernández Duro VIII 308), they are not named in October (low). The Spanish frigate Flora (34) and the corvettes Mercurio and Indagadora had come from Ferrol with the fleet (Desbrière p. 99, who spells the second "Indagora"); his sum of the Spanish share, and Escaño's, is one frigate and one corvette, so one of the two corvettes may not have come (Desbrière p. 99; Escaño, annexes 7; medium for the corvettes). None of them sailed on 19–20 October: the fleet at sea had only the five French frigates and two French brigs (the Thémis, annexes 273; James III 386), and on 1 October the Euryalus saw only four frigates and two brigs with the fleet in the outer harbour (James III 380). Their place on the 19th is not recorded (low); a modern summary has the Flora at Cádiz in 1808. The brig Observateur stayed behind by order: she sailed badly, and Villeneuve put her seamen into the Indomptable and her gunners into the Algésiras (his letter of 18 October, annexes 102; her captain's letter of the 20th, annexes 158; Desbrière p. 174). Villeneuve's state of 16 September lists her among the brigs, and her captain calls her one; Desbrière's heading to her letter calls her a corvette (annexes 86, 158). British counts from the offing ran from 34 to 36. High, except where stated.

### Fleet sizes at each stage

Two kinds of figure. On the far views each squadron is lettered at its track's head with its commander and its strength on the day ("Villeneuve, 20 sail"), and in October each fleet off Cádiz is lettered among its ships ("Nelson, 27 sail"): the first part below gives every such figure with its date, its sources and the versions that differ. In the close views the scene draws the fleets ship by ship, with their frigates and small craft (Cádiz in October, the sortie, the night and the dawn): the table at the end gives those. The ships that come in at a track's head are the ships its label counts, so the figure and the ships drawn there always agree where the ships are drawn; off Cádiz in October the British label runs ahead of the drawing, which shows only the 27 of Trafalgar (see "What the chart draws for these figures"; `src/scenes/02-road/squadrons.ts`, `stagesOf` and `strengthOf`; `src/scenes/02-road/fleets.ts`, `lineStrength` and `NOT_AT_TRAFALGAR`). The research behind the figures, with every page: `research/scene-02/session-21/strengths-by-date.md`.

#### The strengths lettered at the tracks' heads

**The rule (the owner's answers of 12 November 2026).** The label counts ships of the line only, as the dispatches counted a fleet's strength and as the finale's 27 against 33 does. Frigates, small craft and prizes are left out. One rule holds everywhere: the figure is the ships of the line actually sailing together at the head of the track on that date, under the commander's flag. A straggler is added on the day she caught up; a ship that left is taken off on the day she left. Villeneuve's crossing begins with the 14 who went out with him from Cádiz. The figure and the ships that come in at the head as the camera nears always agree, and the figure changes smoothly. A squadron of one ship of the line is lettered with her name ("Nelson, Victory"); one under no flag is not lettered. Where no source gives the day, the most probable is taken; where no hour is recorded, noon is taken unless a reason for another hour is given; both are recorded here. High.

**What "sail" counted then.** Where a dispatch gave a fleet's strength it counted ships of the line apart from the rest. Calder's own dispatch has the enemy at "20 Sail of the Line", with ships armed en flûte, frigates and brigs listed after them, and his own as 15 of the line, two frigates, a cutter and a lugger (*Naval Chronicle* XIV, p. 163). Nelson's frigates reported the Toulon fleet as eleven of the line, seven frigates and two brigs (Nicolas VI, p. 396). Where strength was the subject, bare "sail" meant ships of the line too: Nelson's memorandum of 9 October supposes the enemy at "46 Sail of the Line" against a British 40 (his margin note, Nicolas VII 90 n.) and leaves "the remainder of the Enemy's Fleet, 34 Sail", the 46 less the twelve to be cut off, to the Commander-in-Chief (VII 91). His letter to Lady Hamilton of 19 October, in its lines of the 20th, shows both uses: "forty Sail of Ships of War, which I suppose to be thirty-four of the Line, and six Frigates" (VII 132). In a sighting the same word took in every vessel in view (the Defiance's "24, then 27 sail" when 20 of the line were there; Corbett 1910, p. 196).

**Villeneuve, 30 March to 20 August.** His frigates and brigs sailed with him and are not counted.
- **11 from 30 March**, lettered from about 3 p.m., as the squadron comes onto the chart; he weighed at about 4 p.m. Four 80s (Bucentaure, Formidable, Neptune, Indomptable) and seven 74s (Pluton, Mont-Blanc, Berwick, Atlas, Swiftsure, Scipion, Intrépide), with six frigates and two brigs (Desbrière 1907, pp. 6–7, 9). James has 29 March, his slip in converting the Republican date (III 339). High.
- **14 from about 2 a.m. on 10 April**, when all were under sail off Cádiz (1.30 by the Atlas's log): the Aigle, the Argonauta and the América joined (Desbrière 1907, pp. 9–11, 26 n.). This is the owner's choice. At dawn on the 10th only the Argonauta was with the 12 French, and the América rejoined on the 12th by Reille (p. 11) or "next morning" by Villeneuve's report (p. 10), so strictly the fleet was 13 for one or two days; James has 17 getting under way together, with the San Rafael following (III 342). High.
- **17 from 14 May**, hour not recorded, 4 p.m. taken: the Firme, the Terrible and the España, with the frigate Magdalena, found beating into the bay of Fort-de-France as the fleet came in and anchored during the day (Villeneuve's report, Desbrière 1907, pp. 25–26). James has the three crossing with the fleet and his date is the 13th, his slip (III 342–343). High for the day, low for the hour.
- **18 from about 3 p.m. on 16 May**: the San Rafael, fired on from Diamond Rock at 9 a.m., anchored with the fleet "the same afternoon" (James III 343–344; Desbrière 1907, p. 26). High for the day, medium for the afternoon.
- **20 from 4 June**, hour not recorded, noon taken: Magon's Algésiras and Achille joined at Fort-de-France the day the detachments came back from Diamond Rock (Desbrière 1907, p. 36); James has the junction at sea, on the day the fleet sailed or the next (III 351). 14 French and 6 Spanish. High for 20, medium for where Magon joined.
- **18 from about 8.30 p.m. on 22 July**: off Finisterre the Firme struck soon after 8 p.m. and the San Rafael a few minutes later (James III 361). Lauriston, writing from Cádiz on 2 September with news from the two Spanish captains, has the San Rafael striking at 9 p.m. (Desbrière 1907, annexes p. 111); Desbrière's narrative has both fighting on through part of the night and striking at daybreak on the 23rd (pp. 63–64). High for the day, medium for the hour.
- **15 from 31 July**, noon taken: out of Vigo without the Atlas, the España and the América, left there (Desbrière 1907, pp. 85–86); James has the 30th. 13 French and 2 Spanish. High.
- **13 from 2 August**, noon taken: on entering the bay, with Gravina already in the channel, the Argonauta and the Terrible had to go on into Ferrol under the Emperor's order, and the 13 French anchored at A Coruña (Desbrière 1907, p. 86). An hour is recorded: Escaño has the Argonauta and the Terrible anchoring at Ferrol at 9 a.m., the French going on to A Coruña (annexes p. 7), so the split was over by then; the label changes at noon (`src/scenes/02-road/squadrons.ts`), three hours after it. James, Mahan and Corbett's narrative have him in on 1 August. High for the split, medium for the day.
- **26 from about 2 p.m. on 10 August**: the French had worked out of A Coruña on the 8th to 10th; on the 10th Gourdon's five (Fougueux, Redoutable, Argonaute, Duguay-Trouin, Héros) and eight Spaniards came out of the ría of Ferrol; about noon the wind died before the last three cleared the narrows, and all anchored in the bay of Ares (Desbrière 1907, p. 97). Escaño has them out at 12.30, with nine Spaniards, which would make 27, in an entry for the 10th to the 13th (annexes p. 7). Medium.
- **29 from about 9 a.m. on 13 August**: the last three Spaniards joined in the forenoon, and all were under sail by 2 p.m. (Desbrière 1907, pp. 97–98); Escaño has the fleet sailing from the ría of Ares at 9 a.m. (annexes p. 7). 18 French and 11 Spanish. James has the fleet out on the 11th. High for 29 and the day; medium for the hour (9 a.m. is Escaño's sailing from the ría; Desbrière has the last three joining before noon).
- **29 at Cádiz on the evening of 20 August**, to the label's end: the six Spaniards already in port were not in company, their fitting-out ran on into October, and they are not counted; James counts 35 of the line in port (III 375; Desbrière 1907, pp. 107, 134). High.

**Nelson, 3 April to 19 August.**
- **11 from 3 April**, when he sailed from Pula Roads at 5.30 a.m. (Nicolas VI 392, 398 n.): the Victory, Royal Sovereign, Canopus, Superb, Spencer, Swiftsure, Belleisle, Conqueror, Tigre, Leviathan and Donegal. The count is his own; the names are inferred. High for the count.
- **10 from about 5 p.m. on 11 May**: the Royal Sovereign left with Knight between 4 p.m., when Knight came into the squadron, and 6.50, when Nelson bore up (the Victory's log, Nicolas VI 433). Knight's Queen and Dragon were in company for those hours, not under Nelson's flag, and are not counted. High.
- **12 from about 5 p.m. on 4 June**: off Carlisle Bay from noon, he anchored at 4.50 p.m. and found Cochrane's Northumberland there, the Spartiate "just joining" (Nicolas VI 445). High.
- **11 from noon on 13 June**: he weighed from St John's Road, Antigua, at 11.50 a.m., leaving the Northumberland there (Nicolas VI 452–453). High.
- **2 from 6 p.m. on 15 August**: off Ushant at "the close of the day" he left nine with Cornwallis and went on with the Victory and the Superb (Nelson to Marsden, 18 August, Nicolas VII 8); 7 p.m. would fit as well. High.
- **No label from noon on 19 August**: he struck his flag the day after reaching Spithead, hour not recorded (Nicolas VII, Preface; the log of the 18th, VII 8). The Victory lay at Spithead and the Motherbank, and at St Helens by 14 September. High.

**Nelson's passage, 14 to 28 September.**
- **"Victory" from 11.30 a.m. on 14 September**, when his flag was rehoisted in her at St Helens by her log, "about noon" by his letter (Nicolas VII 36). The frigate Euryalus, with him until he sent her ahead on the 26th, is not counted. High.
- **3 from noon on 18 September**: the Ajax and the Thunderer joined off the Lizard, in the morning by his letter to Marsden (Nicolas VII 45), at noon by his diary (Hudson 1917, p. 16); James and Beatty have them join off Plymouth. High.
- His figure holds until 6 p.m. on 28 September, when he joins the fleet off Cádiz (below). On the chart his label fades out with the squadrons' beat over p 0.648–0.658 of the scroll, from about 3.40 a.m. on 27 September to about 3 October on the beats' clock, as the fleets' own labels come in (`src/scenes/02-road/director.ts`, `applySquadrons`; main at `1a1378d`).

**Orde, 8 to 11 April.** 5 from 8 April: the Glory 98, the Defence 74 and the 64s Polyphemus, Agamemnon and Ruby; 6 from about midday on the 9th, when Strachan's Renown joined, the crews at dinner (Corbett 1910, pp. 62–63). High.

**Calder, 20 to 26 July.** 15 throughout, since Stirling joined on 15 July: the Prince of Wales, Glory, Barfleur and Windsor Castle (98s), the Malta (80), the Thunderer, Hero, Repulse, Defiance, Warrior, Ajax, Triumph and Dragon (74s), and the Agamemnon and Raisonnable (64s) (James III 356–357; Calder's dispatch, *Naval Chronicle* XIV, p. 163). His two prizes are not counted. He became 14 at 8 p.m. on the 26th, when the Windsor Castle went home with the prizes (James III 371), after his label's window closes at noon that day; James already counts 14 from the 23rd, leaving out the disabled Windsor Castle while she was in tow, where under the rule she was still in company. High.

**Collingwood, 19 to 22 August.** 3 from 19 August: the Dreadnought 98, the Colossus and the Achille (James III 375). 4 from midnight on 20–21 August, when the Mars came back from Tangier Bay and he tacked inshore "with his four sail of the line" (James III 375; Desbrière 1907, p. 143); Desbrière's narrative has him back off the port the same evening with four (p. 107). Bickerton's four, the Queen, Tonnant, Minotaur and Bellerophon, joined on the 22nd, hour not recorded, and made 8 (Desbrière 1907, p. 143; James III 379 names only the Queen); Desbrière's first list, after Leyland, who was certain only of the Queen, has the Mars for the Tonnant and puts the Tonnant with Calder (p. 109 and n.), which does not change the count. Codrington, who came out with Calder, has Collingwood with "six or seven" and Bickerton joining afterwards with three more (*Memoir* I 46), and Francis Austen's log has Calder's ships joining Collingwood's "five sail of the line" on 30 August (Hubback 1906, p. 142). His label closes at noon on the 22nd, as they join: from there he is the fleet off Cádiz, which the scene letters only from Nelson's arrival. High.

**Not lettered: the British off Cádiz, 22 August to 28 September.** About 26 after Calder joined on 30 August: Desbrière's corrected sum (p. 145), with the parts in James (III 379) and Corbett (p. 267), and Allemand's intelligence of 11 September (Desbrière 1907, p. 108). Desbrière's first count, 32 (p. 109), counted three ships twice and the Dragon and Goliath, which never arrived; he corrects it on pp. 143–145. Lower counts of the moment: from the San Sebastián tower 24 or 25 of the line, and on 3 September Lauriston's 5 of the line and 2 frigates at 3 leagues and 17 more ships at 6 leagues (pp. 125, 145); Francis Austen's "five sail of the line" with Collingwood when Calder's 18 joined (Hubback 1906, p. 142) would make 23. Medium.

**The British fleet off Cádiz, by date.** Lettered "Nelson" among its ships from the start of October to the dawn of the 21st (beats 4 and 5, and beat 0 as the camera rises from scene 1). The figures below are counts by date. On the chart both fleets' labels come in over p 0.655–0.675 of the scroll, about 1 to 10 October on the beats' clock, and stand at half strength only from about 6 October (`src/scenes/02-road/director.ts`, `fleetsShownAt`; main at `1a1378d`): the 29 gives way at about 2 % of its strength, the 23 stands at half or more for about 30 px of scroll before the 24 replaces it, and the combined fleet's 32 gives way at about 6 %, so under the owner's rule of 14 November 2026 (a label reads at half opacity or more) the 29 and the 32 never read and the 23 barely does; the figures from the 24 of 7 October on read.
- **29 from 6 p.m. on 28 September.** Nelson's three joined Collingwood's 26: 23 in the main body, the Victory's three among them, and 6 inshore under Rear-Admiral Louis (the Victory's log and signal log, Nicolas VII 53 n.; Nelson to Acton and to Elliot, 30 September, VII 54, 59; Collingwood's 26 named in Desbrière 1907, p. 143). All 29 were in company only if the Leviathan had not yet gone to Gibraltar (below). High for 29 under his command.
- **23 from 6 p.m. on 2 October.** Louis sailed for Gibraltar and Tetuan with the Queen, Canopus, Spencer, Tigre and Zealous. The log enters this under the nautical "October 3rd. P.M. at 6" (VII 63 n.; Nelson's diary, Hudson 1917, pp. 19–20; Austen, Hubback 1906, p. 148). The Leviathan was also away at Gibraltar: Nelson wrote on the 5th that he had sent "six Sail of the Line" and had "twenty-three Sail with me" (VII 75). High for Louis's five and for 23 by the 5th.
- **24 from the morning of 7 October**, 9 a.m. taken: the Defiance joined from Portsmouth ("this morning", Nelson to Marsden, VII 84). High.
- **26 from 4 p.m. on 8 October.** The Leviathan came back from Gibraltar, hour not recorded, and the Royal Sovereign joined at about 4 p.m.; the label takes the two as one step at 4 (VII 88 and n., 107; Nelson's diary, Hudson 1917, the transcript facing p. 20; Corbett 1910, p. 328; James III 380 for the Leviathan). High for the day, medium for the hour.
- **27 from the afternoon of 10 October**, 3 p.m. taken: the Belleisle joined from Plymouth, having made her number at noon ("this afternoon", VII 101; the diary, Hudson p. 21). High.
- **28 from the forenoon of 13 October**, 10 a.m. taken: the Agamemnon joined, with the frigate L'Aimable ("this forenoon", VII 119; the diary, Hudson p. 21; Corbett 1910, p. 330). High.
- **28 through the night of 13–14 October.** The Prince of Wales left with Calder on the evening of the 13th (VII 119–120; Beatty 1825, pp. 5–6; Corbett 1910, p. 330; Desbrière 1907, p. 148 n.), and the Africa joined by the forenoon of the 14th (the Victory's log under the nautical "October 14th", VII 121 n.; VII 94 n., 121). Strictly the fleet was 27 for those hours, too brief a change to read; the label takes the two as one exchange at 6 p.m. on the 13th, so the figure stays at 28, one ship for the other. High for the Prince of Wales in the evening, medium for the Africa in the morning.
- **27 from 17 October to the battle**, 3 p.m. taken: the Donegal was sent ahead of her turn to Gibraltar for a ground tier of casks (the diary, Beatty 1825, p. 97; Hudson p. 22; Nicolas VII 126–127; James III 383). No hour is recorded. 3 of 100 guns, 4 of 98, 1 of 80, 16 of 74 and 3 of 64. High for the 17th, low for the hour.

The peak was 28. Sources that differ: James (III 379) and Beatty (1825, p. 2) give 27 on Nelson's arrival, James with 22 cruising about 15 miles off Cádiz and 5 inshore, Beatty with "five or six" inshore; James (III 383), Clowes (V 128) and Beatty (1825, pp. 4–6, as in 1807) imply a peak of 29, but only by their loose groupings: the Africa among the ships joining "between the 7th and the 13th" (Beatty) or the 9th and 13th (James, Clowes), and the Prince of Wales parting "on the 13th in the evening" (Beatty), against the Victory's log, which enters the parting first (VII 121 n.), and Nicolas, who has the Africa join on the 14th (VII 94 n.); James and Clowes put the Royal Sovereign "between the 9th and 13th" and the Prince of Wales on the 14th, the log's nautical date; Beatty's narrative has the Donegal leaving on the 18th (p. 6), probably the log's date; Desbrière has Louis sailing on 3 October (p. 148), the log's date read literally. High for 29, 23 by the 5th and 27; medium-high for the steps between.

**Louis's brief return, 4–5 October.** On the morning of the 3rd the Euryalus told Louis by signal that the enemy had embarked their troops. He sent the Zealous and the Endymion on with the convoy and turned back with the other four (Austen, Hubback 1906, pp. 148–149; James III 380). On the 4th they were in sight to the southward (the diary, Hudson 1917, the transcript facing p. 20; Codrington, *Memoir* I 53, took them for the ships expected from England). They were with the Commander-in-Chief on the morning of the 5th and sent off again that day (Austen; Beatty 1825, p. 4, has them rejoin on the 4th). Nelson counted 23 that day and called them "a good look-out Squadron" while the easterly lasted (VII 75, 77); against it they reached Gibraltar only on the 9th (Hubback p. 149). The label does not count those hours. Medium-high.

**The Leviathan.** She was at Gibraltar from early October and came back on the 8th "loaded" (Nelson to Collingwood, 8 October, VII 88; James III 380). No source records the day she went. The 29 of 28 September include her, and Nelson's 23 of 5 October leave her out. The Victory's log names only Louis's five and the Endymion parting on 2 October, and Austen makes Louis's group five of the line, so she did not sail with them. Nelson's letters of 2 and 5 October count the ships sent to Gibraltar and Tetuan as six (VII 63, 75). She had been in Louis's inshore squadron in August (Austen's log, Hubback 1906, p. 142), and on 1 October Louis came out to the fleet with only part of his squadron (Beatty 1825, p. 4), so she may have gone straight from the inshore station on 30 September or 1 October. The label takes her off with Louis's five at 6 p.m. on 2 October. Low for the day. Her own log (Bedfordshire Archives, X170/1/2) or the Victory's for those days would settle it; neither is online.

**The combined fleet in Cádiz.** Lettered "Villeneuve" among its ships over the same beats. **32 from 28 September** (a count: on the chart it falls in the fleets' fade-in and does not read; see "The British fleet off Cádiz, by date"): 18 French and 14 Spanish ready. Gravina told Villeneuve that day that his 14 were entirely ready and the Santa Ana, a three-decker just out of dock, would follow in four or five days (Gravina to Decrès, 28 September, Desbrière 1907, annexes p. 91); Desbrière has 14 Spaniards in the roads once the Rayo and the San Leandro went out on the 28th (p. 134). **33 from early October**, noon on the 3rd taken: Desbrière has the Santa Ana ready only in the first days of October (p. 134; his p. 136 paraphrases the same letter as nine days), and on 8 October the council of war counted her with the fleet, "à peine sortis de l'arsenal" (annexes p. 97). Medium; low for the day. Nothing else came or went until the sortie: the Terrible and the San Fulgencio lay disarmed or in dock (see "Left in port"), and British counts from outside of 34–36 (35 or 36 by Nelson on 28 September; 34, 18 French and 16 Spanish, by the Euryalus on 1 October, James III 380) take in ships in harbour that were not with the fleet. Seven of the line got out on the 19th and the rest on the morning of the 20th; the label stays at 33 through the sortie, one fleet under one flag, and at dawn on the 21st.

**What the chart draws for these figures.** A squadron at a track's head is a block of its ships of the line only, the ships its label counts, each place a ship or ships in turn: the frigate Euryalus is not drawn with the Victory in September, nor Calder's two prizes with his line, and off Finisterre the two that struck are the Firme and the San Rafael, Spanish glyphs. The October fleets draw the 27 and 33 of Trafalgar, each ship from the day she was with the fleet (the Defiance, Royal Sovereign, Belleisle, Agamemnon and Africa joining, the Leviathan away from 2 to 8 October, the Santa Ana from 3 October). The seven British ships of the line there in those weeks but not at Trafalgar (Queen, Canopus, Spencer, Tigre, Zealous, Prince of Wales, Donegal) are counted in the label on their days and not drawn: a fleet's ships of the line come in only where every one of them reads one by one, and off Cádiz the British fleet's do not before the Donegal has gone (`scripts/dev/fleet-gates.mjs` shows it).

**Drawn with other types, not counted wrong.** In the squadron blocks at the tracks' heads (`src/scenes/02-road/squadrons.ts`): Nelson's Canopus and Tigre, 80-gun two-deckers, are drawn as 74s and his Royal Sovereign, a first rate of 100 guns, as a 98; every Spanish ship of the line but the Príncipe de Asturias is drawn as a 74, the 80s (San Rafael, Argonauta, Neptuno) and the 64s (América, España) among them; Calder's Malta (80) and his two 64s and Orde's three 64s are drawn as 74s. The four French 80s are drawn as 80s. In the October fleets and at dawn (`src/scenes/02-road/fleets.ts`, `RIGS`), each at a size of its own: the Royal Sovereign and the Britannia (100s) as 98s, 1.03 times the 98's size; the Tonnant (80) as a 74, 1.03; the British 64s as 74s, 0.93; the Spanish 80s as 74s, 1.05; the San Leandro (64) as a 74, 0.93; and the Rayo (100) as a Spanish 112, 0.97.

**Doubts about the figures.**
- The day the Leviathan went to Gibraltar. Low.
- Whether the Africa joined in the night of 13–14 October or in the morning. Medium.
- The hour the Donegal left on the 17th. Low.
- The day the Santa Ana joined the fleet in the roads. Low.
- Whether Louis's four were in company on the 4th (Beatty) or the 5th (Austen); either way, for hours only.
- The six inshore on 28 September, of whom only the Canopus, Spencer and Tigre are certain.
- James's 27 on arrival, which leaves out two ships no source names as absent.
- The hour the Firme and the San Rafael struck.
- The hour the Firme, Terrible and España joined on 14 May, and whether eight or nine Spaniards came out of Ferrol on 10 August (26 or 27).
- Whether the strict reading should letter the British 26 for the night of 20–21 October, while the Africa was out of touch to the north. The label keeps 27: she was under Nelson's flag and fought.
- Whether the América's two days astern after Cádiz (13, not 14) should be lettered. The owner chose 14 from Cádiz.

**Sources for the figures** (public domain; the pages are given above): Desbrière 1907, with the annexes: https://archive.org/details/la-campagne-maritime-de-1805; Nicolas VI: https://archive.org/details/dispatchesletter06nelsiala; Nicolas VII: https://archive.org/details/dispatchesletter07nelsuoft (p. 53, the joining: https://archive.org/details/dispatchesletter07nelsuoft/page/n88/mode/1up; p. 54: https://archive.org/details/dispatchesletter07nelsuoft/page/n89/mode/1up); Hudson 1917: https://archive.org/details/nelsonslastdiary00nelsuoft; Beatty 1825: https://archive.org/details/authenticnarrati00beat (p. 2: https://archive.org/details/authenticnarrati00beat/page/n11/mode/1up; p. 97: https://archive.org/details/authenticnarrati00beat/page/n108/mode/1up); Hubback 1906: https://archive.org/details/janeaustenssailo00hubbuoft; James III: https://archive.org/details/navalhistoryofgr03jameuoft (p. 379: https://archive.org/details/navalhistoryofgr03jameuoft/page/n394/mode/1up); Corbett 1910: https://archive.org/details/campaignoftrafal00corb; Codrington, *Memoir* I (1873): https://archive.org/details/memoirlifeadmir00codrgoog; *Naval Chronicle* XIV, pp. 163–164: https://archive.org/details/navalchronicleco14londiala/page/n184/mode/1up; Clowes V: https://archive.org/details/royalnavyhistory05clowuoft. Searched without result for the Leviathan's day: the *Naval Chronicle* XIV and the web; her original log is at Bedfordshire Archives, X170/1/2 (https://bedsarchives.bedford.gov.uk/Newsletters/BedfordshireAndTrafalgar.aspx), not online.

#### The fleets in the close views

The ships of the line, frigates and small craft the scene draws ship by ship, and what each view shows (see "Not on screen in the two fleet views" under "Positions on the chart").

| Moment | Date | Ships of the line | Frigates and small craft | Drawn | Confidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The combined fleet in Cádiz | 9–19 October | 33 ready (18 French, 15 Spanish), all in the bay on the morning of the 19th, the Fougueux alone in the narrows; 35 of the line in port with the Terrible and the San Fulgencio, laid up, most likely at La Carraca | the frigates Cornélie, Hermione, Hortense, Rhin and Thémis and the brigs Argus, Furet and Observateur: 41 hulls | 32 at anchor in the outer bay, with the 5 frigates and 3 brigs: the 40 of the harbour's close view; the Fougueux in the narrows, outside it | High; low for La Carraca |
| The sortie, first day | 19 October | 7 out (6 French and the Spanish Bahama); 19 sail under way at 8 a.m., many anchoring again in the calm; 26 of the line still at anchor that night | the Rhin and the Hermione out in the forenoon, the Thémis in the evening, probably with the Cornélie and Hortense; the brigs stayed in | the 7 weighing from 7 a.m. about an hour apart, the Rhin at 6.15 and the Hermione at 8.15, the Thémis, Cornélie and Hortense from 4.30 p.m. (see "Assumptions and licences", 9) | High; medium for the Cornélie and Hortense |

### Signals in 1805, by day and by night

- **Flags.** The 1799 *Signal Book* numbered every signal and hoisted the number as numeral flags, one per digit (No. 370 took three), in bold designs of red, white, blue and yellow. Their values were changed in 1803–04, and over 80 signals were added by hand (Perrin 1922; FOTW, from a 1799 copy). High; the 1804 values were not transcribed.
- **Popham's telegraph.** A diagonal red and white flag announced a message, one hoist per word or phrase from Popham's numbered vocabulary (1803). Issued to ships of the line from 1803; in September 1805 the Admiralty decided every ship, cruisers included, should carry it (Corbett 1910, pp. 298–301). Which half was red is disputed: Popham says only "a diagonal red and white flag" (1803, p. 6), Perrin white and red, Wilson red over white and Kent white over red, and Barraclough found both in contemporary books (all via FOTW); invisible at chart scale. High for the code, medium for the flag's colours.
- **Repeating.** A signal stayed flying until the ships in turn had repeated it to the commander-in-chief, and his answer went back down the line (Popham 1803, p. 6; *Signal-Book* 1796). This rule made a chain work. High.
- **Guns.** Guns drew the eye to a hoist or were part of a signal: two for a general chase. On 10 October Nelson ordered the ship bringing news of a sortie to fire a gun every three minutes, and rockets from the masthead (Nicolas VII 109–111). On 20 October the Euryalus's signals went with one, two, three or seven guns (NRS II 165, 167). High.
- **Distant signals.** When colours could not be read, shapes took their place: "a flag, a ball, and a pendant" at different mastheads (Cumby 1899, p. 719), as Nelson had ordered (Nicolas VII 70–71). Medium: one eyewitness, writing years later.
- **Reading range.** No period figure was found; colours were probably readable at about 4–11 nm, depending on haze. On 19 October the Mars's colours could not be read with a glass when she was hull-down, some 15–20 nm off (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R22). Low.
- **Night numerals.** The 1799 *Night Signals* made a number from guns (tens), lights in figures (one to four) and false fires or rockets (five); guns went five seconds apart, and a signal was answered with one light. Near an enemy, lantern figures replaced the guns only for the signals the book draws on p. 25: the fleet's manoeuvres, and No. 8 (a ship has passed an enemy without taking possession); the look-outs' reports of the enemy (Nos. 17, 21–29, 33, 35–37) kept their guns (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R15). High.
- **Watching at night.** Watching ships were to keep the enemy in sight, hide their lights from him, report by secret signals the Admiral set, and uncover signal lanterns all at once and keep them shown (*Night Signals* 1799, pp. 39–41). High.
- **Night lights.** Lanterns were plain; no coloured glass is recorded, and red and green sidelights date from 1848 and 1858. False fires, a blue flame in a wooden tube, burned for half a minute to several minutes (Smyth 1867, the only definition found); blue lights, or Bengal lights, are taken to burn alike, with a bluish-white glare (inference from period compositions: *Encyclopaedia Britannica*, 1778, "Pyrotechny"). Rockets threw gold sparks; strong colours came only after about 1811, with chlorate compositions (Physics World; Lancaster 2012, RSC). Gun flashes were orange-white, the report seconds behind (Fraser 1906, p. 94), and the French saw *amorces*, brief priming flashes (Desbrière 1907, annexes p. 154). French observers remarked on the colour of the British fires (Cornélie, Argus; Lucas in Fraser 1906, p. 91); the likeliest reading is bluish blue lights against their own yellow lanterns and gold rockets (`research/scene-02/h3-signals.md`, 3.8). Medium.
- **Nelson's night code.** His memorandum of 20 October: if the enemy stood south or towards the Straits, "burn two blue lights together, every hour"; if west, "three guns, quick, every hour" (Nicolas VII 136). High.

### The signal chain, 19 to 21 October

Times are ship's times as logged, and the clocks disagreed: the Euryalus ran about 45 minutes ahead of the Victory on the 20th (Corbett 1910, p. 339 n. 2), and the Phoebe and the Mars logged the same hailing at 12.20 and 1.25 a.m. Compare times across ships with that in mind. Bearings in the logs are by compass (see "Dates and conventions", "Bearings").

- **The chain as set.** Nelson's orders, his station and the cordon of 19 October are under "Nelson off Cádiz" ("The station", "Blackwood's squadron", "The chain"): the fleet out of sight to the west, the frigates inshore, ships of the line between. Of the 27 of the line off Cádiz (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R7), 3–4 were links on the 19th and 23–24 lay with Nelson. High.
- **The relay of 19 October.** The inshore links are timed in the Euryalus's log and signal log; the middle links are not logged; the last link, the Mars to the Victory at 9.30, is in Nelson's diary (Hudson 1917, p. 22) and Beatty (1807, p. 7), and only how it was made, the distant signal, rests on one eyewitness (Cumby 1899, p. 719). The Agamemnon was in the cordon (Beatty 1807, p. 6), but nothing shows she passed the signal (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R5–R6). The links of No. 370, in order from Cádiz: the Sirius, inshore off San Sebastián; the Euryalus; the Phoebe, sent west at 7.20 to repeat between the Euryalus and the fleet, and by 8 due west (true) of her; the Defence, 7–10 leagues west of Cádiz, with the Agamemnon stationed by her; the Colossus, which lay with the Mars by the diary of 14 October; and the Mars, 5 leagues east of the fleet and the nearest ship to it, which made it to the Victory. The Naiad was placed between the Euryalus and the Phoebe only at 8.10, after No. 370 had gone on, and carried Blackwood's telegraph messages from 10.20 (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R5). High for the logged links, medium for the rest.

| Time (ship's) | Link | What happened | Source | Confidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 6.04 a.m. | Sirius to Euryalus | telegraph in seven hoists: the enemy have hoisted their topsail yards | NRS II 160; Corbett 1910, p. 333 (six o'clock); the Sirius's log enters it under its "At 4" watch heading | High |
| 7.00 | Sirius to Euryalus | No. 370, "enemy's ships are coming out of port" (the NRS editor, from the 1799 book; "or getting under weigh" in Cumby 1899, p. 719, "or getting under sail" in Corbett 1910, p. 333); the northernmost ships under way | NRS II 145, 160 | High |
| 7 (Sirius's clock) | Phoebe | makes all sail "to the northward, firing guns" | NRS II 312 (the Sirius's log) | Medium |
| 7.20 | Euryalus to Phoebe | No. 370, with No. 163 "West" (repeat signals between the Admiral and the look-outs to the west); the Phoebe sent off | NRS II 145, 160–161. Corbett's ten minutes later (7.10) has no support in the logs | High |
| 8.00 | Phoebe | WNW by compass (due west true) of the Euryalus, under sail, "firing three minute guns"; the Defence in sight from the Euryalus's masthead | NRS II 146 (the Euryalus's log) | High |
| 8.10 | Naiad | placed between the Euryalus and the Phoebe; carries the telegraph traffic from 10.20 | NRS II 146, 161–164, 302 | High |
| about 8.10 to 9 | Euryalus to Weazle and Pickle | not a link: the Weazle sent by telegraph with the news for Gibraltar and Tetuan, to bring out Louis's ships, the Pickle to tell every ship off Cape Spartel that the enemy was out; the Weazle signalled to part company at 8.40 (the Euryalus's signal log), the Pickle ordered off at 9, both parting at 9 by the Sirius's log | NRS II 146, 160–161, 312; Blackwood to his wife, Nicolas VII 131 n. | High |
| not logged | Defence, Colossus | repeat, probably with flags and guns | `research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R5 | Medium (Defence), low (Colossus) |
| about 9 | Mars | a ship seen from the fleet firing guns and making signals (Walker names none); hull-down, her colours unread, the Mars hauls her flags down and makes the distant signal | Walker, NRS II 322; Cumby 1899, p. 719 | Medium |
| 9.30 (Victory) | Victory | answers the Mars and makes the general chase in the SE quarter: one flag, the SE compass flag, two guns; then prepare for battle | Hudson 1917, pp. 22–23; Beatty 1807, p. 7; Corbett 1910, p. 333 n. 1 | High (Browne's letter: 10 a.m.; Walker: about nine) |
| within 15 minutes | the fleet | under all sail on the general chase for the Strait: E by S, then SE by E by compass, about 80° then 103° true; the wind S by E, then WSW | Walker, NRS II 323; the Téméraire's log in Corbett 1910, p. 333 n. 1 | Medium |
| about 2 p.m. (Beatty) or 3 p.m. (diary) | Colossus (and Mars) to Victory | a second signal down the chain: the enemy's fleet is at sea, though only seven of the line were out | Beatty 1807, p. 7; Hudson 1917, p. 23; Nicolas VII 131; Corbett 1910, pp. 333–334; `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-30c | High (event), medium (hour) |

- **How long.** About 2½ hours on the logged clocks: 7.00 by the Euryalus, 9.30 by the Victory. Blackwood wrote "within two hours" in a letter of that day, written while he was "talking to Lord Nelson" by Popham's telegraph, his frigates repeating it along the chain (Nicolas VII 131 n.; the Euryalus's telegraph messages to and from the Naiad, 10.20 a.m. to 1.50 p.m., NRS II 161–164); Browne's 10 a.m. makes three; if the Euryalus's clock already ran 45 minutes ahead of the Victory's, as Corbett found on the 20th, it was about 3¼ (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R10). Medium.
- **Direction and length.** Nelson's original diary has Cádiz "ESE by compass", 16 leagues, at the chase (Hudson 1917, p. 23; Beatty 1807, app. p. 97): about due east true. The noon letter ("E.S.E., 16 Leagues", Nicolas VII 132), Blackwood (16 leagues) and Browne ("east, 16 leagues", NRS II 194) agree; Corbett and Mahan round the distance to fifty miles, and Robinson's "twenty leagues" (1858) is a late memory. The Victory's noon position, 36°36′ N 7°30′ W, puts it 19 leagues off (Corbett 1910, p. 333 n. 1). So the chain ran about due west (true) of Cádiz, 48–58 nm long. The "E.N.E." in Nicolas VII 133 is a copying slip, and the "W.S.W." of James (III 389) and Nicolas's narrative probably derives from it. High.
- **Weather and light.** Off Cádiz, clear with light airs, calm by noon (NRS II 145–146); at the fleet, an easterly wind, then very light airs from S to SSW at the chase (Hudson 1917, p. 22; Beatty 1807, p. 7). Sunrise about 6.26; the sun stood about 6° up at 107° true at 7.00 and 32° up at 135° at 9.30 (computed, local apparent time). High.
- **19 and 20 October.** That night rockets and blue lights went along the chain as the fleet stood for the Strait, six fast ships ahead carrying lights (NRS II 146–147, 302; Hudson 1917, p. 23). On the 20th, in rain, signals went with minute guns. At 5 p.m. by the Victory (5.40 by the Euryalus, NRS II 149; "a little before sunset" in Beatty 1807, p. 9) Nelson signalled that he relied on Blackwood to keep sight of the enemy, and the Naiad reported 31 sail NNE (at 5 in the original diary, Hudson 1917, p. 24, and in Beatty's appendix; "shortly afterwards" in James; 6 p.m. in Nicolas VII 137, from a facsimile). High.
- **The night chain, 20–21 October.** Nelson's memorandum set it: Blackwood with two frigates in sight of the enemy, two more between him and the Defence, the Colossus between the Defence and the Mars, and the Mars in touch with the Victory (Nicolas VII 136). The logs put the Euryalus and Sirius by the enemy and the Phoebe and Naiad between. The schooner Pickle, sent to Cape Spartel on the 19th, was with the fleet again by the afternoon of the 20th; from 5.40 p.m. she repeated the Sirius's signals to the fleet, and she kept company with the Sirius until midnight (NRS II 146, 317; Nicolas VII 135 n.). High.
- **Its length.** The Euryalus kept 2–3 nm on the enemy's weather beam. At midnight the enemy's body lay about 3 miles SE by S of her (NRS II 149; SE by E in Nicolas VII 134 n.) and the British fleet's light 5 or 6 miles to the south and west, so the night chain was under 10 nm long, not 50. High for the stations, low for the distances.
- **What the logs record.** From about 6 p.m. the frigates burned blue lights and false fires and fired rockets and guns all night, and the fleet answered; the Mars saw blue lights NE at 2 a.m. and ENE at 3.20 (NRS II 149, 245, 306, 313). Some links worked by hailing: the Phoebe spoke the Mars at 12.20 a.m. (1.25 by the Mars's clock), the Naiad the Colossus at 1.30 (2.00) (NRS II 245, 265, 303, 306). Nelson wrote that the frigates and look-out ships told him by signals which tack they were upon (Hudson 1917, p. 24), and Beatty that counted guns with false fires and blue lights reported each change and were repeated from the Euryalus to the Victory (1807, pp. 9–10). High.
- **Which half of the code.** The Combined Fleet stood S to SSW on the starboard tack most of the night (Desbrière 1907, annexes pp. 159, 178; Fraser 1906, pp. 91–92): the case for paired blue lights. James (III 388) has it standing north-west after 7.30 p.m., against the French reports, and Beatty (p. 10 in both editions) has the enemy wearing twice in the night, which the French reports cited here do not mention (see "At sea"). No log says whether the frigates also used the 1799 book's tack signals. Medium.
- **The fleet's wears.** At 8 p.m. (8.40 in James III 390) the fleet wore to the SW by Night Signal No. 31, the Victory burning blue lights to show her position (Corbett 1910, p. 340 n. 2). At 4 a.m. it wore again, to the NE by Nelson's diary (Hudson 1917, p. 24) but N by E by the Victory's log (Corbett 1910, p. 340), the wind WNW (Mars at 4, Dreadnought and Conqueror 4.20, Phoebe 4.30 by their clocks). Beatty's narrative (p. 11) has 2 a.m., against his own appendix and the logs. By the book each ship showed three lights while wearing; no log records it. High for the wears, medium for the lights.
- **Dark and light.** Moonless, cloudy and very dark. The watching ships hid their lights (an officer of the Defence, Fraser 1906, p. 95), and the British side showed little but the Admiral's lights, answers and blue lights. The Combined Fleet showed stern and top lights along a line some six miles long, with rockets, flares and guns of its own (stern and top lights, rockets, and guns and lanterns for the admiral's signal at midnight: Prigny, Desbrière 1907, annexes p. 139; signals with guns and rockets at 7.40 p.m.: the Sirius, Nicolas VII 134 n.; flares: Lucas, Fraser 1906, p. 92; six miles: Fraser 1906, p. 95). High.

### How the chart shows the signals

**As built, 14 November 2026: none of it is drawn yet.** The chart has no signal hoists, gun puffs, blue lights, rockets or lanterns, and no smoke at Finisterre. Of the chains it has only the ships, placed from the tables at the end of this section: the Defence, Agamemnon, Colossus and Mars stand at their places in the chain of 19 October from 28 September, before the chain was set (4 October) and before those stations (14–15 October), until 9.30 a.m. on the 19th, when they join the chase (`src/scenes/02-road/movements.ts`, `CHAIN`; "Assumptions and licences", 8), but nothing on them signals. **Planned**, in this order: the smoke at Finisterre and the fleets' lanterns at night; then the relay of 19 October in beat 4, and with it the night signals of 20–21 October. The owner's answers of 14 November 2026: the smoke at Finisterre goes up only through the part of the camera's pause over the action when the firing really went on (from about 5.15 p.m. to its end, between about 7.30 and 9.30 p.m. by the sources: "Cape Finisterre, 22 and 23 July", "The action"); the chain's four ships of the line, the Defence, Colossus, Mars and Agamemnon, are shown apart from the fleet, as the repeating ships they were, so they may show on their own while Nelson's main body shows as its label; and the night signals of 20–21 October (blue lights, rockets, guns) are built with the relay, as the same chain by night. In the record the links of that night were the same kind of ships, the four frigates and, by Nelson's memorandum, the Defence, Colossus and Mars (not the Agamemnon, though she repeated signals by day on the 20th), but they formed a short chain between the closing fleets, under 10 nm long, not the line of 48–58 nm due west of Cádiz (see "The night chain, 20–21 October" and "Its length"); the night of 19–20 October, when rockets and blue lights went along the chain as the fleet stood for the Strait, is the nearest to the same chain by night (see "19 and 20 October").

What beats 4 and 5 are to show, and why: the plan, as researched. The two tables at the end give the positions the sources support; the scene places its glyphs from them.

**By day: the relay of 19 October (beat 4).**

- **Geometry.** A loose line about due west (true) of Cádiz, 48–58 nm long and dipping south at the Defence: the frigates within 17 nm of San Sebastián, the Defence about 27 nm out, the Mars and Colossus 15 nm short of the fleet (the Defence–Mars gap about 15.7 nm, inside masthead range; `research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R31), the Victory with 23–24 of the line near 36°34′–35′ N 7°24′ W (table below). Never south-west. Medium for the line, low for single ships.
- **One link.** Two or three flag dabs at the masthead, hand-tinted red, white, blue and yellow, and a counted pair or three of white gun puffs at the side; after a pause the next ship does the same. Hoists stay up until the Victory answers, so lit links accumulate westward, then come down as the answer runs back. Neither is logged on the 19th: the held hoists follow the rules, and the counted puffs follow the counted guns the Euryalus logged with her signals on the 20th (NRS II 165, 167), while along the chain the records of the 19th have only guns fired by the Phoebe and by a ship seen from the fleet (NRS II 146, 312, 322; see "Assumptions and licences"). Medium.
- **The ends.** At first light, the Sirius's seven telegraph hoists under the red and white flag. From 7.20 the Phoebe makes sail (to the northward by the Sirius's log) and by 8 lies due west (true) of the Euryalus, a puff every three minutes of story time; the Naiad joins between after 8.10. At the Mars, coloured flags the fleet cannot read, then three dark shapes at three mastheads. At the Victory, one flag, the SE compass flag and two puffs, and the fleet makes sail for the Strait, first about E by N, then about E by S true. Medium.
- **Light and air.** At 7.00 a low, warm sun about 6° up in the ESE (107°), roughly behind Cádiz seen from the west; by 9.30 it stands about 32° up in the south-east (135°), and the light is no longer warm (about 37° at 147° if the Victory's clock ran 45 minutes slow). Light airs: flags barely stream and smoke hangs (inference). No muzzle flash by day. High for the light, medium for the rest.
- **Pacing.** 2½ hours of story time in discrete pulses: the inshore steps within 20 minutes, then the long run west. Date line 19 October 1805; if hours are shown, 7 a.m. at Cádiz and 9.30 at the Victory. Medium.

**By night: 20–21 October (beat 5).**

- **Geometry.** A short chain between the closing fleets, running SW from the enemy's western flank: the Euryalus about 3 nm NW to W of the enemy's body, the Sirius 4 nm WSW of it with the schooner Pickle in company, then the Phoebe, Naiad, Defence, Colossus and Mars, and the Victory 5–6 nm SW of the Euryalus (table below). Place them on the enemy's track, itself uncertain (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-32i). Low.
- **Paired blue lights, hourly.** The leading motif: two small, intense bluish-white flares together at a frigate, lighting her sails and their own smoke for half a minute or more; minutes later the same at the next link, down to the Victory, answered by one light. Medium.
- **Between them.** Gold rockets from the masthead, a streak of a few seconds and a small burst; counted orange-white gun flashes, each leaving a lit puff; brief priming flashes. Busiest about 6 p.m., 9 p.m. and midnight. Medium.
- **The fleet's wears.** At 8 p.m. the Victory's No. 31 (three guns and two lights, or near an enemy a square of four lanterns) and her blue lights; then two lights at each ship's ensign staff and one at her bowsprit end while the fleet wears in succession from the rear. At 4 a.m. the same (the Africa saw gun flashes WSW at 4, NRS II 297); no log says whether the Victory burned blue lights then. Medium.
- **Lanterns and the dark.** The British side: a few warm points, the Admiral's lights and answering lanterns shown all at once and held; the watching ships dark. The enemy: a string of warm points about 6 nm long, with its own gold rockets and gun flashes. No moon until a thin crescent rises at about 5.20 a.m. High.
- **Range.** Lanterns carry about 1–3 nm, blue lights about 5–14 nm, rockets and gun flashes further (estimates, `research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R21). The night chain could see itself end to end; British hulls stayed unseen. Low.
- **Date line.** "20 October 1805" until midnight and "21 October 1805" after it, since the hailings (12.20 to 2 a.m.), the Mars's blue lights (2 and 3.20) and the 4 a.m. wear fall on the 21st; or "20–21 October 1805" for the whole night beat. High.

**What would be wrong.**

- **Later inventions.** Semaphore arms or shutter telegraphs on ships; flashing lamps (adopted in 1867), beams or searchlights; red or green lights or lanterns; International Code flags. High. Vivid red or green fireworks are wrong too: before about 1811 firework colour was weak at most, pale reds and yellows by *Physics World*, faint greens and reds by the RSC (Lancaster 2012). Werrett (2015) argues that early modern manuals already gave a variety of recipes for coloured fire and that the later stress on colour comes from writers of the Chemical Revolution; the French remarks on the colour of the British fires are read under "Night lights". Medium.
- **Wrong in kind.** A glowing line or travelling light between the ships (also on the avoid-list); an instant relay; a chain of frigates only; a day chain drawn south-west; lantern figures as the frigates' reports; flags at night; a brightly lit British fleet; moonlight. High.

The interpolated point and the dawn position imply a track of about 150° true (the drawn centroid of all 33, Gravina's squadron with them; about 147° to the main body's centre, 36°09′ N 6°14′ W) against the signalled 193°: the track is drawn loosely, and the glyphs face the signalled course only at its keys (see "Assumptions and licences", 7) | Low |
