Positions are worked by plane sailing from San Sebastián light (36°31.7′ N 6°19.0′ W), with compass bearings corrected by 20–21° W (see "Dates and conventions", "Bearings"). All lie in open water, the chain's positions about 30–550 m deep today (EMODnet). The night positions move with the enemy's track.

### Positions on the chart

- **Method.** Logged positions are worked by plane sailing from San Sebastián light (36°31.7′ N 6°19.0′ W), compass bearings corrected for 21° W variation (see "Dates and conventions", "Bearings"). Every point was checked against the chart's own land (Natural Earth 10 m), its 15″ ETOPO tile and modern depths (EMODnet, from the Spanish hydrographic model of the bay; open water today, not 1805 soundings). In the bay they were also checked against Faden's *Plan of the Harbour of Cadiz* (London, 22 September 1797), from the Spanish chart of Vicente Tofiño (the sheet gives no date; his *Atlas Marítimo de España* appeared in 1789), georeferenced on eight forts and towers (Santa Catalina at El Puerto, Puntal, Matagorda, San Sebastián, Torregorda, Puertas de Tierra, Fort San Luis, Rota) with a residual of 0.18 nm rms over the eight points and about 0.1 nm at the forts round the bay. Medium.
- **The bay in 1797.** The plan sounds the entrance between Las Puercas (36°32.9′ N 6°18.2′ W) and the shoals of El Diamante (about 36°33.6′–34.6′ N 6°17.4′–18.0′ W) at 6–7 (fathoms or Spanish brazas, about 10–13 m), where French ships anchored after the battle "entre les Porques et le Diamant, par les 7 brasses d'eau", and the outer bay north-east of it at 5¾–7. It draws a line of seven Spanish ships of 1797 in the outer bay, running about north–south (true) along about 6°15.8′ W from 36°33.5′ N to 36°31.8′ N toward the narrows. Two more stand in the narrows between the Puntal and Matagorda forts, and the rest of that fleet beyond them in the inner bay toward Fort Louis and La Cantera. Its north points to the left of the sheet, about 30° above the horizontal. It is a guide to the water, not to the moorings of 1805. The outer bay is a few metres shallower today (about 6–12 m in the glyphs' box), and the narrows are bridged. Medium.
- **The anchorage, 10–19 October.** `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` (H2-36c–e) gives the description: out of range of the city's guns, moored with springs, a main group in the outer bay north-east of the entrance shoals, and a few ships deeper in until the 19th, of which the sources name only the Fougueux (see "Outer and inner ships"). The main group is centred on 36°33.8′ N 6°16.4′ W and strung WNW–ESE over about 1.2 nm, from 36°34.0′ N 6°17.1′ W to 36°33.6′ N 6°15.7′ W, perhaps in two or three lines (not recorded): Gravina and Villeneuve had a defensive line of moorings studied, and the ships were to be placed so that, moored with springs, they could defend themselves (Desbrière p. 137; Escaño, annexes 98–99), but no source gives the lines' shape or the ships' order, so the three lines drawn are a choice. At the estimate's spacing its glyphs stay inside 36°33.4′–34.1′ N, 6°15.5′–17.2′ W: 5¾–7 on the plan, about 6–12 m today, at least 0.6 nm from every shore and 1.0 nm from the chart's coast. Drawn 1.2 times as far from the middle (see "Assumptions and licences", 16), they reach about 36°33.2′–34.2′ N, 6°15.3′–17.4′ W, no ship more than 0.26 km (0.14 nm) from her estimated berth. The 1.5–2 nm that `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` gives would reach El Diamante at the WNW end. Medium for the description, low for the coordinates.
- **Outer and inner ships.** The outermost ships, the seven that got out on the 19th, lie at the WNW end toward the passes, about 36°33.7′ N 6°17.2′ W, in 9 m today. That is 0.25 nm north-east of the point `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` (H2-36d) gives them, 36°33.5′ N 6°17.4′ W. That point lies on the line of the modern El Diamante buoys, in the fairway out of which `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` keeps the anchored fleet, so it serves here as the harbour mouth instead. Two eyewitnesses, in the Fougueux and the Montañés, saw the outermost ships weigh first (annexes 213, 373); that the seven were those ships, and only those, is an inference (medium-high). The Fougueux lies alone at the outer end of the Puntal–Matagorda narrows, about 36°31.0′ N 6°15.2′ W. She is the one ship any source places there: between the Puntal fort and the fort on the Puerto Real shore, too far into the bay to weigh easily, by her two reports (annexes 213, 216). That fort is read as Matagorda, the pair Escaño names for the Argonauta in August (annexes 8); Fort San Luis, about 0.5 nm east of it, stands on the same shore, and either way she lay in or just inside the narrows (medium-high). No source places the San Justo, the Rayo, the Santa Ana or any other named ship there on the 19th; the ships furthest in, which the Thémis reports still at anchor (annexes 273), and the ships Fraser has towed or warped toward the sea that evening (pp. 84–85, no source given), are not named (low for a second ship in the narrows). Her place is 8 m today, 0.2 nm from the Matagorda fort and 0.6 nm from the Puntal, in the channel where Faden's plan of 1797 draws two Spanish ships between the forts. The midpoint between the forts that `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` gives (36°30.7′ N 6°15.4′ W) falls on Natural Earth's coastline. Natural Earth has no Matagorda point at all: the chart shows open water east of the isthmus and no narrows, and the glyph stands 0.25 nm off the chart's Puntal shore. Low.
- **Frigates and brigs at anchor.** No source puts any frigate inside the lines, toward the city (high for that absence). The Thémis weighed with some ships and frigates that lay at the head of the anchorage; her report sets them against the ships furthest in, so the head is its outer end (annexes 273): medium that she lay there. The Rhin, towed out from 6.15, and the Hermione, named on the 18th for Magon's night sortie, most likely lay with the outer group (annexes 101, 207–208, 242): medium. The Cornélie and Hortense have no recorded berth; they were still at anchor that morning (medium). The brigs Argus, Furet and Observateur lay in the bay (high), their berths not recorded. They served the admirals as repeaters and messengers: in the line of battle of 6 October the Furet stood with Villeneuve's squadron, the Argus with Gravina's and the Observateur with Dumanoir's, abreast the Rayo (annexes 124), and that morning the Observateur's captain went about by boat with orders (annexes 268). The Argus had her topsails hoisted from 6 a.m. (annexes 268). Low for any berth.
- **Headings at anchor.** About 294° true: the line of the two anchors (NW and SE by compass), and the way a moored ship lay to the westerlies of 10–17 October and to the flood on the 18th–19th. Medium.
- **Not here.** The centre the research notes first gave (`research/scene-02/h2-boulogne-cadiz.md`, section 9.2), 36°33′ N 6°18′ W, lies 0.17 nm from the Las Puercas reef and 0.7 nm from the city wall, within range of its guns, against Escaño's statement that the fleet lay out of range (`research/scene-02/v-h2c.md`, H2-36d). It is also in about 4 m today, too shallow for a ship of the line (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-36d). High.
- **The harbour mouth.** The passes between Las Puercas and El Diamante: 36°33.5′ N 6°17.4′ W, in 9–10 m today, 0.9 nm north-east of Las Puercas, on the line of the modern El Diamante buoys and about 0.4–0.5 nm south of the shoal's shallow core (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-38b; `research/scene-02/a-v-draft-h2.md`, A1). Medium.
- **The sortie.** At noon on 19 October the Rhin's bearings, the San Sebastián tower SW 5° S and Rota NNW 5° N by compass, put her just outside the passes, about 36°33.7′ N 6°18.1′ W (annexes 208). At 4 p.m. the Algésiras lay about 36°33.0′ N 6°22.0′ W, about 3¾ nm W by S of the passes (Philibert's cross-bearings, Tour de Cadix SE½E and Rota NNE½E by compass, Desbrière 1907, annex p. 230; the 36.564 N in `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-38b, does not follow from them). At 6 p.m. Magon's division was about 36°29.4′ N 6°23.1′ W, 4 nm south-west of San Sebastián (W by S by compass), standing north (Euryalus; Desbrière's 2 leagues WSW of the light agrees within 2 nm). At 9 a.m. on the 20th the fleet was outside about 36°34′ N 6°25′ W (the Montañés's bearing of the San Sebastián tower). Medium; low for the last.
- **The watch on 19 October.** The inshore frigates kept close off the port; the chain ran about due west (true) from Cádiz and sagged south at its inner end, at the Defence (tables under "How the chart shows the signals"). The Weazle and the Pickle kept with the Euryalus (about 36°28.0′ N 6°21.0′ W at 5.30 a.m.) and the Sirius, within a mile or two of them, until 9 a.m. (NRS II 145–146, 312). Medium for the frigates and small craft, low for single ships of the line.
- **The main fleet.** Its October station was 15–20 leagues west of Cádiz on about the port's parallel, centred near 36°32′ N 7°22′–25′ W (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-40c). At 9.30 a.m. on 19 October Victory lay about 36°34′–35′ N 7°24′ W, between the diary's 16 leagues ESE by compass (36°33′ N 7°18′ W; Hudson 1917, p. 23; Beatty 1807, p. 97) and the noon log, 36°36′ N 7°30′ W: due west (true) of Cádiz. The "W.S.W." of James and of Nicolas's narrative follows the "E.N.E." misprint (`research/scene-02/a-h2b.md`, H2-20b). The fleet lay to, then made the general chase in the SE quarter (the SE compass flag; SE by compass is about 114° true), steering E by S, then SE by E by compass, about 80° then 103° true (Walker, NRS II 323; the Téméraire's log in Corbett 1910, p. 333 n. 1; see the relay table). Medium.
- **Dawn, 21 October: the British.** As in "The setting", some 20 nm west of Cape Trafalgar: about 36°08′ N 6°28′ W (±6′; `research/dawn-setting.md`, section 1.2). 26 of the line "sans ordre" in two groups strung north to south (Scipion), the Africa apart to the north; some ships 3–4 nm from the Victory at 4 a.m. (Mars, Ajax); heading N by E by compass, about 350° true, until they bore up after the Victory's signal No. 76 at 6.50 by her clock (about 06:40–07:00 in scene 1's notes), to ENE (about 047° true), then E by N (058° true) from 8 a.m. (the Victory's log). The Africa, parted in the night, had Cape Trafalgar SE 8–9 leagues at 6 a.m.: about 36°21′ N 6°30′ W. Desbrière (p. 179) has them about 21 miles north-west of the cape; the logged bearings put them west, slightly south of west (`research/dawn-setting.md`, section 1.2). Medium; low for Africa.
- **Dawn, 21 October: the combined fleet.** Its centre about 36°09′ N 6°14′ W, 9–12 nm east of Victory. 33 of the line in a ragged line about 5 nm long running north–south true (NNE–SSW by compass), in two irregular divisions with Gravina's squadron to windward of the van, heading about south (180–195° true) on the starboard tack under easy sail. At daybreak Gravina's flagship, the Príncipe de Asturias, led the whole line: both her journals put her at the head of a badly formed line (annexes 379, 384), Desbrière has her take the head in the night with the Thémis and the Argus near her (p. 165), and Prigny saw the squadron of observation working to take the head at dawn (annexes 139). On the starboard tack, heading south, the head is the line's south end. The Euryalus lay between the fleets, 5–6 nm west of them (`research/dawn-setting.md`, section 7). Medium.
- **Dawn, 21 October: frigates and small craft.** No log fixes any of them at first light; each place is an estimate from where the night left her. The British frigates lay between the fleets, where the night chain had them (see "The night chain, 20–21 October"). The schooner Pickle, last logged in company with the Sirius at midnight, hove to at 1.30 a.m. with the enemy ENE and the Commander-in-Chief SW by W, made sail at 3, and at 5 wore toward the enemy and set her fore topsail and topgallant sail (NRS II 317): about 36°11.5′ N 6°21.0′ W, near the Sirius, standing east. The cutter Entreprenante saw the enemy to the east at daylight and counted 35 sail, taking them all for ships of the line (NRS II 319–320), so she was with the British, too far off to tell the enemy's frigates from his ships of the line; as the admiral's tender she most likely kept near the Victory, about a mile on her lee, about 36°07.6′ N 6°26.9′ W. Of the French, the Cornélie had taken post close to leeward of the van at 11.30 p.m. (annexes 159); the Hermione and the Thémis were sent to windward of the line to reconnoitre by Villeneuve's signal at 6.15 a.m. and stayed there until near 10 (annexes 243; Desbrière pp. 166, 173–174); the Hortense and the brig Furet had their stations in the line of battle by the Bucentaure and the Santísima Trinidad, and were to leeward of Villeneuve's squadron from about 8.30 (annexes 124; Desbrière p. 174); the brig Argus was back by Gravina from 5 p.m. on the 20th and, after carrying his word down the line in the night, was under the lee of the Príncipe de Asturias by the opening of the battle (annexes 269; Desbrière p. 174). No source places the Rhin. Low for every place.
- **Where this differs from scene 1.** Nothing here contradicts "The setting" or "The fleets". It adds how far apart the British ships lay, Africa's place and the British heading at first light (the chart draws them on 047° true, the course after bearing up, some 20–30 minutes after scene 1's sunrise: a deliberate licence, the owner's decision of 1 November 2026). Villeneuve's own reckoning (Cape Trafalgar ESE about four leagues; Fraser 1906, pp. 110–111) puts his centre about 2½ nm north-west of the scene 1 position, and French officers put the coast 18–24 nm off (`research/dawn-setting.md`, F-13). Desbrière's midnight estimates cannot be joined to the dawn positions to better than 5–15 nm (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-32i), so the night tracks are drawn loosely and end on the dawn positions. Both fleets stand at their true spacing (the owner's decision, 1 November 2026), eased apart ship by ship so that every ship shows in the last frame (see "Assumptions and licences", 16): a line nearly five miles long (James III 389), its northern end about 7–9 nm off the coast by the British reckoning and 18–24 nm by the French, and single British ships 3–4 nm from the Victory at 4 a.m.; the logs say nothing of how far apart the two British groups were. Scene 1's brief table gives the courses after bearing up, ENE and E by N by compass, as "about NE then ENE true"; worked with 21° W they are about 047° and 058° true, NE and NE by E (`research/dawn-setting.md`, F-27). Medium.
- **Tetuan and Gibraltar.** The anchorages on Nelson's tracks: Tetuan Bay off the mouth of the Martil (35°38′ N 5°15′ W, 17 m), where he watered on 4–5 May, and Gibraltar Bay off the town (about 36°07′ N 5°22′ W, 15 m), in Rosia Bay, where he anchored on 19 July; the town points of `research/scene-02/h2-boulogne-cadiz.md` are on land (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-39). Louis's ships went to the same anchorages in October; they are counted in the British label while with the fleet and never drawn (see "Fleet sizes at each stage"). Medium.
- **Stations without tracks.** Orde, Calder and Collingwood have no drawn routes: each squadron's block stands at a station while its label is in the story (`src/scenes/02-road/director.ts`, `STATIONS`). Orde off Cádiz, drawing off toward Lagos: about 36°27′ N 6°33′ W, heading 280°. Calder off Finisterre: about 43°50′ N 11°33′ W, heading 245°, some 15 nm NNE of the smoke's planned place and at none of the four British positions under "Where"; during the action the two blocks are laid out as a meeting (see "Assumptions and licences", 18). Collingwood off Cádiz, chased toward the Strait: about 36°18′ N 6°27′ W, heading 150°. Estimates within the areas the sources give. Low.
- **Boulogne.** Natural Earth runs the coast at about 1°34.5′ E on the harbour's latitude, west of the Liane, so on the chart the harbour and its basin are land and the flotilla lies in the roads. Its twenty craft are a chart's convention, each standing for a division (see "Assumptions and licences", 6): seventeen at anchor in two lines parallel to the coast off Boulogne, Wimereux and Ambleteuse, the outer, mostly of the heavy craft, from about 50°42.4′ N 1°29.6′ E to 50°48.0′ N 1°31.5′ E, the inner, of the light luggers, from 50°42.7′ N 1°32.3′ E to 50°47.8′ N 1°34.0′ E, and three under sail about 3 km outside them (`src/scenes/02-road/boulogne.ts`, `LINES`, `UNDER_SAIL`). The camps, south to north, each drawn as rows of huts along its plateau and sized roughly by its corps' strength, not by a count of huts (`CAMP_PLANS`): Ney's corps at Étaples (about 50°32.0′ N 1°38.4′ E), Soult's left camp on the Outreau plateau (50°42.2′ N 1°35.6′ E), his right camp on the heights north of the harbour, beyond the Tour d'Ordre (50°44.6′ N 1°36.0′ E), Oudinot's grenadiers at Wimereux (50°46.8′ N 1°37.3′ E) and Davout's corps at Ambleteuse (50°49.2′ N 1°37.0′ E). They, Wimereux, Ambleteuse, Étaples and Pont-de-Briques all fall on the chart's land. High for which troops lay at which port (see "Which troops lay where"); low for each camp's place on its heights, which no source fixes for 1805 ("Boulogne from the sea", "Where they stood"); the flotilla's lines are a convention, not a position.
- **What the chart draws, October.** Each moment has its own beat. From the start of October (see "Assumptions and licences", 15), seen from far off, each fleet is lettered among its ships with its commander and its strength on the day ("Nelson, 27 sail"; the figures by date, and which of them read as the labels come in, are under "Fleet sizes at each stage"), and no glyph stands for it; its ships of the line come in together, and only where every one of them reads one by one (the owner's rule of 12 November 2026): the combined fleet's as the camera comes down over the harbour, the British fleet's not before the Donegal has gone on the 17th, so that the British ships drawn are always the 27 of Trafalgar, the figure lettered whenever any of them shows. The small craft that keep with a fleet's body (the Entreprenante; the combined fleet's frigates and brigs) come in with its ships; Blackwood's frigates off the port come in as they read. At anchor, to the sortie, all heading 294° true and drawn 1.2 times as far from the anchorage's middle as estimated (item 16 there): 32 of the line in three lines strung WNW–ESE in the outer bay (see "The anchorage, 10–19 October"), the seven that got out on the 19th at their WNW ends; the Thémis, the Rhin and the Hermione at the head, the lines' WNW ends; the Cornélie and Hortense inside the lines, on their southern side, toward the city, where no source puts a frigate (item 10 there); the Furet and the Observateur by the Bucentaure, the commander-in-chief's flagship, and the Argus by Gravina's, the Príncipe de Asturias, a cable or so south of each (the Observateur's station in the line of battle of 6 October was in Dumanoir's squadron, abreast the Rayo: see "Frigates and brigs at anchor"); and the Fougueux alone at the outer end of the narrows (see "The anchorage, 10–19 October", "Outer and inner ships" and "Frigates and brigs at anchor"). Every ship at anchor is drawn with her sails furled. Off the port lie Blackwood's four frigates with the Weazle and the Pickle; the Entreprenante keeps by the Victory with the fleet. On the 19th the Rhin weighs at 6.15 a.m. and is towed out, the seven of the line weigh from 7 a.m. about an hour apart, the Hermione at 8.15, and the Thémis, the Cornélie and the Hortense between 4.30 and 4.50 p.m.; all stand off the port, about 4 nm west of the passes, until the morning of the 20th. The Weazle and the Pickle part from the frigates at 9 a.m.: the Weazle stands south-east for the Strait and fades from the chart that afternoon; the Pickle runs to Cape Spartel, is back with the fleet on the 20th, and from the evening keeps with the Sirius. On the 20th the rest of the line weighs between 6 and about 6.50 a.m., the Fougueux from her place in the narrows, the Furet with them; the Rayo weighs last at about 8.20 and the Argus at 8.30; the fleet forms outside, about 36°34′ N 6°25′ W, by 11, and the Observateur stays at anchor to the end (see "Assumptions and licences", 5 and 9; `src/scenes/02-road/fleets.ts`, `movements.ts`). Medium for the layout, low for single glyphs.
- **What the chart draws on the 20th and in the night.** Beat 5 cuts away to Ulm. The fleets' ships and labels leave the story over p 0.835–0.85 of the scroll, about 2.30 to 6 p.m. on the 20th on the beats' clock; the camera rises and goes east to Ulm (p 0.855–0.885, about 6.50 to 9.10 p.m.); and the fleets come back over p 0.905–0.925, about 10.25 to 11.45 p.m., each on its night track toward its dawn place (see "Assumptions and licences", 7). So the fleets' afternoon of the 20th, the British wear at 8 p.m. and the combined fleet's evening heading are not on screen; the night tracks show from about 11.45 p.m. to dawn (`src/scenes/02-road/director.ts`, `fleetsShownAt`; `src/scenes/02-road/camera.ts`; main at `1a1378d`). Medium.
- **What the chart draws at dawn.** On 21 October both fleets stand at their places as the two "Dawn, 21 October" bullets give them (the owner's decision, 1 November 2026), eased apart ship by ship so that every ship shows in the last frame (see "Assumptions and licences", 16). The British lie in loose order in two groups strung north to south: Nelson's within about 2 nm of the Victory, at 36°08′ N 6°28′ W, and Collingwood's about 3½ nm to the south of her (single ships were 3–4 nm from the Victory at 4 a.m.), with the Africa apart, about 13 nm to the north, at her 6 a.m. position; all on 047° true. The combined fleet's main body stands in its ragged line north to south, about 5.2 nm long at the estimate's spacing and drawn about 7.4 nm, from about 36°13.3′ N to 36°06.0′ N, in two irregular divisions; Gravina's squadron lies to windward (west) of its southern half, with his flagship, the Príncipe de Asturias, at its head, the south end of the whole line, about 36°05.4′ N 6°16′ W. The whole is centred about 36°09′ N 6°14′ W, 9–12 nm east of the Victory, heading 180–195° true; the main body's northern end lies 6.7 nm off the chart's coast at the estimate's spacing (7–9 nm by the British reckoning), 5.7 nm as drawn. The British frigates lie between the fleets, where the night chain had them, the Euryalus about 5 nm west of the main body and 3 nm from Gravina's squadron (5–6 nm west of the enemy in the sources); the Pickle by the Sirius, heading 075° true toward the enemy; the Entreprenante about a mile on the Victory's lee. The French frigates and brigs lie as "Dawn, 21 October: frigates and small craft" has them: the Hermione and the Thémis to windward (west) of Gravina's squadron, the Cornélie to leeward of the line's south end, the Argus about a mile on the Príncipe de Asturias's lee, the Hortense and the Furet to leeward of the line's middle, about the Bucentaure, and the Rhin inshore of them, 6.3 nm off the chart's coast. The Observateur lies at anchor in Cádiz. As the camera settles on the last frame the clock comes to rest at dawn, and every ship stands still at her dawn place to the end (see "Assumptions and licences", 17). Each ship's place within her group is an estimate (see "Assumptions and licences", 10). Seen from the view of the whole Gulf each fleet is its label, never a lone flagship; its ships come in together as the camera comes down, once every one of them reads one by one, and the scene's last frame comes close enough for the whole formation to read, looking east-north-east into the sunrise from west of the British (see "Assumptions and licences", 1). Glyphs are drawn larger than true scale and kept off the chart's shore (see "Assumptions and licences", 3). Medium for the layout, low for single glyphs; the British heading is a licence (see "Assumptions and licences", 1).
- **Not on screen in the two fleet views.** The harbour's close view, held from about 7.45 to 8.40 a.m. on 19 October on the beats' clock (p 0.72–0.75 of the scroll; the camera comes down from about 6.35), looks north-north-east across the bay from over the city and shows the 40 allied vessels in the outer bay: 32 of the line, the five frigates and the three brigs. The last frame, at dawn on 21 October, looks east-north-east from west of the British and shows 72 ships: the British 26 of the line, four frigates, the Pickle and the Entreprenante, and the combined fleet's 33 of the line, five frigates and the brigs Argus and Furet (`decisions.md`, "Narrowed, ship by ship"; `src/scenes/02-road/camera.ts`). Every other vessel the sources put in the bay, or with either fleet, at those two moments, and why it is not seen there:
  - **The harbour view.** The Fougueux lay alone in the narrows, about 3 nm south-south-east of the main group, until she warped out at 5.30 p.m.; she is drawn there (until she weighs on the 20th, item 5 of "Assumptions and licences"), outside the frame. Blackwood's four frigates, with the Weazle and the Pickle, lay off the port a few miles south and south-west of San Sebastián, behind the camera, and are drawn there. The Terrible and the San Fulgencio were laid up, most likely at La Carraca, about 6 nm south-east of the anchorage, and were not with the fleet; the two Spanish ships judged worthless in August and the other ships laid up in the arsenal are not named in October; none of them is drawn. The Spanish frigate Flora and the corvettes that came from Ferrol with her (the Mercurio and the Indagadora, or one of them) did not sail, and their place on the 19th is not recorded, so they are not drawn. The French schooner Téméraire, which joined at Ferrol in August, is placed at Cádiz by no source and is not drawn. Gunboats at the harbour mouth on the 19th are contested, the Spanish sources against James, and none is drawn. The merchant and coasting vessels kept in the bay by the blockade were not researched and are not drawn.
  - **The last frame.** The Africa had parted in the night and lay about 13 nm north of the Victory at 6 a.m.; she is drawn there, outside the frame. The brig Observateur lay at anchor in Cádiz, about 25 nm north of the combined fleet, and is drawn there. The brig-sloop Weazle had left for Gibraltar and Tetuan at 9 a.m. on the 19th and missed the battle; she fades from the chart that afternoon. A third small sail that some observers counted with the British at dawn is contested and its identity unknown, a ship of the line misjudged at a distance or the merchant brig the Agamemnon detained on the 19th; it is not drawn (see "Open doubts"). Louis's five ships of the line were far east of the Strait with the Malta convoy, the Prince of Wales was on her way home and the Donegal had gone to Gibraltar on the 17th: none was with the fleet, and none is drawn (see "Fleet sizes at each stage").
  - High for who was where; low for the places of the ships laid up, and every small craft's and frigate's place at dawn is an estimate (see "Dawn, 21 October: frigates and small craft").

### Routes on the chart

- **How they are drawn.** The engine joins each route's waypoints with a centripetal Catmull–Rom curve in Mercator map space. Every waypoint, every straight segment and the drawn curve were checked against the chart's own coast (Natural Earth 10m land and minor islands): no line touches land, and every line keeps at least 2 nm off the coast except at anchorages and harbour approaches (Toulon road, Fort-de-France, Carlisle Bay, the Boca Grande, St George's, St John's, Tetuan, Gibraltar, Lagos, Vigo, A Coruña, Ares, the Cádiz channel, Dunnose, Spithead and St Helens road). Elsewhere the lines pass Cape Finisterre at 6.7 nm, Cape St Vincent at 6.1 (8.7 on the other lines), São Miguel's north coast at 3.3–3.6 and Diamond Rock at 2.5–2.6; Nelson's lines pass Tarifa at 2.3–2.9 nm and come within 2 nm of Europa Point and Punta Carnero only in the mouth of Gibraltar Bay; the September line passes the Eddystone at 2.8 nm and the coast by Dartmouth at 2.9 (drawn curve). Natural Earth lacks the Cíes, Ons, Sálvora, the Sisargas, Toro, the Hormigas, Isla Grosa, Tabarca, the Isla de Benidorm, the Columbretes, the Isla de Sancti Petri, the Eddystone, Wolf Rock, the Seven Stones, the Farilhões, the Las Puercas reef and Diamond Rock, so the lines were checked against them by hand, and at Vigo against OpenStreetMap's coastline: the Vigo legs use the south mouth of the ría, 0.6 nm or more from the rocks off the south end of the Illa de San Martiño (the drawn curve 0.9 nm), and about 0.5 nm or more from the shores inside it. Points that no source gives are marked "est." in the route data. The scene draws every route in its beat, from `research/scene-02/routes-draft.json` with the corrections of `research/scene-02/a-v-draft-h1.md` (rulings 1, 2, 11 and 16), and Nelson's September passage from `research/scene-02/nelson-september.json`. High for the check against Natural Earth, medium for the islands and rocks checked by hand.
- **Toulon to Cádiz.** Table A of `research/scene-02/h1-campaign.md`, section 7, refined by `research/scene-02/v-h1c.md` (H1-35) and `research/scene-02/a-h1ac.md` (H1-35e), then checked against the chart's coast (`research/scene-02/a-v-draft-h1.md`, "What changes on the chart"); see the table below. The Atlas's noon latitudes are firm, her longitudes exist only for 1–3 and 10 April, and the other longitudes keep the line in the channels the sources name. Changes from `h1-campaign.md`: the start is in the eastern half of the Grande Rade, about 0.8 nm off its north shore (its 43°06′ N 5°56′ E lies in the inner road on the chart's coast, and a first leg from it passed 0.06 nm from the chart's coast by Cap Sépet); row 2 is worked from the Atlas's log by compass; row 5 lies in the open sea south of the Gulf of Lion and row 6 south-east, not SSE, of Barcelona; the Strait is passed at about 11 a.m., not noon; the 4 p.m. point is just past Cape Trafalgar, off Conil; an estimated point in the offing keeps the line about 3 nm off Cádiz's west shore (a straight run from Conil to the anchorage passed 1.8 nm from San Sebastián castle); the anchorage is in open water 3.2 nm west of Las Puercas, not the outer-bay anchorage of 10–19 October; and three estimated points (east of Cabo de la Nao, off Garrucha and Águilas, south of Málaga) are dropped: they add nothing, and without them the line keeps 8.7 nm off Cabo de la Nao, 3.6 nm off the coast between Cartagena and Cabo de Gata and 5.3 nm off the Alborán Sea coasts. No point makes the line zigzag: its turns of 40° or more are real changes of course (row 4, where the fleet hauled west on 31 March; the dog-leg round Cabo de Palos to Cartagena, where it lay becalmed on the 7th; row 14, west for the Strait; row 17, round Cape Trafalgar), and none reverses it. Tested on the chart's coast, every point is at sea and no straight segment or stretch of the drawn curve touches land. Closest approaches (straight leg / drawn curve): Cap Sépet 2.1 / 2.2 nm; Punta Cires 2.8 / 2.4; the shore east of Cartagena 3.35 / 2.5; Cabo Tinoso 4.3; Tarifa 3.7 / 4.1; Cape Trafalgar 4.45 / 4.9; Europa Point 7.2 / 6.4; Punta Almina 7.2 / 6.7; Cape Spartel 10.2 (curve); San Sebastián castle 2.7 / 2.8; Rota 4.2. Islands Natural Earth lacks (curve): Hormigas 8.4, Isla Grosa 13.4, Tabarca 18.3, Isla de Benidorm 13.2, Columbretes 19.6, Isla de Sancti Petri 4.3, Las Puercas 3.2. Islands it has (curve): Alborán 5.4, Perejil 3.1. Leaving Toulon road, the first leg passes 1.4–1.5 nm from Natural Earth's east shore of the road's mouth, which is about 3.4 nm wide, so no exit can keep 2 nm from both sides; the road is the one stretch the rule exempts. The curve departs at most 4.4 nm from the straight legs, on the leg of 2–3 April between Barcelona and the Columbretes. Villeneuve's eleven sail at its drawing head as a squadron (see "Assumptions and licences", 3). High for the shape, medium for the line, low for the 4–8 April longitudes.
- **The crossing.** Table B of the same section, the Atlas's 32 noon positions by reckoning (Desbrière 1907, p. 26 n.), drawn with no day marks after the noon of 10 April, table A's last row, lettered "10 April" as the leg's first. Dropped as zigzags: 8 May (16°00′ N, 55 nm south of the line between 7 and 9 May) and 11 May (an impossible run of 5½°, about 330 nm, in a day). The last reckoning point lies about 1°50′ east of the truth, so the line runs on to the landfall, through the St Lucia Channel and up the lee coast to Fort-de-France. Medium.
- **Villeneuve's way home.** Table C (the places named, the track between them estimated), then table D: Corbett's chart after Desbrière, read to about 0.3°, with the Sally's position. The chart's daily marks stop on 24 June, and the line runs straight to Corvo (2 July). Left out: both 19 June positions of the Curieux, James's 30 June point NE of Corvo (it would double back), the doubtful noon of 23 July and the 40 leagues WSW of Finisterre of the 27 July letter. The Z of 9–20 July west of Portugal is kept because it is real, not reckoning noise; dropping 12 and 16 July gives a plain line. Medium; low from 25 July.
- **Vigo, A Coruña, and Ferrol to Cádiz.** Estimated courses round Finisterre and into the two bays; then table E: one British fix (the Iris, 14 August), the rest estimated; Reille's noon of the 15th (about 43°39′ N 11°48′ W if its bearing is true) lies about 12 nm south of the line. The stand towards Cape Prior and the morning chase of 20 August are left out as hooks. The line enters Cádiz bay by the channel between Las Puercas and the El Diamante shoal, 0.1–0.2 nm from the buoys and 0.4 nm from Las Puercas, and ends at the centre of the anchorage of 10–19 October (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-36d; see "Positions on the chart"). That is an estimate for the evening of 20 August, when the fleet anchored in Cádiz road (James III 375); where the ships lay at first, the Argonauta between the Puntal and Matagorda forts, is under "Into the roadstead". Low.
- **Nelson.** Tables F–J, as corrected in `research/scene-02/a-h1b.md`. In the Mediterranean the line starts at Pula (the Palmas–Pula–Toro moves would double back along Sardinia's south coast), loops to Palermo and back with two control points north of Capo San Vito (without them the curve crossed the cape), and is estimated on the beat west, when no positions were recorded. Across the Atlantic and back it follows the daily marks of Faden's chart of 1807, "copied from his Lordship's original MS." and redrawn by Corbett, which agree with seven log fixes within about 0.3°; among the Azores, between Terceira and São Miguel, the 8 July log fix replaces the chart's mark, and a control point at 37°58′ N 26°05′ W keeps the line about 3½ nm off São Miguel's north coast. Dropped as zigzags: 24 June (a doubling back, 70 nm west of the line), 4 and 6 July (wobbles of 14–28 nm in the calms), and 2, 4, 5 and 6 August (tacks against the northerlies, up to 30 nm either side of the line between the diary's positions). The detour to Tetuan (22–23 July) and back to Tarifa (25 July) is left out. Medium.
- **Nelson, Portsmouth to Cádiz.** Nelson's diary from the manuscript (Hudson 1917, pp. 15–19), with the Victory's log extracts and his letters (Nicolas VII 35–53); the table is in `research/scene-02/h6-september.md`, section 3, as corrected in `research/scene-02/v-h6-september.md`. Twenty-two points run from Spithead, where the Ushant line ends, to the Victory's position at 8 p.m. on 28 September. Sixteen rest on the sources' own places: St Helens road; off Christchurch, Portland, Berry Head (four miles), Plymouth and Lagos Bay; the noon latitudes of 23, 24 and 28 September; and seven bearings: the Lizard north, Scilly NE 27 leagues, Cape Finisterre abreast at 17 leagues, the Rock of Lisbon SSE 10 and NNE 13–14 leagues, Cape St Vincent SE by S 6 leagues, and San Sebastián light E by N 5–6 leagues. Every bearing is read by compass with gufm1's 21–25° W, the out-of-sight Scilly reckoning included, since Nelson gave his own out-of-sight reckoning of 19 October by compass (Hudson pp. 22–23); read as true, the Scilly point would lie 35 nm WNW. "Abreast" of Finisterre is read as the cape due east, on the beam of a ship running south. The noon latitude of the 23rd may be 42°02½′ rather than 42°25′. Longitudes are estimated except where a bearing gives them, and so are the offings where a place is named without a distance. Five estimated points take the line out of St Helens road on the log's SSE by compass and round the Isle of Wight, Start Point, the Eddystone and Cape St Vincent; the Spithead point carries the hour of Nelson's arrival at Portsmouth, as the ship already lay at St Helens. Between the Decade and Finisterre (20–23 September) no position exists, and the line runs straight. Checked like the others: no point, segment or drawn curve touches land, and all keep 2 nm or more off the coast except at Spithead and St Helens road. Closest are the Isle of Wight off Shanklin at 2.3 nm, the Eddystone 2.8, the coast by Dartmouth 2.9 (curve), Bolt Head 3.0, Start Point 3.4, Sagres 3.7, Cape St Vincent 6.1 and Cabo da Roca 12.2. The line ends where the Victory lay at 8 p.m., south-west by west of Cádiz and in sight of it, not at the fleet's later station. High for the dates, medium for the line, low for the offings.
- **The army's march.** Drawn by dates as the fleets' tracks are, but in the army's style: a dashed line in the land's dark ink, lying on the relief, from Boulogne on the morning of 28 August to Ulm at 2 p.m. on 20 October. It is the composite of "The dashed line", labelled as the army: the head of Soult's IV corps from Boulogne to Donauwörth, a joining stretch up the Danube's left bank to Höchstädt, then Ney's VI corps west along the left bank by Dillingen and over to Günzburg, and by Elchingen to Ulm. The roads between the towns are smoothed by the same curve as the sea routes. The coast check above is for the sea routes; this line lies on land. It starts at the town of Boulogne, since where Soult's divisions were hutted in the summer of 1805 was not found. The line is being built in session 32 from the table below, 22 of the 40 stages that `research/scene-02/session-32/army-march.md` traces ("For the chart: a thinner set"), every stage left out lying within about 8 km of a straight segment between its neighbours; the note gives the optional points (Germersheim, Öhringen, Stenay, Höchstädt). At the merge this paragraph and the table give the waypoints and lettered dates as built. Positions are present-day town centres (OpenStreetMap Nominatim), not the bridges or gates of 1805; the note gives them in decimal degrees for the route data. "Derived" dates come from the nightly stages of the 40th of the line, in Suchet's 4th division of the same corps on the same road (Berjaud 2009), shifted five days earlier, since the 1st division left Boulogne on 28 August and the 40th on 2 September. Shifted, they fall exactly on the 1st division's two dates in period documents: at Mézières from 9 to 11 September by the war ministry's digest of march reports, and leaving Metz on the 18th by the order of movement; and they agree with every period date found for the 3rd and 4th divisions (Alombert and Colin I 415–416, 421; II 72–75); a halt may still fall a day differently (see "Open doubts"). Lettered beside the line: "28 August" at Boulogne, "26–30 September" at the Rhine by Speyer, the span of Soult's corps' crossing (the head does not wait there: it reaches the river on the 26th and goes on, Bruchsal on the 27th, Heilbronn on the 29th; 25–30 September is the whole army's span), "7 October" at Donauwörth and "20 October" at Ulm; the research offers a fifth, "18 September" at Metz, to show the pace across France. Any hour the line leaves Boulogne at is a convention: the sources say only "morning". High for the road and for the dates from the Rhine on; medium-high for Metz to the Rhine (the order's itinerary, which the 4th division kept to the day); medium for the dates between Boulogne and Metz.

### Assumptions and licences

1. **The British heading at the handoff.** The Victory's log has the fleet steering N by E by compass, about 350° true, at first light and at sunrise; it bore up after signal No. 76 at 6.50 by her clock to ENE, about 047° true, and steered E by N (058°) from 8 a.m. The chart draws the British on 047°, the course some 20–30 minutes after scene 1's sunrise, for continuity with scene 1's Victory sailing east (scene 1 keeps a stylised west-to-east view): a deliberate licence, the owner's decision of 1 November 2026. The scene's last frame looks east-north-east into the sunrise from west of the British, as scene 1 began (the owner's decision of 5 November 2026): the British in their two bodies in the foreground, bows to the left as they sail away toward the enemy on 047°, and the combined fleet's line strung across the water beyond them under the coast.
2. **The dawn layout.** Both fleets stand at the sources' spacing, as "Dawn, 21 October" under "Positions on the chart" gives it (the owner's decision, 1 November 2026), eased apart ship by ship so that every ship shows in the last frame (item 16): the combined fleet's ragged line north to south, about 5 nm long by the sources and drawn longer, its northern end 7–9 nm off the coast by the British reckoning; the British in two loose groups, single ships a few miles from the Victory, the Africa apart to the north. The distance between the fleets is the sources'. From the view of the whole Gulf each fleet is lettered, its ships coming in only where every one of them reads one by one (item 3); the scene's last frame comes close enough for the whole formation to read (item 1). Each ship's place within her fleet is an estimate (item 10).
3. **Glyphs and counts.** Ships are glyphs drawn far larger than true scale, as a chart convention, and each is kept to the open water beyond it, so that none stands on the chart's shore (`decisions.md`, "No glyph stands on the shore"). The anchorage of 10–19 October and the dawn of 21 October each have their own beat and count (see "Fleet sizes at each stage"). On the far views (the Atlantic, Europe, the whole of Spain) no ship is drawn: a squadron is its track, drawn by date, with a point at its head and its commander and strength lettered there, in the manner of a period chart ("Villeneuve, 20 sail"); the October fleets off Cádiz are lettered among their ships. As the camera comes down, a squadron's or a fleet's ships of the line come in together, and only where every one of them reads one by one, so a fleet shows either as its label or as all its ships, never as a lone flagship and never as neither (the owner's rule of 12 November 2026; `decisions.md`, "the far views told by the tracks" and "The strengths by date, and the ships at a head always as many"). The close views with ships are the Gulf of Cádiz, the anchorage, the meeting off Finisterre, the dawn off Trafalgar and Boulogne's roads, where the flotilla comes in behind a gate of its own (`src/scenes/02-road/director.ts`, `FLOTILLA_GATE`). At a track's head a squadron is a block of its ships of the line only, the ships its label counts, laid out on screen rather than as they sailed; no frigate sails in a block and no prize (see "Fleet sizes at each stage"). The small craft are drawn at the one scale of every glyph type, hull for hull: the brigs about 29.5 m on the waterline, the Pickle about 22 m and the Entreprenante about 21 m, near their recorded lengths (the Argus 29.2 m; her sister the Observateur 27.6 m by the British measure of 1806; the Furet 29–31 m; the Weazle, drawn a little larger, 30.5 m; the Pickle 73 ft, 22.25 m; the Entreprenante 67 ft, 20.4 m; Winfield 2008 and Winfield and Roberts 2015, as threedecks.org and Wikipedia report them). Their spars are estimates in proportion, so a brig stands about half as tall as a seventy-four. Their rigs follow their logs and Steel's account of each rig: the Pickle a topsail schooner, square sails on the foremast only, the main on a long boom (NRS II 317–319); the Entreprenante one tall mast with a topmast and a square topsail over a great boom mainsail, her bowsprit nearly level (NRS II 320–321; Steel 1794, I, the description of sails and "Mastmaking", p. 2); the brigs square-rigged on two masts with a boom mainsail (Steel 1794, I, the description of sails).
4. **Squadrons over small islands.** At ocean scale a squadron's block of glyphs may partly cover small islands: it is a map symbol, as a printed chart's fleet symbol is (the owner's decision, 6 November 2026). The exception is a beat about the island itself (Martinique, Barbados and the like): there the island and its name stay readable.
5. **The narrows.** Natural Earth has no Matagorda shore, so the chart shows open water east of the isthmus of Cádiz and no narrows. The Fougueux, the one ship any source places in the narrows, stands where today's channel is (see "Outer and inner ships"). She lies there until she weighs with the fleet on the morning of the 20th; her warp of the evening of the 19th, from 5.30 p.m., to a berth off the city (annexes 213, 216) is not drawn.
6. **Boulogne's flotilla: twenty craft, each a division.** The chart draws twenty craft: seventeen at anchor in two lines parallel to the coast off Boulogne, Wimereux and Ambleteuse, the light luggers (bateaux and péniches) 2.6 km off the coast and the heavy craft (chaloupes, with prames at the ends and in the middle, and two bateaux) 5.6 km off, and three under sail outside them (see "Positions on the chart", "Boulogne"). Each stands for a division of eighteen craft (see "The flotilla"). A chart's convention, kept on purpose: the true lines held far more craft far closer together, and at beat 1's approved view of Boulogne none of their craft could read one by one, so under the owner's rule that ships come in only where each reads one by one they would never show (`decisions.md`, "The flotilla: a chart's convention, each craft a division", and the owner's decision of 13 November 2026, "The convention kept, and the hold over Boulogne longer"). It shows that the flotilla lay in the roads and of what kinds, not its numbers (twenty divisions would be some 360 craft, more than any line documented) nor its layout (the heavy craft seaward follow Saint-Haouen's proposal of 3 August 1805, not a documented line). On the chart's coast the harbour is land, so no craft lies in port. The true picture is in "Boulogne from the sea", "The flotilla in the roads and in the harbour" ("The moored line", "August 1805", "Where the line lay", "Spacing").
7. **The night tracks.** No source joins the night positions of 20–21 October to the dawn positions to better than 5–15 nm, so the night tracks are drawn loosely and end on the dawn positions. The combined fleet's glyphs face its signalled course, about 193° true, at 11 a.m. and at nightfall on the 20th and at midnight; between those moments and on to dawn they turn onto the drawn segment, about 189° true in the afternoon, 145° in the evening (not on screen: the camera is away at Ulm; see "What the chart draws on the 20th and in the night") and 150° after midnight (the drawn centroid of all 33; 147° to the main body's centre), where the French reports have the fleet standing S to SSW (see "Which half of the code"; `src/scenes/02-road/movements.ts`).
8. **The fleets' order, 19–21 October.** No source places single ships within either fleet's body between the anchorage and the dawn. The scene keeps each ship in her place in her fleet's dawn order and draws that order closer while the fleets sailed in closer order: each ship stands at a share of her dawn offset from her fleet's centre (for the British, the Victory). The British keep their full dawn order about the Victory at the station until the chase on the morning of the 19th, but for the Defence, Agamemnon, Colossus and Mars, which stand at their chain places of 19 October from 28 September until then: before the chain was set (4 October) and before those stations (14–15 October), a licence the British fleet's gate hides today (its ships come in only after the 17th) and that showing the chain apart will bring on screen; and the frigates, the Pickle and the Weazle, which have places of their own throughout (the tables under "How the chart shows the signals"); the Entreprenante keeps her dawn place by the Victory. They draw in to about 0.4 of it as they chase into the mouth of the Strait (1 a.m. to daybreak on the 20th) and open out again through the day and the night (0.6 at noon on the 20th, 0.85 at midnight, 0.95 at the 4 a.m. wear); the combined fleet forms off the port at 0.45 of it at 11 a.m. on the 20th and opens to 0.75 at nightfall and 0.9 at midnight. Both reach the full order at dawn. At the full order the northern ships, the Africa above all (13 nm north of the Victory at dawn), would cross the coast at Conil and Barbate as the British chased into the Strait, and the combined fleet forming off the port would stand on the shore at Rota; drawn closer, no ship under way comes within 1.5 km of the chart's coast from 28 September to dawn (`src/scenes/02-road/movements.ts`; `decisions.md`, "Kept off the land"). The combined fleet keeps the shape of its dawn line throughout, where the sources have it form in three columns (or five) in the afternoon of the 20th and in line of battle on the starboard tack from about 8 p.m. (see "At sea").
9. **The sortie's hours.** The record of the two mornings is under "19 October" and "20 October"; the scene draws each ship's weighing from it, more simply than it went. The scene draws the Rhin weighing at 6.15 a.m. and passing the harbour mouth, towed, at about 10.20; the seven of the line weighing from 7 a.m., about an hour apart, in no order the sources give (the Algésiras and the Héros were among the first under way), each passing the mouth about 1¾ hours after she weighs, the last weighing at about 12.45 p.m., where the Montañés has all seven under sail by noon (annexes 373); the Hermione weighing at 8.15 and passing the mouth at about 9.55; and the Thémis, the Cornélie and the Hortense weighing between 4.30 and 4.50 p.m. and passing it at about 6.10–6.30. It does not draw the many that weighed in the morning and anchored again, nor the topsails hoisted at anchor: every ship at anchor is drawn with her sails furled, where at daylight eight had their topsails at the mastheads, and by 8 a.m. all but two of those not under way (NRS II 145–146). So the morning drawn is far quieter than the record: at 8 a.m., when the Euryalus counted 19 of the enemy under way, the chart has three weighing (the Rhin, the Neptune and, just begun, the Duguay-Trouin). The seven and the frigates then stand off the port under sail until the forenoon of the 20th. After midnight the Bahama and Achille anchored near Rota (see "The night of 19–20 October"), which the scene does not draw (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-30c, H2-30g). On 20 October the scene follows the signal at first light, 6 a.m. (the Montañés and the Observateur; Desbrière has 6.30). The rest of the line weighs between 6 and about 6.50 and is out of the bay by about 8.30, the Fougueux from her place in the narrows (item 5) and the Furet with them; the Rayo, last, weighs at about 8.20 and passes the harbour mouth at about 10, and the Argus weighs at 8.30, behind her; the fleet forms outside, about 36°34′ N 6°25′ W, by 11. On the 20th the drawing departs little from the record (see "20 October"; `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-31a–b; `src/scenes/02-road/movements.ts`; `decisions.md`, "Smooth from near to far").
10. **Estimated points.** Positions no source fixes are estimates inside the sources' range: on the Toulon–Cádiz line, the Toulon berth, the Atlas's longitudes for 4–8 April, the points marked "est." (south of Cabo de Palos and of Cabo de Gata, through the Strait, the 4 p.m. point off Conil and the offing of Cádiz) and the anchorage of 9 April; the passages between the places named in the West Indies; the tracks between the named places on Villeneuve's way home and round Finisterre into Vigo and A Coruña; the line from Ferrol to Cádiz but for the Iris's fix of 14 August, with the bend of 15 August; Nelson's beat west in April and his passage of 15–28 September between the places his letters and diary name; the control points that keep curves off the coast (north of Capo San Vito, and at 37°58′ N 26°05′ W off São Miguel); the Finisterre smoke (planned); the stations of Orde, Calder and Collingwood and the meeting off Finisterre (item 18); the moorings of 10–19 October, the frigates' and brigs' among them (the Cornélie and Hortense inside the lines, where no source puts a frigate; the Furet and the Observateur by the commander-in-chief's flagship and the Argus by Gravina's, though the Observateur's station of 6 October was in Dumanoir's squadron, abreast the Rayo); the tracks of the Pickle to Cape Spartel and back and of the Weazle toward the Strait; Nelson's anchorages at Tetuan and in Gibraltar Bay; the chain's single ships on 19 October; the British fleet's daybreak position on 20 October ("between Trafalgar and Cape Spartel"); every position of the night of 20–21 October; each ship's place within the two fleets at dawn on 21 October; and at Boulogne each camp's place on its heights (the flotilla's lines are a convention, item 6).
11. **Signals by the book.** None of the signals is drawn yet (see "How the chart shows the signals"); this item and the next say what the plan takes from where. Some of what the chart is to show comes from the rule books, not from the logs of 19–21 October. By day each hoist stays flying until the Victory's answer runs back (Popham 1803, p. 6, and the *Signal-Book* of 1796; the day book of 1799 was not read). By night, from the 1799 *Night Signals*: No. 31 made as three guns and two lights, or near an enemy a square of four lanterns; two lights at each ship's ensign staff and one at her bowsprit end while the fleet wears; one light in answer to a signal. No log records hoists held down the chain on the 19th, though on the 20th the Agamemnon, ordered to repeat signals between the Euryalus and the Admiral, logged that she kept the signal up (NRS II 148, 300); no log of the night records the wear lights. The counted pairs or threes of gun puffs at each link on the 19th follow the counted guns the Euryalus logged with her signals on the 20th (NRS II 165, 167); along the chain on the 19th the records have only guns fired by the Phoebe and by a ship seen from the fleet (NRS II 146, 312, 322). The night's leading motif, two blue lights together every hour, is the half of Nelson's code for an enemy standing south, as the French reports have the combined fleet standing for most of the night; James has it standing north-west after 7.30 p.m. (see "Which half of the code").
12. **The Mars's distant signal.** At the relay's last link the Mars is to show first coloured flags the fleet cannot read, then three dark shapes at three mastheads (a flag, a ball and a pendant), on the word of one eyewitness writing years later (Cumby 1899, p. 719); no printed description of the Distant Signals of 1805 was found.
13. **The army's march.** The dashed line from Boulogne to Ulm is a composite, labelled as the army: no single corps marched the whole of it. Boulogne to Donauwörth was the road of Soult's IV corps, from the Boulogne camps; Soult then turned south. Ney's VI corps came down from Giengen to the Danube at Höchstädt on 7–8 October, spread west along the left bank by Dillingen, Lauingen and Gundelfingen, took the bridge at Günzburg on the 9th, fought at Elchingen and closed on Ulm. Only from Donauwörth to Höchstädt does the line join the two corps' roads, where no corps marched. The head's dates between Boulogne and Metz are derived from another division's stages on the same road (see "The dashed line" and "Routes on the chart", "The army's march"; `research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R6; `research/scene-02/session-32/army-march.md`).
14. **Routes simplified.** A route keeps the positions that give its shape and drops those that would make it zigzag or double back, or that do not fit: on the crossing 8 and 11 May; on Villeneuve's way home both 19 June positions of the Curieux, James's 30 June point, the doubtful noon of 23 July and the 27 July letter's 40 leagues WSW of Finisterre; on Nelson's line 24 June, 4 and 6 July and 2, 4, 5 and 6 August; and Nelson's line in the Mediterranean starts at Pula. Short moves are left out: Nelson's detour to Tetuan and back to Tarifa (22–25 July), Villeneuve's stand towards Cape Prior after leaving Ferrol, the chase of Collingwood on 20 August, and the Victory's beating down the Channel against westerly winds on 16–18 September. The crossing has no day marks after the noon of 10 April, and Villeneuve's homeward daily marks stop on 24 June (see "Routes on the chart"). Nelson's September line starts at Spithead, where his August line ends, at the hour of his arrival at Portsmouth on the 14th, though the Victory already lay at St Helens.
15. **The fleets off Cádiz, August to October.** From 22 August to the end of September the scene draws neither fleet off Cádiz, but for Nelson's own three arriving on 28 September. Beat 4 then runs from his arrival to 18 October in little more than a viewport height of scroll, the story's clock slowed where the British strength changes a day apart so that each figure reads (`src/scenes/02-road/beats.ts`). From the start of October both fleets are in the story, each lettered with its strength by date; they come in over about 1 to 10 October and stand at half strength only from about 6 October, so the first figures (the British 29 and 23, the combined fleet's 32) do not read (see "Fleet sizes at each stage", "The British fleet off Cádiz, by date"). The British fleet's ships of the line, at the station and in the chain, are the 27 that fought at Trafalgar, each with the fleet from the day she was (the Defiance, Royal Sovereign, Belleisle, Agamemnon and Africa joining between 7 and 14 October, the Leviathan away at Gibraltar from 2 to 8 October); Louis's five, the Prince of Wales and the Donegal are counted in the label while they were with the fleet and are never drawn. They need not be: the fleet's ships come in only where every one of them reads one by one, and off Cádiz they do not until after the Donegal has gone on the 17th, so the ships drawn are always the figure lettered (`src/scenes/02-road/fleets.ts`, `NOT_AT_TRAFALGAR`; `scripts/dev/fleet-gates.mjs`). The four frigates of 21 October lie off the port from the same moment, where Blackwood's squadron gathered from 29 September (see "Blackwood's squadron"), with the Weazle and the Pickle, which joined it on 8–9 October; the Entreprenante, which joined the fleet on 4 October, keeps by the Victory at the station. These three small craft are drawn from the start of October, some days before they came. The combined fleet lies at its anchorage of 10–19 October from the same moment, with its five frigates and three brigs, where the sources have it further in until 9–10 October (see "Into the roadstead"; `src/scenes/02-road/movements.ts`, `squadrons.ts`, `director.ts`).
16. **The fleets eased apart.** At the sources' spacing the glyphs, kept clear of each other, hid most of the ships in the two views that are about the fleets. By the owner's licence of 10 November 2026, narrowed ship by ship at his request on 11 November, each ship at dawn is moved from her estimated place by a set distance east and north (`DAWN_EASE` in `src/scenes/02-road/fleets.ts`), the least that lets every ship in the last frame stand clear of the others at a readable size on the owner's screen (1280×720), where it is tightest. The allied main body's line is drawn about 1.4 times its estimated length, about 7.4 nm instead of 5.2, so that its 21 ships stand side by side on screen; its middle stays nearly in place and its ends move out along it, the south end a little more than the north, as Gravina's flagship, drawn large at the line's head, stands in front of it. Fourteen ships move more than 1 km, all at the two ends of that line: at its north end, northward, the Formidable (1.91 km), Neptune (1.87), Indomptable (1.50) and Redoutable (1.05); at its south end, southward, the San Leandro (2.02 km, 1.09 nm, the largest shift), San Justo (1.97), San Francisco de Asís (1.82), Monarca (1.74), San Agustín (1.70), Neptuno (1.59), Rayo (1.55), Santa Ana (1.20), Duguay-Trouin (1.10, south-south-west) and Héros (1.09). The others move less: Gravina's squadron 0.88 km at most (the Príncipe de Asturias), the frigates and small craft 0.82 km (the Hortense), the British 68 m (the Conqueror); the mean over all 73 ships is 0.43 km, over the ground. The Victory stays where she was, as scene 1's hand-off stands on her place, and the British centre with her; the allied line's centre moves about 0.3 km south along the line, so the distance between the fleets is the sources'. At anchor the three lines and the frigates are drawn 1.2 times as far from the anchorage's middle (36°33.8′ N 6°16.4′ W), 0.26 km at most (the Thémis) and 0.15 km on average; the brigs keep by their flagships, and the Fougueux in the narrows stays where she was (`decisions.md`, "the fleets eased apart" and "Narrowed, ship by ship").
17. **The last frame holds still.** The camera settles on the last frame a little before the scene's end and holds it. As it settles, the clock comes to rest at dawn on 21 October, and every ship stands at her dawn place through the hold: a still tableau for scene 3 to take up. In the sources both fleets were under way at that hour; the scene stops them at first light (`src/scenes/02-road/beats.ts`; `decisions.md`, "Narrowed, ship by ship").
18. **The meeting off Finisterre.** No source gives either fleet's layout ship by ship. The chart draws Villeneuve's and Calder's blocks as two lines on 247° true, about the course on which they fought (WSW), Villeneuve's above and Calder's below with a lane between them. They are formed from about 4 a.m. on 22 July, hours before the fog lifted about noon and before the lines formed in the afternoon, stand until 6 p.m. on the 23rd, while the fleets lay in sight of each other and neither renewed the action, and are apart by noon on the 24th (`src/scenes/02-road/director.ts`, `MEETINGS`; `decisions.md`, session 20). Without tracks, Orde, Calder and Collingwood stand at estimated stations (see "Positions on the chart", "Stations without tracks").

### The names on the chart

The brief asks for the names used in 1805. The chart sets the English forms (the site is in English), settled in `research/scene-02/a-h4.md` with the corrections of `research/scene-02/a-v-a-h4.md`. They rest on the charts read for the names, among them Faden's chart of Nelson's track (12 August 1807), which the scene imitates, Heather's *Coast of Portugal* (1800), Laurie & Whittle's *Atlantic Ocean* (1794) and their *Spain and Portugal* (plate of 12 May 1794, issued in their atlas of 1804), Cary's maps of 1801–1805 and Arrowsmith's of 1802–1803; and on usage in Nelson's letters, the logs, the *Naval Chronicle*, Norie, Malham and Brookes. The names the beats add were settled under the same rule in `research/scene-02/h5-names.md`; its check, `research/scene-02/session-14/v-h5-names.md`, is the final word on them. Besides the charts above, they rest on Knight's *Chart of the British Channel* (12 January 1806), Laurie & Whittle's *British Channel* after Jefferys (1794), the Ordnance Survey's *Kent* (1801), Stockdale's *England and Wales* (a copy of 1809), Cary's maps of Hampshire and of the Circle of Austria (1801), the Netherlands (1804), France and Cornwall (1806) and England (1807), with his *Swabia* and *Germany* of 1799, Wilkinson's *Swabia* and *Rhine* (1802) and *Bavaria* (1806), Laurie & Whittle's post roads of Germany (1802), Arrowsmith's *Alpine Country* (1804) and the plate titles of Serres's *Little Sea Torch* (1801); and on the English accounts of the 1805 campaign: the two *Annual Registers* for 1805, the *New Annual Register* and the magazines of late 1805.

- **The rule.** Where the English charts of 1800–1807 agree, the chart letters their form even if it looks odd today (Barbadoes, Bourdeaux, Guadaloupe). Where they disagree, it drops any form a visitor would misread (Martinico, Guadalquiver, Cape Verd). The naval and printed usage of 1805 then decides among the rest (Atlantic Ocean, Canary Islands, Carthagena, Barbary), and Faden's chart of 1807 is the last tie-break. A region no chart of the period letters in its body takes the name of the period's prose and chart titles (West Indies; `research/scene-02/a-v-a-h4.md`, 2). High for the forms, medium for the choices between equal forms.
- **Type.** Ordinary characters only: the engravers' raised letters become the printed abbreviation with a stop (Sᵗ as "St.", Pᵗ as "Pt."), as printed English of the period had it ("Cape St. Vincent" in Malham, Norie and the *Naval Chronicle*). No accents or umlauts (Cadiz, Nordlingen), as on every English chart of the period. Only oceans and seas are in spaced capitals (the brief); period plates also set countries, capitals and island groups in capitals. Double names take a small "or", as the plates set it. High.
- **Capes and the article.** Capes take "C." with a stop, the usual small-scale form (Faden 1807 for every European cape); the period also spelled "Cape" on large-scale plates (Heather 1800 CAPE Sᵀ. VINCENT) and for some capes on small-scale maps (Faden's Cape Geer, Cape Nun and Cape Bojador; Arrowsmith 1802; Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal*). Points are set as the charts name them: Start Pt. (Cary 1804's Start P.; Faden's bare Start would read as an instruction), the bare Lizard of Faden and of the sailors (beat 3 below), and Europa Point and Rock of Lisbon would be spelled out as the period spelled them (neither is lettered on the chart now). Leaving out "The" ("Atlantic Ocean", "English Channel") is a choice: Faden letters the ocean and the Channel without it, as Cary 1803 and Arrowsmith 1803 letter the ocean, but Faden has THE NORTH SEA, Heather 1800 THE ATLANTIC OCEAN and THE MEDITERRANEAN SEA, Cary 1804 THE ATLANTIC OCEAN and Laurie & Whittle 1794 THE CHANNEL. The Downs keeps its article (beat 1 below). High.
- **Four choices a visitor would notice.** The chart letters English Channel (Faden 1807; the chart titles in Heather's catalogue of 5 November 1806; Norie, Malham), not The Channel; C. St. Vincent, with the stop after "St." that the period printed; Azores or Western Islands (Laurie & Whittle 1794; Heather's catalogue), so that a visitor finds the Azores, not Western Islands alone; and Martinique, not Martinico. West Indies, a region rather than a water, is set in roman like a land and fades out close up. High; medium for Martinique.

Where the period's own sources disagree (each form with who used it):

- **Martinique or Martinico.** Martinique: Faden 1807, Cary's West India map (1803), the *Naval Chronicle* of 1805 and Norie's tables of 1805. Martinico: Nelson's own letters, Arrowsmith's West Indies chart (1803), Laurie & Whittle (1794), Malham (1795) and Brookes (1807). The chart follows Faden and the captions.
- **The Channel.** English Channel: Faden 1807, the chart titles in Heather's catalogue of 1806, Norie 1805 and Malham. British Channel: Cary's *Europe* (1804). The Channel: Laurie & Whittle's Atlantic (1794), and Nelson and his officers in their letters.
- **The Azores.** Azores or Western Islands: Laurie & Whittle 1794 and a chart title in Heather's catalogue of 1806. Azores or Western Isles: Faden 1807. Western Islands alone: Arrowsmith 1802, Cary 1805, Nelson and Norie.
- **The ocean.** ATLANTIC OCEAN: Cary 1803 and Arrowsmith 1803. THE ATLANTIC OCEAN: Heather 1800 and Cary 1804. "Atlantic Ocean" was also the usual name in print (Malham 237 to 7, Brookes 1807, Norie). ATLANTIC or WESTERN OCEAN: the ocean charts of Laurie & Whittle (1794) and Faden (1807), Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal* (1794), and two titles in Heather's catalogue of 1806 (nos. 67 and 69); two others there say "Part of the Atlantic Ocean" (no. 41) and "The Atlantic and Southern Oceans" (no. 68). WESTERN OR ATLANTIC OCEAN: Cary's *Spain* (1801). The *Naval Chronicle* of 1805 lists four Spanish prizes taken "on the Western Ocean", all in one list.
- **Gulf of Cadiz.** Of the English charts and maps read, two letter it. Laurie & Whittle's *New Map of the Kingdoms of Spain and Portugal* has the imprint 12 May 1794; it is attributed to Thomas Kitchin and was issued in their *New Universal Atlas* of 1804. It sets GULF OF CADIZ in italic capitals across the open water from off Cape St Mary to off Cádiz. Heather's *New Chart of the Coast of Portugal* (3 April 1800) sets it in large open roman capitals from off the mouth of the Guadiana towards Cádiz. Faden (1807), Cary (1801, 1804) and Heather's Strait plate (about 1809) leave the water unnamed. The gazetteers of 1795–1810 (Brookes, Walker, Cruttwell, the *Britannica* of 1810) use the name for the same bight, and Brookes (1807) puts Alcoutim, Faro and Tavira on it. Nelson, the logs, the *Naval Chronicle* and Malham never use it: sailors said "off Cadiz". The spelling "Gulph of Cadiz" also occurs in books of 1798–1810 (`research/scene-02/v-h4.md`, from archive.org's full-text search). Tuckey (1815) says the name was more usually kept for the Bay of Cádiz. The chart sets it on the open bight, as both period maps that name the water do. Medium for the extent.
- **Canary Islands or Isles.** Islands: Arrowsmith 1802, Laurie & Whittle 1794, Heather's chart titles, Nelson, Malham and Brookes. Isles: Faden 1807 and Cary 1805.
- **West Indies.** The chart letters West Indies across the Caribbean as a regional label. It was the universal name in 1805: Nelson's letters (84 times in Nicolas's vols. 6–7, notes included), Malham (312) and the *Naval Chronicle* of 1805 (41). It names charts and is used on them: Arrowsmith's *Chart of the West Indies* (1803); Heather's catalogue of 1806, with a section for "the West Indies" and no. 60, a chart of "the whole of the West-Indies"; Cary's *West India Isles* (1803); and the label on Faden's outward track (1807), "Track of H.M.S. Victory to the West Indies 1805". No English chart of 1794–1807 that was read letters it in the map body: the arc of islands is CARIBBEE ISLANDS on Faden (1807), Cary (1803) and Laurie & Whittle (1794), and NORTH and SOUTH CARIBBEE ISLANDS on Arrowsmith (1803); the sea where the chart's label lies is CARIBBEAN SEA on Faden, Cary and Laurie & Whittle, and on Arrowsmith per `research/scene-02/a-h4.md`. High for the form; medium as a label in the map body.
- **Barbary.** The chart letters Barbary. Laurie & Whittle (1794, in large spaced capitals across North Africa) and Heather (1800, and his Strait plate of about 1809) letter it too. Nelson (17 times in vols. 6–7) and Malham (58) write it. Heather also titles his harbour plates of 1809 "in Barbary" (`research/scene-02/v-h4.md`). Faden (1807) and Cary (1804, 1805) letter the states instead: Faden FEZ, MOROCCO and ALGIER, Cary FEZ, MOROCCO, ALGIERS, TUNIS and TRIPOLI (`research/scene-02/v-h4.md`).
- **Barbadoes.** Faden 1807, Cary 1803, Arrowsmith 1803, Nelson and the *Naval Chronicle*. Barbados: Laurie & Whittle 1794 and once in Malham.
- **Straits of Gibraltar.** "Straits" on the charts (Heather 1800; Cary 1801; Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal*; Heather's catalogue no. 44) and in naval writing; STR. OF GIBRALTAR on Cary 1804; Faden 1807 leaves the Strait unnamed. "Streights" survived in official documents of 1800–1807 (`research/scene-02/v-h4.md`), and Faden's chart title of 1806 has the singular "Strait" (`research/scene-02/h4-names.md`).
- **Tangier.** The chart letters Tangier, as Faden 1807, Heather 1800 and his Strait plate of about 1809, Cary 1801 and Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal* (1794) engrave it; Nelson (9 times, Tangiers once), Malham (17 to 0) and Brookes (3 to 0) write it. Tangiers: Cary's *Europe* (1804). Tanger: Laurie & Whittle's *Atlantic* (1794).

The beats add the names below, each lettered as given (`src/scenes/02-road/gazetteer.ts`); this section's prose keeps today's names (A Coruña, Speyer, Donauwörth). Every form `research/scene-02/a-h4.md` had settled for these places stands, among them Straits of Dover, Carthagena, Tetuan, Guadaloupe, CARIBBEAN SEA, Corunna, Scilly Isles, Lizard, Start Pt., Strasburg and Munich. The check of the names corrects none of them, though Lizard now stands on other grounds. It changes two of the forms `research/scene-02/h5-names.md` proposed (St. Helens Road to St. Helens, and Scilly Islands back to Scilly Isles) and the grounds of six (C. Grisnez, The Downs, Spire, Heilbronn, Nordlingen, Donawerth); each entry says so.

Beat 1, Boulogne and the Strait of Dover (Dover, Calais, Straits of Dover, C. Grisnez, Ambleteuse, Vimereux, Etaples, The Downs, Deal):

- **The agreed forms.** Dover, Calais, Ambleteuse, Etaples and Deal are the forms of the English charts of 1794–1809 that letter them, Knight's of 1806 among them. Two plates vary: Stockdale (1809) has Ambletuse, and Cary's *England* (1807) Etables, an engraver's slip, not copied. The usage of 1805 agrees, but for the older spelling Estaples in the *Naval Chronicle* of 1805 and in Malham (Norie, 1805, has Etaples). High.
- **Straits of Dover.** a-h4's form stands, and the plates engrave it in full, not only abbreviated: Laurie & Whittle's *British Channel* (1794), the Ordnance Survey's *Kent* (1801), Knight (1806), Cary's *France* (1806) and *England* (1807), and Stockdale (1809). Str. of Dover: Laurie & Whittle's *Atlantic* (1794) and Cary's *Europe* (1804). In print, "Straits of Dover" (Malham; the *Naval Chronicle*); "Streights of Dover" in none of the texts searched. High.
- **C. Grisnez.** The commonest printed form of 1804–05: "Cape Grisnez" in the *Naval Chronicle*, with Griznes and Griz-Nez once each in 1805. The plates disagree. C. Grisnez: Laurie & Whittle's *British Channel* (1794). Gris Nez, two words without "C.": Knight (1806), the one plate of 1800–1807 found that names the cape. Probably C. Griznez: Stockdale (1809). Cape Griznez: Malham (1795), the *Naval Chronicle* of 1806 and the *New Annual Register* for 1805. Cape Gris ness: Norie (1805). Faden (1807) leaves the cape unnamed. `h5-names.md` rested the form on the charts; the check rests it on the usage of 1804–05. Read strictly, counting only plates of 1800–1807, the rule would give C. Gris Nez, the form nearest today's Cap Gris-Nez; the plates either side of 1806 and the print of 1804–05 close the name up. Medium.
- **Vimereux.** The flotilla port north of Boulogne, made in 1803–04 (Wimereux today; see "The ports"). No English chart of 1794–1809 that was read letters it: Laurie & Whittle (1794), drawn before the port was made, and Stockdale (1809) name only Wimille inland, and Knight (1806) draws an unnamed fort there. Faden's plate of this coast with the new works of 1805 was not seen. So the usage of 1805 decides. Vimereux: the *Naval Chronicle* (nine times in 1805; once Vimereaux, in its index), and as a privateer's name in both *Annual Registers* and the *New Annual Register*. Wimereux: once in the *Naval Chronicle*, for the privateer *Le Wimereux*. A visitor should recognise Wimereux in it; Wimereux is the fallback if it were judged a misprint. Medium.
- **The Downs.** The anchorage between Deal and the Goodwin Sands keeps its article. THE DOWNS: the Ordnance Survey's *Kent* (1801). The DOWNS: Cary's *England* (1807). "the Downs": Laurie & Whittle (1794), and the naval writing of 1804–05 (Nelson, Norie, the *Naval Chronicle*). DOWNS, without the article: Knight (1806), as the *Naval Chronicle*'s station lists and datelines write it. So the charts disagree, against `h5-names.md`'s reason that every chart sets the article. The bare "Downs" is dropped, since a visitor would take it for the chalk downs of Kent behind Deal. High.
- **The close-up of Boulogne.** In beat 1's low close-up the chart letters Ambleteuse, Vimereux and Deal as minor names. They show while the camera comes down and they have room, and give way, fading smoothly, as they would crowd the names above them: Boulogne, C. Grisnez, Dover, Calais and The Downs (Ambleteuse also gives way to Vimereux). Deal's name stands inland, west of its mark. The Downs is lettered over the roadstead east of Deal, towards the Goodwins (the owner's decision, 11 November 2026; `decisions.md`, "The minor names give way, they are not left off"). High for the forms; the fading is a chart choice.

Beat 2, the chase (Carthagena, Sardinia, Palermo, Lagos, Tetuan, Salvages; in the West Indies Antigua, Barbuda, Montserrat, Guadaloupe, Dominica, St. Lucia, Trinidad, Tobago, Grenada, Fort Royal, CARIBBEAN SEA):

- **The agreed forms.** Sardinia, Palermo, Lagos, Antigua, Barbuda, Dominica, St. Lucia, Trinidad, Tobago and Grenada are the forms of Faden (1807), Cary (1801–1804) and Laurie & Whittle (1794) wherever they letter them, and of the usage of 1805. The engraved Sᵗ Lucia is set "St.", and the French St. Lucie occurs only in reports of French origin. Lagos Bay (Heather 1800; Nelson) is the anchorage off the town. High.
- **Carthagena.** Carthagena for the Spanish port: Cary 1804; Laurie & Whittle's *Atlantic* (1794) and their *Spain and Portugal* (1794; atlas of 1804); Heather's catalogue of 1806 (no. 39, "The Harbours of Gibraltar, Malaga, Carthagena…"); Nelson (39 times in vols. 6–7, Cartagena never); the *Naval Chronicle*; Malham (42 to 0); Brookes and the *Annual Registers* for 1805. Cartagena: Faden 1807, for the Spanish port; the same plate letters the port in New Granada "Carthagena". Cartagena occurs in none of the texts searched. An old English spelling, not a misprint; the usage of 1805 decides. High.
- **Tetuan.** Tetuan: Heather (1800) and Laurie & Whittle's *Atlantic* (1794), and the only spelling in print (Nelson, Malham, the *Naval Chronicle*, the *Annual Registers* for 1805, the logs). Teutan, twice: Cary's *Spain* (1801). Tetwen: Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal* (1794). The charts disagree; both odd forms read as slips and are dropped, and the usage gives Tetuan. Faden does not name the town. High.
- **Montserrat.** Montserrat: Faden (1807), the Victory's log, Malham, the *Naval Chronicle* of 1804 and the *New Annual Register*. Monserrat: Cary (1803), Arrowsmith (1803) and the *Naval Chronicle* of 1805. Nelson wrote both in June 1805, "under Monserrat" and "at Montserrat". The charts disagree, and Monserrat reads as a misprint today, so the chart follows Faden. High.
- **Guadaloupe.** Guadaloupe: Faden (1807), Cary (1803), Arrowsmith (1803), Nelson (8 times to 1), Malham and most of the *Naval Chronicle*'s uses in 1804–06. Guadeloupe: Laurie & Whittle (1794), about a fifth of the *Naval Chronicle*'s uses, and today. The charts of 1803–1807 agree, so the chart keeps the form, though it looks odd today. The name is set on the sea to the west, level with the isthmus where the island's two wings meet; their joint centre falls on the water of the bay between them. High.
- **Fort Royal.** Fort Royal: Cary (1803) and Arrowsmith (1803), by the town on Martinique's west coast; Nelson ("off Fort Royal"), Malham, the *Naval Chronicle* of 1804–06 and Norie. Fort-Royal: an *Annual Register* for 1805. Fort de France, the French name: only in a French document the *Naval Chronicle* printed in 1804. Faden does not name the town.
