Its mark stands in the old town by the cathedral, on the chart's land; the Savane, 0.3 nm south, falls just off the chart's generalised shore. High.
- **CARIBBEAN SEA.** a-h4's form: Faden (1807), Cary (1803), Laurie & Whittle (1794) and Arrowsmith (1803). Malham and Brookes use it; the naval writing of 1805 does not. The chart sets it at Faden's latitude, level with Martinique. High.
- **Salvages.** Salvages: Faden (1807), on Nelson's track south of Madeira, and Cary's *Africa* (1805); Nelson, Malham and Norie. No other spelling in the texts searched (their "Savage Islands" are other places). Selvagens today. High.

Beat 3, Finisterre, Ferrol, Nelson's return and his September passage (Corunna, Vigo, Ushant, Scilly Isles, Lizard, Start Pt., Plymouth, Isle of Wight, Spithead, St. Helens):

- **The agreed forms.** Vigo, Ushant, Plymouth and Spithead are the forms of every chart that letters them, among them Faden (1807), Laurie & Whittle (1794), Cary (1801–1807) and Knight (1806), and of the usage of 1805; Ouessant occurs only in Malham. Isle of Wight is spelled out from the engraved I. Wight (Faden) and Wight I. (Laurie & Whittle 1794), as Cary's *England* (1807) and Knight (1806) engrave it. High.
- **Corunna.** Corunna: Faden (1807), Cary's *Spain* (1801), the harbour plate "Ferrol and Corunna" of Serres's *Little Sea Torch* (1801), and the usage of 1805 (the *Naval Chronicle*, the *Annual Registers*, Malham, Norie). Corunna or the Groyn: Laurie & Whittle (1794). The Groyne, the older sailors' name, survives as a second name in Malham, Norie, Brookes and the *Annual Register* for 1806; it would puzzle a visitor. A Coruña today. High.
- **St. Helens.** The roadstead off St Helens and Bembridge, where Nelson joined the Victory on 14 September 1805. The chart letters St. Helens, as Knight's sea chart (1806) sets it on the water and as Nelson dated his letters that day ("Victory, at St. Helens"). St. Helens Road: Cary's *Hampshire* (1801) and *England* (1807, as Sᵗ Helens Rᵈ). St. Helen's Road: Malham (1795), the only "Road" in the print searched. St. Helen's: the *Naval Chronicle*. St. Hellens: Laurie & Whittle (1794), and Cary (1801) for the village, dropped as a misprint today. `h5-names.md` set St. Helens Road; the check sets St. Helens. High for the spelling; medium for leaving out "Road".
- **Scilly Isles.** a-h4's form stands: `h5-names.md` had proposed Scilly Islands, and the check refutes it. Two English plates of 1806 engrave the name in full, both as Isles: the inset of the islands on Knight's chart (January 1806) and on Cary's *Cornwall* (November 1806), whose engraved note begins "The Scilly Isles". Faden (1807) and Laurie & Whittle (1794) engrave Scilly Iˢ, which fits either. Scilly Islands: Malham (1795), Norie (1805) and the title of Laurie & Whittle's coastal view (1794); no chart of 1800–1807 that was read engraves it. Most naval writing says plain "Scilly". The charts of 1800–1807 agree, so the usage is not reached. Today the Isles of Scilly; Scilly Isles is still current English. High.
- **Lizard and Start.** Lizard: Faden (1807), Cary's *Europe* (1804), Knight (1806) and the plate titles of Serres (1801). LIZARD POINT: Cary's *Cornwall* (1806) and *England* (1807). Lizard Pᵗ: Laurie & Whittle (1794). So the charts disagree, and `research/scene-02/a-v-a-h4.md`'s ground for the bare form (Faden and Cary agree) does not hold. It wins instead on the sailors' "the Lizard" (Nelson, "off the Lizard", 18 September 1805; Malham; Norie; the *Naval Chronicle*), against a few uses of "Lizard Point" (Malham, Norie, Brookes), and on Faden. Start: Faden (1807), Knight (1806) and Serres's plate titles (1801). Start P.: Cary's *Europe* (1804). Start Point: Moore (1800), one of Serres's titles, and a few times in Malham, Norie, Brookes and the *Naval Chronicle*. "The Start": the sailors' usage (Malham, Norie, the *Naval Chronicle*). The bare Start, like the sailors' "the Start", would read as an instruction on a scrolling page, so the chart sets Cary's form as Start Pt., the engravers' Pᵗ (Knight engraves Prawle Pᵗ on the same coast). High.

Beat 5, the cut-away to Ulm, with the army's march (Metz, Strasburg, Spire, Heilbronn, Nordlingen, Donawerth, Ulm, Augsburg, Munich, Vienna, Rhine, Danube):

- **The agreed forms.** Ulm, Metz and Vienna are the forms of every English map of 1800–1807 that shows them and of all the print of 1805 ("Wien" in none). Faden's chart does not show these places, so his tie-break never applies here. Vienna takes the capital's ring. Munich takes a town's, though it was the capital of the Electorate of Bavaria (a kingdom from 1 January 1806) and four of the five plates engrave it in capitals, as they engrave capitals: a chart choice. High.
- **Strasburg.** The English maps of 1800–1807 agree on Strasburg: Wilkinson's *Swabia* (1802), Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802, with a long s), Cary's *Europe* (1804) and *France* (1806). The print of 1805 is split. Strasburg: the Rivington *Annual Register*, the *New Annual Register*, Brookes (1807) and Nelson. Strasburgh: the Otridge *Annual Register*, once each in the *New Annual Register* and Brookes, and the *Scots* and *Monthly* magazines of late 1805. Strasbourg: in none. The usage is not reached. High.
- **Spire.** Spire for Speyer, a French town in 1805, which Brookes (1807) enters as a city of the department of Mont Tonnerre. The maps of 1800–1807 disagree, three to one. Spire: Wilkinson's *Swabia* and *Rhine* (both 1802) and Cary's *Netherlands* (1804), as on Cary's *Swabia* and *Germany* (1799) and in Brookes. Speyer: Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802), drawn from an Austrian map and German throughout. Neither reads as a misprint, so the usage decides, and it is all of the Spire stem: Spire in Brookes, and Spires in every English account of the crossing of the Rhine (both *Annual Registers* for 1805, the *New Annual Register*, the *Scots* and *Monthly* magazines); Speyer in none of the print of 1795–1807 searched. Spires is on no chart found, so of the two chart forms Spire wins. `h5-names.md` rested the form on maps that agree; the check rests it on the usage. Beside a town's ring it reads as a place, not a church spire. High for the form; the ruling rests on the usage.
- **Heilbronn.** Heilbronn: Wilkinson's *Swabia* (February 1802), as on Cary's two maps of 1799. Heilbron: Wilkinson's *Rhine* (September 1802) and Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802). A letter short of today's name, Heilbron would read as a misprint, as Monserrat does, so it is dropped and Heilbronn is left. The print of 1805 nearly always wrote one n: Heilbron (Brookes 1807; the *Scots Magazine*), Hailbron (the Rivington *Annual Register*), Heilbrun (the Otridge *Annual Register*); Heilbronn only in the *Monthly Magazine* of 1 December 1805. `h5-names.md` rested the form on maps that agree; they do not, and if Heilbron were not taken for a misprint the usage would give it. Medium.
- **Nordlingen.** Nordlingen: Wilkinson's *Swabia* (1802) and *Bavaria* (1806), Cary's maps of 1799, and all the print of 1805. Nordingen: Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802), an engraver's slip, not copied. No umlaut, in print or on the chart. High.
- **Donawerth.** Donawerth for Donauwörth. Six English plates of 1801–1806 give four spellings. Donawerth: Arrowsmith (1804) and Cary's *Europe* (1804). Donauwert: Wilkinson's *Swabia* (1802) and Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802). Donawert: Cary's *Austria* (1801). Donauwerth: Wilkinson's *Bavaria* (1806). Cary's maps of 1799 have Donawerth and Donauwerth. None reads as a misprint, so the usage of 1805 decides, and the Dona- forms lead about 20 to 6. Donawerth: the Rivington *Annual Register*, the *New Annual Register*, the *Scots Magazine*, Cobbett's *Political Register* and the *Gentleman's Magazine*. Donawert: the Otridge *Annual Register* and Brookes. Donauwerth: mostly French bulletins as the magazines and Cobbett translated them. Donauwert: in none. `h5-names.md` rested the form on most of the maps; the plates of 1801, 1802 and 1806 undo that, and the usage carries it. If Donawerth were judged a misprint, the fallback is Donauwerth, the latest plate's form and the nearest today's. Medium.
- **Augsburg.** The English plates of 1800–1807 agree on Augsburg: Cary's *Austria* (1801), Wilkinson's *Swabia* (1802) and *Bavaria* (1806), Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802) and Arrowsmith (1804). The print of 1805 is split, with Augsburgh at least as often. Augsburgh: the Otridge *Annual Register*, Cobbett, the magazines of late 1805 and Brookes. Augsburg: the Rivington *Annual Register*, the *New Annual Register*, the *Annual Register* for 1806 and Brookes. Augsbourg, from the French: Cobbett. The usage is not reached. High.
- **Munich.** Munich: Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802), Cary's *Europe* (1804), Wilkinson's *Bavaria* (1806) and all the print of 1805. MUNCHEN: Arrowsmith (1804). MUNCHEN or Munich: Cary's *Austria* (1801), as on his *Germany* (1799). Munchen, without its umlaut, would read as a slip, so it is dropped with the double name. High.
- **Rhine and Danube.** Lettered without the plates' "R." ("Rhine R." on Wilkinson's plates of 1802 and Cary's *Netherlands* of 1804; "Danube R." on Cary 1801 and 1804, Laurie & Whittle 1802 and Wilkinson 1806). The Rhine's name lies between Strasburg and Spire, on the stretch the crossings of 25–30 September span (see "To the Rhine"), not at a single crossing. The Danube's lies east of Donawerth, on the reach toward Neuburg where the river runs nearly east; between Ulm and Donawerth by Höchstädt, where Cary's *Austria* (1801) sets it, it covered Donawerth's name (`src/scenes/02-road/gazetteer.ts`). High.

For later scenes, settled the same way:

- **Huelva.** Huelba: Faden 1807 and Heather 1800 (inset of Palos); the charts of 1800–1807 agree, so a later scene sets Huelba and records it, like Barbadoes. Guelva: Malham 1795. Huelva: today's spelling. Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal* (plate of 1794) has Huelva or Huelba; a river line cuts the letters.
- **Cape St Mary.** C. St. Mary: Nelson's "Cape St. Mary", the logs, the *Naval Chronicle* and Malham; Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal* (1794) engraves Cape Sᵗ Mary. C. S. Mary: Faden 1807 (the chart sets his S. as St.). C. St. Maria: Heather 1800. C. Sta. Maria: Cary 1801.
- **Gulf of Lyons.** Gulf of Lyons: Cary 1801 and 1804, Nelson, Malham and Brookes. Gulf of Lyon: Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal* (1794). Gulf of Lion: older charts (La Rochette 1780; Laurie & Whittle 1794, per `research/scene-02/h4-names.md`).
- **Cape Verde Islands.** Cape Verde Islands: Arrowsmith 1802. Cape Verd Isles: Faden 1807 and Cary 1805. Cape Verd Islands: Laurie & Whittle 1794 and Heather's catalogue of 1806 (no. 52). Cape Verd, for the cape: Norie (1805), Moore (1800) and Heather's catalogue (nos. 47–48).
- **El Puerto de Santa María.** St. Maria: Heather 1800 (Sᵗ Maria). Sta. Maria: Knight c. 1795. P. S. Mary, that is Port St. Mary: Laurie & Whittle's *Spain and Portugal* (1794). Puerto de Santa Maria: Nelson. A later scene would set St. Maria.
- **Other names.** Rochefort (Faden 1807, the *Naval Chronicle* of 1805 and Nicolas's vol. 7; Rochfort on Cary 1804, in Nicolas's vol. 6 and Malham); Algeziras (Heather 1800, Cary 1801, the *Naval Chronicle* of 1805, Malham; Algesiras is the usual spelling in Nelson's letters; Algeciras on Heather's Strait plate of about 1809 and once in the logs); Rock of Lisbon (Faden, Heather, Laurie & Whittle, Nelson, Malham; C. of Roca on Cary 1801; Cape Roxent in Malham and Tuckey); Douro (Cary 1801, Malham, Brookes, for the river at Oporto; R. Duero on Cary 1804, the Spanish name of the whole river); Guadalquivir (Cary 1801; the commoner English Guadalquiver on Faden, Heather, Knight c. 1795 and in Malham and Brookes reads today as a misprint). The Sahara stays unlettered, as on sea charts (Saara or Great Desert on Arrowsmith 1802, Great Desert of Zaara on Cary 1805, Zahara in Brookes 1807; Faden 1807 names the coast AZANAGA). Forms kept although they look odd today: Bourdeaux (every chart read; Bordeaux only in rare print) and Huelba, see above; Carthagena and Guadaloupe, on the chart now, are under beat 2. The full table is in `research/scene-02/a-h4.md`, with the corrections of `a-v-a-h4.md`.

### Open doubts

Each doubt with who holds each version; the sections above give the argument where it arises.

- **Toulon to Cádiz, 30 March to 10 April.** The sailing: 30 March (Reille, the Atlas's log, Villeneuve's letter of 9 germinal; Nelson) or 29 March (James III 339, his conversion slip; Clowes). The first sighting on 31 March: 8 a.m. (Nelson to Marsden and to Otway, Nicolas VI 396, 398), 10 a.m. (his note to Ball, VI 400) or the afternoon, Cape Sicié north 10–12 leagues (James III 339; Clowes); the second fix at 8 p.m. (to Marsden) or at sunset (to Otway and Ball). Cabo de Gata: passed in the night of 7–8 April (Desbrière's narrative; the Atlas's noon of the 8th) or off it on the 7th (a neutral's report to the Leviathan, James III 341; Nelson's order to Capel of 18 April, Nicolas VI 408). The Strait: 9 April (every British document written at or near the scene; the French log) or 8 April (the Ragusan's report, Nicolas VI 408; Nicolas's chronology; Mahan for Gibraltar; Adkin, as Wikipedia cites him; James, copied by Clowes); the scene follows 9 April. The stop at Cádiz: about six hours (Desbrière; Reille) or under four hours (Corbett p. 68). Lauriston's troops: about 3,070 (Desbrière) or 3,500 (James); the Spanish troops 1,930 (Decrès's return) or 1,600 (James); the frigates six (Desbrière) or seven (the British frigates' count). Ganteaume's return to Brest on the day Villeneuve sailed rests on Desbrière alone (p. 5). No source gives the Toulon berth, the 4 p.m. point off Conil, the anchorage of 9 April or the Atlas's longitudes for 4–8 April: all are estimates.
- **The orders the Didon brought.** Decrès's dispatch of 29 April, with a copy of the Emperor's orders of 14 April (Desbrière pp. 31–34; Villeneuve's letter of 1 June). The 35-day wait came from the Emperor's orders; the dispatch pressed the attack on the islands, and its postscript of 1 May the return to Ferrol in any case (see "The plan"). She reached Martinique on 29 May (Mahan), 30 May (Reille), 31 May (Corbett) or 1 June (James).
- **The crossing and the West Indies.** Whether Desbrière's second table of the Atlas's positions carries printed dates or the noon of the day after is genuinely disputed (`research/scene-02/a-h1ac.md`, H1-07), so the crossing has no day marks after the noon of 10 April. Diamond Rock: a garrison of 107 (James) or 128 (Desbrière), surrendered on 2 June (Corbett: 3 June). Magon's junction: at Fort-de-France on 4 June (Desbrière p. 36; Corbett p. 160) or at sea, on the day of sailing or the next (James III 351); his soldiers 861 (Desbrière) or 840 (James). The fleet left Fort-de-France on 5 June (Desbrière) or the 4th (James). The convoy of 8 June: 14 of 15 taken (Reille, Desbrière p. 41; Villeneuve's report; Nelson's "fourteen Sail") or all 15 (James III 351, 353); its escort the schooner Netley under Lieutenant William Carr (Corbett p. 166) or the frigate Barbadoes (Nourse) and the Netley (Harward) (James III 351; Corbett finds no authority for either, p. 166 n.). The turn for Europe: 10 June (Corbett; Desbrière, who has the borrowed troops in the frigates by the 10th) or 11 June (Villeneuve's letter dated at sea; Desbrière once has him sailing that day); James has "the 9th or 10th". Martinique reached on 14 May (Villeneuve's report, Desbrière 1907, pp. 25–26) or 13 May (James, his conversion slip; Clowes gives both).
- **Nelson's pursuit.** His eleven ships in the Mediterranean are inferred by name. He turned west off Palermo on 9 April (his letter of 18 April) or 10 April (his letter dated off Palermo that day). Campbell's intelligence: given as Nelson passed the Strait on 6 May (Campbell's own complaint, Nicolas VI 431 n.; Corbett, James, Clowes) or at Lagos (Mahan, Desbrière). The Martin: "goes off this day" in Nelson's letter of 10 May (VI 432), parted on the afternoon of 11 May by the log (VI 433). Dacres kept four of Cochrane's ships at Jamaica (James, Clowes) or five of six (Corbett); Nelson's 12 at Barbados is not affected. The Amphion's parting on the way home is not recorded. Nelson's signal-log bearing of 13 July (Cape St Vincent S 89° E, 183 leagues) does not fit Faden's track for that day; unresolved. The anchoring at Gibraltar: 19 July (log, Nicolas VI 473; James; Desbrière) or 20 July (Mahan, the day Nelson went ashore). The Termagant off Tarifa on 25 July: 3.30 a.m. (Mahan; the log as read) or "four o'clock" (Nelson to Collingwood, VI 497). The anchoring in the Gulf of Palmas: 26 March (the log, civil; Nicolas VI 374) or 27 March (James; Nicolas's chronology: the log date). Corbett has him at Ustica on 7 April, against his letters, which put him off Palermo on 9–10 April. In Gibraltar Bay on 6 May (the log) or 7 May (James: the log date). Off Cape St Vincent from the evening of 10 May (Corbett, on Hardy's postscript; not in the log) or only after weighing from Lagos at 9.10 a.m. on 11 May (the log); Knight's arrival on 11 May (the log) or 12 May (James, Clowes: the log date). His squadron: ten names (Nicolas VI 432 n.; Corbett p. 108), of which James and Clowes print nine. St John's Road, Antigua, on 12 June (the log) or 13 June (James, Clowes and the Decade's journal: log dates); Tetuan left on 23 July (the log) or "the 24th at noon" (James: the log date).
- **Villeneuve's run home.** The Curieux's sighting on 19 June: 33°12′ N 58° W by Bettesworth's report (Corbett p. 181; James III 354) or about 30°13′ N 58°06′ W on Corbett's chart after Desbrière's; the report puts the fleet two days ahead of the chart's daily marks. Bettesworth reached the Admiralty late on 8 July (Corbett p. 181) or by daylight on the 9th (Mahan, Desbrière). Villeneuve's frigates rejoined about 20 leagues NE of Corvo on 30 June (James), while Reille has Corvo first sighted on 2 July. The run east: from 19 July (Reille, Desbrière p. 57) or the 17th (Corbett p. 195). The gale: Reille's 20 messidor (9 July); James's 9 July is his conversion of the Moniteur letter's 21 messidor (10 July) and matches only by chance. On 12 July Corbett puts the fleet some 300 miles off the coast west of Oporto; his chart has about 365 nm.
- **Cape Finisterre.** Calder's noon: 43°30′ N 11°17′ W (his dispatch), 43°54′ N 11°38′ W (James) or 44°17′ N 11°52′ W at 11.30 a.m. (Desbrière); the Windsor Castle's minutes give 44°10′ N 11°22′ W, no hour (*Naval Chronicle* XIV, p. 168); the French reckoning (13°53′ W) and Corbett's chart (12°20′ W) lie too far west. The allies' course at noon: ESE by compass (Villeneuve's order, Desbrière p. 59) or about east for Ferrol (Corbett p. 196); the two agree, since ESE by compass was 089° true by the 1805 variation. The allied force: 20 of the line, 7 frigates, 2 brigs and the Matilda (James; Troude), or as Calder saw it 20 of the line, three large ships en flûte, five frigates and three brigs. The enemy first reported SSW (Corbett) or SW (James). The firing began at about 5.15 p.m. (the British) or about 5 (Desbrière), and stopped at about 7.30 (Desbrière), nine (Calder at his trial) or 9.30 (James). The prizes struck soon after 8 p.m. (the British) or at daybreak on the 23rd (the French); the San Rafael was an 80 (84 in the Gazette). Losses: British 41 killed and 158 wounded (Calder's return) or 39 and 159 (James's own count); allied 476 (James), 496 (Corbett's table) or about 580 (Desbrière's footnote). The fleets lost sight of each other on the evening of 24 July (James III 365), the morning of the 25th (Corbett p. 205) or the 26th (Mahan).
- **Vigo, A Coruña and Ferrol.** Vigo reached at nightfall on 28 July (Desbrière; Mahan) or on the evening of the 26th (James); the Cíes sighted at 6 p.m. on the 27th or the 28th (Desbrière's sentence is ambiguous); the 27 July letter's 40 leagues WSW of Finisterre fits neither. Vigo left on 31 July or the 30th (James). A Coruña: 2 August (Desbrière, from the French records; Corbett for the junction, p. 219) or 1 August (James, Mahan, Corbett's narrative, p. 218). Out of Ferrol on 13 August (Desbrière; Villeneuve's letter) or the 11th (James). The Príncipe de Asturias: 112 guns (Desbrière) or 120 (Corbett).
- **The turn south and Cádiz.** The day of the turn: nightfall on 15 August (Villeneuve's letter of 22 August, Desbrière p. 112 n.), 16 August (Desbrière's narrative, p. 102), 13 August (his print of the letter, p. 103) or the evening of the 14th (implied by James). Reille's noon of 15 August: about 43°39′ N 11°48′ W if the bearing is true (Corbett, who reads positions true and courses magnetic, p. 261 n.) or about 42°50′ N 12°00′ W by compass with 23–24° W; Villeneuve's 80 leagues is impossible (Corbett); Desbrière's staff map puts the fleet near 44°40′ N 12°25′ W (rejected by Corbett, p. 261 n.). The bend drawn at about 43°45′ N 12°20′ W is an estimate. Off Cape St Vincent on the 17th (Desbrière's text) or the 18th (James; Desbrière's map). The chase of Collingwood on 20 August was led by Magon (Desbrière) or Gravina (Corbett); Collingwood was back off the port that evening with four (Desbrière) or before daylight on the 21st (James). The 10 a.m. position that day: about 36°07′ N 6°32′ W if the bearing of Cádiz, NE nine leagues, is by compass, 36°13′ N 6°42′ W if true. The arrival: 20 August (James, Desbrière, Corbett, Escaño's relation, Collingwood's "yesterday"), 21 August (the Précis; Escaño's later letter to MacDonnell; Austen's log, probably the nautical date) or 17 August (Troude, impossible). Desbrière's staff map "Situation au 15 août" (leaf n126) prints "19 Août" beside the Spanish ships at Cádiz; read as the arrival, it is a day before his own text (p. 107) and James (III 375). The count in port: 35 of the line (James) against Collingwood's "36 sail of men-of-war", which counts frigates.
- **Nelson's September passage.** The sailing: 15 September (the Victory's log; the diary; Beatty, James, Desbrière and Nicolas's chronology) or the 14th (Corbett, who has the signal to weigh made that day, p. 292; the Royal Museums Greenwich), the day he embarked; he was on board at 2 p.m. (the diary as Hudson and Beatty print it), left the beach at two (as Nicolas prints it) or went off to the ship early in the morning (Corbett). The Ajax and Thunderer joined in the morning (Nelson to Marsden) or at noon (the diary). The Decade: the Victory hove to for her from 1.30 to 2.20 p.m. (the log) and spoke her at 2 (the diary); Beatty's noon is when the diary has her first seen. "27 leagues S.W. from Scilly" is drawn by compass; read as true it lies 35 nm WNW, and the mean of the two, as drawn for Cape Sicié, lies between them. The noon latitude of 23 September is drawn as 42°25′ N; the manuscript's "2′5" could be 42°02½′, which the six-hour run from Finisterre in a fresh beam gale would favour. The Rock of Lisbon bearings are worked from Cabo da Roca; taken on the Sintra hills, about 5 nm east, both points would move a few miles east. The Euryalus went ahead at 4 p.m. on the 26th (the diary; James and Beatty give the 26th) with a letter dated the 25th (Nicolas VII 49); Corbett has her sent from off Cape St Vincent (p. 317), and Duff names her among the ships that joined with Nelson (Nicolas VII 70 n.). Nelson heads letters off Cape St Vincent on the 26th and 27th, and Desbrière keeps him there both days (p. 147), but the diary has the Rock of Lisbon NNE 13–14 leagues on the 26th and the cape first in sight at daylight on the 27th. At noon on the 28th the manuscript has nine sail and eighteen at 1 p.m., where Beatty and Nicolas print eighteen at noon. Desbrière gives the San Sebastián bearing of the 28th at 8 a.m. (p. 147), the log at 8 p.m. The arrival: the evening of 28 September (the log, the diary, Nelson's letters, Duff, Codrington, Beatty, James, Corbett, Desbrière), the 29th, the day he took command (Fraser p. 22; some modern accounts), or 27 September (other modern accounts; `research/scene-02/h2-boulogne-cadiz.md`, H2-15). The line reads every bearing with gufm1's variation, which runs 1–3½° above the Victory's own observations of mid-August (Corbett 1910, p. 261 n.); read with 3° less, the Scilly point would move about 4 nm and the Lizard point about half a mile (`research/scene-02/v-h6-september.md`, F18). The Victory's own logs, read only in Nicolas's extracts, would settle the positions and give a latitude and longitude for every noon. When the Victory moved from the Motherbank to St Helens before 14 September is not recorded in the sources read; the line starts at Spithead so as to join the Ushant line.
- **The British off Cádiz, August to October.** The strengths by date, and every version that differs from them (James's and Beatty's 27 on arrival, James's and Clowes's peak of 29, the days of Louis's sailing and return, of the Royal Sovereign, the Prince of Wales, the Donegal and the Leviathan), are under "Fleet sizes at each stage", with their doubts. Besides them: at the end of August the fleet was 26 (Desbrière's corrected sum, p. 145; the parts in James III 379; Allemand's intelligence of 11 September, Desbrière p. 108), with lower counts of the moment (24 or 25 from the San Sebastián tower, p. 145; Austen's "five" with Collingwood, Hubback p. 142); Desbrière's first count of 32 (p. 109), with the Dragon and Goliath joining on the way as Cornwallis's orders intended (Hallowell's letter of 17 August, p. 103 n.; Corbett p. 247), he corrects on pp. 143–145; the Dragon was off Vigo on 10 October (Desbrière p. 108 n.). Ajax and Thunderer joined Nelson off the Lizard (Nelson to Marsden; his diary; Desbrière p. 146) or off Plymouth (James, Beatty). Louis's inshore squadron on 28 September: 6 (the Victory's log; Nelson to Acton), 5 (James III 379; Corbett) or "five or six" (Beatty, p. 2 in the 1807 and 1825 editions). His detachment of 2 October: Nelson wrote of "six Sail of the Line" (to Marsden, VII 63); five are named, and the sixth was most likely the Leviathan, away at Gibraltar until the 8th. On 18 October coast signals told Villeneuve that four of the line had left Gibraltar with a convoy (Desbrière p. 141); Louis had five (Austen). The Prince of Wales: 98 guns (James, Clowes) or 90 (Desbrière, Mahan). The fleet's station: 16–18 leagues west of Cádiz (orders of 4 October), "15 lgs. to twenty" (diary, 14 October) or 15–18 (to Ball, 11 October, Nicolas VII 111). The link squadron's distance east of the fleet: "from three to four leagues" in the order to Duff, four leagues in the letter to Blackwood (`research/scene-02/a-h2a.md`, R1).
- **Boulogne.** The flotilla's counts are right for their own dates: 1,301 craft built in 1803–04 (Desbrière), 2,054 in the spring of 1805 (the embarkation return), 2,293 in seven ports on 20 July 1805 (James; Clowes). Whether and where a line lay in the roads in August 1805, and where Soult's divisions were hutted that summer, are among the open doubts of "Boulogne from the sea". Wimereux and Ambleteuse: both new ports Napoleon had built ("deux nouveaux ports", Corr. IX No. 7832, 2 July 1804), or Wimereux made a port and Ambleteuse's existing harbour deepened and enlarged (James III 217). The two embarkations rest on James alone (III 326). The modern figures of about 60,000 men at Boulogne and over 175,000 in the camps have no source. The craft's rigs: bateaux canonniers and péniches lug-rigged (Desbrière, *Projets* III 90 and the plate facing it), or some gun-vessels schooner-rigged and the péniches mostly schuyts (James III 216–217); "Boulogne from the sea" ("Rigged gun craft, not barges") gives the rigs the check found.
- **The turn east and Ulm.** The postponement: on 23 August, if the fleet were held at Cádiz (No. 9120), or, in Berthier's letter to Marmont as James quotes it, on the 22nd, if the fleets could not reach the Channel (James III 332, from Dumas). When Napoleon knew Villeneuve was in Cádiz: by 31 August at the latest (the postscript of 13 fructidor, Corr. XI 157), 1 September (Corbett p. 278, the same postscript), 1–2 September (Desbrière pp. 115–116, the full dispatches), or about four days after the 22nd (James III 332, with the Daru story, which does not fit the dates). The force to stay at Boulogne: an army of 60,000–80,000 men on the heights and 500 craft able to carry 40,000–50,000 (No. 9209, art. 4; dated 13 September by the editors), 80,000 or 100,000 men encamped as a pretext (art. 5), 500 craft and 80,000 men (Desbrière 1907, p. 120), or 30,000–40,000 men encamped on the heights with the gun-vessels in the basin and road (James III 332). The second divisions marched on 30 August (Berthier's order) or 29 August ("demain" in Napoleon's letter of the 28th); Napoleon left Boulogne on 2 September (Corr. XI No. 9177; Desbrière) or 4 September (James III 332). The Rhine was crossed on 25–30 September by corps (Alombert and Colin) or on 25–26 September in Napoleon's 1st Bulletin; Soult's corps crossed at Rheinhausen on 26–30 September (Alombert and Colin II 42, 507–508), over "the bridge at Spire" on the 26th in the Bulletin. Donauwörth was taken in the night of 6–7 October (Soult's report of the 7th, Alombert and Colin II 868) or at about 8 p.m. on the 6th (Maude pp. 199–200). Napoleon was at Donauwörth on 7 October in the afternoon (Schuermans) or the morning (Maude). The coalition's dates rest on Maude alone: Russia approached Austria in October 1803, the courier with Austria's adherence left Vienna on 7 July 1805 and the two signed a treaty on 9 August (pp. 76, 90), while on 23 August Napoleon still expected Austria's act to be signed "cet hiver" (Corr. XI No. 9117); the treaties and Britain's part were not researched. Ulm: the capitulation signed at noon (Alombert and Colin) or with Berthier coming at 2 p.m. (the Austrian eyewitness); about 23,000 (the eyewitness), 25,365 (Alombert and Colin; Maude), 27,000 (7th Bulletin) or 30,000 (8th Bulletin) laid down their arms, Napoleon's own figures on the 17th having been 15,000 and 20,000; the muskets 13,600 on the page image, 43,600 in the OCR. Some modern summaries give 19 October for Ulm, without period support. The gate given up on 18 October: the Stuttgart gate at 10 a.m. (Alombert and Colin III/1 226) or the "porte Neuve" at 9 (the Austrian eyewitness they print). The army's march (`research/scene-02/session-32/army-march.md`): the head's dates between Boulogne and Metz are derived, not read from the 1st division's own reports. They are the nightly stages of the 40th of the line, in Suchet's 4th division, from J. M. Berjaud's modern chronicle (2009), which gives no source for each entry, shifted five days earlier. Shifted, they match the fixed dates at Mézières (9–11 September, Alombert and Colin II 74–77) and Metz (leaving on the 18th, I 415), and five period dates confirm the 40th's own stages, but a halt may fall a day differently: Berthier varied the halts between divisions to avoid crowding (his letter to Soult of 28 August, I 365). Berjaud's "Livry" between Stenay and Verdun is no place on that road (Liny-devant-Dun or Dun-sur-Meuse is meant); "Courcelles" is taken as Courcelles-Chaussy on the Saint-Avold road, not Courcelles-sur-Nied; Surques as the first night is the 4th division's stop, and the 1st's is not recorded. Metz to Germersheim (18–24 September) is the order's itinerary, not a report of the march; the 4th division carried out its own to the day (Berjaud, against I 416), and the 1st division's advance guard was at the Rhine by noon on the 26th. In that itinerary the date column's first line is printed "1er vendémiaire", a misprint for the 1st complementary day (18 September), which the order's own heading gives. Which division led: Saint-Hilaire's 1st as far as Heilbronn (its advance guard crossed the Rhine first), Vandamme's 2nd from Öhringen to Donauwörth. Thiébault, a brigadier in the 1st division, gives the road as Speyer, Heilbronn, Hall, Ellwangen, Nördlingen and Donauwörth (Memoirs II 128), but his dates in that passage are confused, so only the road is used. Where Soult's divisions were hutted in the summer of 1805 was not found ("Boulogne from the sea", "Where they stood"), so the line starts at the town. The positions are present-day town centres: Günzburg's is the old town, south of the Danube, and the bridge Malher's division took was just north of it.
- **Cádiz: orders, Rosily and the fleet in port.** The orders of 14 September reached Cádiz on 27 September (Decrès's report) or the 28th (Desbrière p. 136; James; Clowes; Corbett, as Nelson appeared off Cape St Vincent). Rosily's instructions: 17 September (No. 9230) or the 18th (Desbrière's narrative). The orders and the "pusillanimité" letter: 14 and 15 September (Nos. 9210, 9220), or one letter of 17 September (James III 378; Clowes V 128 dates the orders the same day). The council of war of 8 October: fourteen officers signed (Desbrière 1907, annexes 97–100), the Spanish brigadier Hore among them, whom Desbrière's narrative leaves out; the argument that the allied crews were unpractised was Escaño's (annexes 97–100) or Prigny's (Corbett p. 324). Rosily reached Madrid in the night of 10–11 October (Beurnonville), on the 10th (Desbrière p. 128) or the 12th (Corbett, the date of Rosily's own letter), and Cádiz on 23 October (expected, Beurnonville) or by the 25th (his first letter from Cádiz), after the fleet had sailed; the date line, which ends at dawn on 21 October, does not show it. Villeneuve heard Rosily was in Madrid on 18 October (his own letter; Desbrière; Corbett) or about 15–16 October (Fraser p. 63). Magon's planned night sortie of 18 October: four French and three Spanish of the line and the Hermione (Villeneuve's letter, Desbrière annexes 101; Corbett) or three ships (Desbrière p. 141). Where the fleet lay in late September and early October: in the inner harbour, the Argonauta between the Puntal and Matagorda forts (Escaño); its fastest ships brought down to just within the harbour mouth within ten days of arrival (Fraser p. 29); still packed in the harbour about 29 September (Corbett p. 318); at anchor in the outer harbour, apparently ready for sea, on 1 October (the Euryalus, James III 380). Corbett also reads Escaño as keeping the ships crowded up in the harbour from about 2 October, for fear of an attack (p. 322); Villeneuve wrote on 2 October that a berth further in might have been safer, but that he would not take it, since weighing from it would be harder still (annexes 95). British counts of the fleet in port from the offing ran from 34 to 36 (Nelson 35 or 36 on 28 September; the Euryalus and Blackwood 34).
- **The anchorage, 10–19 October.** No source fixes the ships' places. The layout rests on Escaño ("fuera del tiro de la plaza"), the French "entre les Porques et le Diamant", Nelson's "Harbours Mouth", the modern depths of `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` and Faden's plan of 1797, which shows the Spanish moorings of 1797, not those of 1805; the sheet does not say whether its soundings are fathoms or Spanish brazas. In the narrows the Fougueux is the one ship any source places, by her own reports (Desbrière 1907, annexes 213, 216); the fort she names on the Puerto Real shore may be Matagorda or Fort San Luis, either way in or just inside the narrows. The ships furthest in that the Thémis reports still at anchor on the 19th (annexes 273), and the ships Fraser has towed or warped toward the sea that evening (pp. 84–85, no source given), are not named, and there is no evidence for the San Justo there; Faden's plan draws two Spanish ships between the forts in 1797. Where the Fougueux's warp ended, across from the city by her second report (annexes 216), is not given. Which ships weighed and anchored again on the morning of the 19th is not recorded, nor are the two ships of the line the Euryalus saw at 8 without their topsails hoisted: the Spanish rear-admiral's, perhaps the Santísima Trinidad, Cisneros's flagship, and one unnamed, perhaps the Fougueux (NRS II 146; James III 386; low for both). The frigates' and brigs' berths are not recorded, but for the Thémis at the head of the anchorage (annexes 273); the Observateur may have lain by the Bucentaure, whose orders her captain carried that morning, or with Dumanoir's squadron near the Formidable and the Rayo, her station in the line of 6 October (annexes 124, 268). The outermost ships are drawn 0.25 nm north-east of the point `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` (H2-36d) gives them, which lies on the modern buoy line; its midpoint between the forts falls on Natural Earth's coastline, which has no Matagorda shore, so the chart draws no narrows.
- **The ships left in port.** No source gives the berths of the Terrible and the San Fulgencio. Gravina had the Terrible disarmed and put the San Fulgencio in dock to survey her bottom (annexes 84); Desbrière puts the Terrible in dock too (p. 134), where his source, Lauriston's letter, says only that she would be sent back in (annexes 112), and Fernández Duro has her exchanged for one of the ships laid up in the arsenal (VIII 308). The arsenal of La Carraca had the bay's only dry docks, so both most likely lay there (low). James makes the San Fulgencio the one ship that did not move down to the harbour mouth on 9–10 October (III 385); the ship the British saw stay behind may as well have been the Fougueux (low for either). The two Spanish ships judged worthless in August are not named, and their place in October is unknown; one of them, or the Terrible or the San Fulgencio, may explain the Euryalus's "16 Spanish" in the outer harbour on 1 October, one more than sailed (James III 380), or it may be a miscount (low). Whether both the corvettes Desbrière names, the Mercurio and the Indagadora, came from Ferrol with the frigate Flora (his sum and Escaño's count one corvette, Desbrière p. 99, annexes 7), and where they lay on the 19th, is not recorded; a modern summary has the Flora at Cádiz in 1808. Whether the French schooner Téméraire, which joined at Ferrol, ever reached Cádiz is not recorded (Desbrière p. 98).
- **Gunboats on 19 October.** The Spanish sources have the gunboat stations withdrawn on the 18th, the guns taken out of the fleet's launches and the small craft hoisted in (Escaño, annexes 100; Gravina's report, annexes 145; the Montañés and the Príncipe de Asturias, annexes 372, 383; Fernández Duro VIII 314). James alone has a strong force of gunboats drawn up in line across the harbour mouth that evening, as a preparatory measure (III 385); he gives no source, and the Euryalus's log of the 18th has nothing of it (NRS II 145). Both can hold only if James's gunboats were the port's own, which no other source places on the 18th or 19th. The chart draws none. High for the fleet's launches; low for James's line.
- **The sortie.** The signal to weigh on the 19th went up at first light, 5–6 a.m. (the French), or at 7 (James). Ships of the line out that day: seven (Philibert, the Héros, the Montañés, Desbrière) against "six à sept" (the Rhin), "7 ou 8" (the Fougueux), eight French (the Observateur), eight, one of them Spanish (Villeneuve's postscript, annexes 102–103), six and a frigate (the Príncipe de Asturias, annexes 383), 14 sail of ships and frigates (the Furet, annexes 174), four at 4.30 p.m. (the Thémis), nine (Prigny, from memory), eleven of the line at noon (the Naiad, from 11 or 12 miles off) and twelve (James and Nicolas, from the Euryalus's count of all sail). The Algésiras at 4 p.m.: 36°33.0′ N 6°22.0′ W from Philibert's bearings; the 36°33.8′ N of `research/scene-02/a-h2c.md` (H2-38b) is a computing slip. On the 20th the signal to weigh came at 6 a.m. (the Montañés, the Observateur) or 6.30 (Desbrière), and the whole fleet was out at about 8–8.30 a.m. (the Observateur, the Montañés, the Fougueux), about 11 (Desbrière p. 163) or shortly before noon (Corbett p. 337), the last two describing the fleet formed. The afternoon wear: between 1 and 3 p.m. (Desbrière p. 164, many reports saying 3), the van on the starboard tack after 1 (the Sirius's master's log, NRS II 313), 3.30 (the Sirius's log as Nicolas prints it, VII 134), about 4 (Corbett; Prigny), or still on the larboard tack until a tack toward the Strait at about 5 (James III 388); in three columns (Prigny, the Fougueux, Desbrière) or five (James; the Spanish official report, per Nicolas). The Santísima Trinidad: 130 guns (Nicolas VII 130), 136 (`research/dawn-setting.md`) or 140 (Desbrière p. 134). On the 20th Nelson was told of "near forty Sail" outside, an overcount.
- **The morning and evening of 19 October.** How many weighed that morning: the Euryalus counted 19 of the enemy under way at 8 and the Argus 17 under sail at 9, many anchoring again in the calm (NRS II 146; annexes 268), against Desbrière's narrative, in which only the most advanced ships tried and the rest stayed at anchor (p. 160); the eyewitnesses are followed here. Which ships weighed and anchored again is not recorded, and that the seven who got out were the outermost ships, and only those, is an inference: several were of Magon's division, named for the night sortie of the 18th, which fits outer berths, but Desbrière notes that the seven came from several squadrons (annexes 101, 229; Desbrière p. 161 n. 3). The Hermione's hours are left blank in her report (annexes 242). The evening: the Hortense has several ships and all the frigates beating outside the roads from the 19th, the Cornélie part of the fleet already weighed by daybreak on the 20th on the signal of the day before, and the Argus 9 of the line and 5 frigates under sail at 5–5.15 p.m., in a sentence its comma makes ambiguous (annexes 159, 188–189, 268); against them, the Montañés has the whole squadron at anchor at nightfall and the Príncipe de Asturias the calm lasting all afternoon and night, perhaps of the Spanish ships only (annexes 373, 383). So the Cornélie and Hortense probably went out that evening (medium); the Swiftsure, signalled at 4 p.m. with the frigates (annexes 174), and one more ship of the line may have weighed too, which would fit Prigny's nine.
- **The combined fleet's night, 20–21 October.** It wore and stood north-west after the Aigle signalled 18 British ships in line to the south at 7.30 p.m. (James III 388); it wore twice (Beatty, p. 10 in both editions; Nicolas VII 136, after Beatty); or the two reports of its going about that reached Nelson were wrong and it stayed on the starboard tack, standing S to SSW (Desbrière p. 178; the French reports); Corbett makes the mean course about south (p. 338). At nightfall it was at most 8–10 miles south-west of Cádiz (Desbrière p. 164): about 36°25′ N 6°27′ W if south-west is true, 36°23.5′ N 6°23.5′ W if by compass. Its midnight position is interpolated, and with the dawn position implies a track of about 150° true (the drawn centroid of all 33; 147° to the main body's centre) against the signalled 193°.
- **The British, 19–21 October.** At noon on the 20th Cádiz bore NE 9 leagues (Nelson's diary): 36°07′ N 6°33′ W if by compass, 36°13′ N 6°43′ W if true; James has 8–9 leagues, Corbett about 20 miles, Desbrière about 25 (p. 177), Clowes about 28 (V 130). The turn north at 4 p.m. was a wear (James III 390) or a tack at 4.40 (Desbrière p. 178). The evening wear to the south-west came at 8 (Corbett p. 340 n.) or 8.40 (James III 390). The morning wear came at 4 a.m. (the diary; the logs) or 2 a.m. (Beatty's narrative, p. 11 in both editions, against his own appendix); its course was N by E (the Victory's log) or "to the N.E." (Nelson's diary). Desbrière's midnight estimate, about 20 miles south-west of Cádiz (p. 178), cannot be joined to the dawn positions to better than 5–15 nm (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-32i), and no position of the night is better fixed than that.
- **The relay of 19 October.** Which ships of the line passed No. 370: the Defence and Colossus are inferred; the Agamemnon was stationed in the cordon (Beatty, p. 6; James III 389), but nothing shows she relayed it (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R5–R6). How long it took: about 2½ hours on the logged clocks, "within two hours" (Blackwood), about 3 by Browne's 10 a.m., or about 3¼ with Corbett's 45-minute clock offset (R10). The arrival at the Victory: 9.30 (the diary; Beatty), 10 (Browne) or "about nine" (Walker). The Euryalus's repeat to the Phoebe: 7.20 by the logs; Corbett's ten minutes later (7.10) has no support, and at 7.10 the Euryalus made only a general signal, No. 108, close nearer the Admiral (NRS II 160; R9). The Phoebe's course: "to the northward" by the Sirius's log at 7, against her bearing WNW of the Euryalus at 8. No. 370's wording: "enemy's ships are coming out of port" (the NRS editor, from the 1799 book), "or getting under weigh" (Cumby) or "or getting under sail" (Corbett). Where the Victory lay at 9.30: about 36°33′ N 7°18′ W by Nelson's 16 leagues ESE by compass, against the noon log position, 36°36′ N 7°30′ W, 19 leagues off, although the fleet had chased east in light airs since 9.30. The distance: 16 leagues (the diary, the noon letter, Blackwood, Browne), about fifty miles (Corbett and Mahan, rounding) or "twenty leagues" (Robinson, 1858, a late memory); the bearing ESE (the diary's manuscript; the noon letter) against "E.N.E." (a copying slip in Nicolas VII 133) and the "W.S.W." of James (III 389) and Nicolas's narrative. Where the Defence lay at 8 a.m.: about 10 nm south of Cádiz's parallel by the Euryalus's masthead bearing ("west"; `research/scene-02/a-h2b.md`, H2-37f), or on it by Nelson's "in the latitude" order and her usual station (`research/scene-02/a-h2c.md`, H2-40c). The Colossus lay with the Mars by the diary of 14 October; only the memorandum of 20 October puts her between the Defence and the Mars, for the night chain. The Sirius's first-light position is not logged: the nearest entry is the 4 a.m. bearing, "Cadiz NE by E, 4 miles" (NRS II 312), and she telegraphed the topsail yards at 6.04 by the Euryalus's signal log. The Mars's distant signal of a flag, a ball and a pendant rests on one eyewitness writing years later (Cumby); no printed description of the Distant Signals of 1805 was found. The second signal down the chain, that the enemy's fleet was at sea: about 2 p.m. (Beatty), 3 p.m. (the diary; Nicolas VII 131) or by 3 (Corbett). If the Victory's clock ran about 45 minutes slow on the 19th (an inference, R10), her 9.30 was about 10.15 local time, with the sun about 37° up rather than 32°. The British strength: 27 of the line off Cádiz (Nicolas VII 130; Browne, NRS II 194; Cumby 1899, p. 718), 23–24 of them with Nelson; Walker's 26 under all sail after the chase signal (NRS II 323) is the low count (R7). Corbett (1910, p. 333) calls the Mars "Duff's easternmost ship"; the diary of 14 October and Beatty (1807, p. 7) put her at the fleet's end of the chain, and Corbett's own note puts the Defence and Agamemnon nearest Cádiz (p. 334 n. 2), so "easternmost" is a slip (`research/scene-02/v-h3b.md`, H3-17d).
- **The signals of the night of 20–21 October.** The 5 p.m. signal: 5 by the Victory, 5.40 by the Euryalus (NRS II 149), "a little before sunset" (Beatty 1807, p. 9); the Naiad's report of 31 sail at 5 (the original diary; Beatty's appendix), "shortly afterwards" (James) or 6 p.m. (Nicolas VII 137, from a facsimile). Whether the frigates also used the 1799 book's tack signals (Nos. 28–29) besides Nelson's code, and whether the Sirius's three guns at 9.35 p.m. meant that the enemy was standing to the westward (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R18); the six guns she heard then may be No. 31 repeated by two flagships, an inference only (R16, R25). The Euryalus's midnight bearing of the enemy: SE by S (NRS II 149) or SE by E (Nicolas VII 134 n.). The Sirius was 4 miles off the enemy at midnight (NRS II 313) or 6 at 11.30 (Nicolas VII 134 n.). Whether the Victory burned blue lights at the 4 a.m. wear: no log says, and Corbett calls the blue lights Nelson's custom; the Africa saw gun flashes WSW at 4 (NRS II 297).
- **Signals: colours, books and ranges.** The 1804 values of the numeral flags (Perrin's Plate XIII) were not transcribed, and the day *Signal Book* of 1799 was not read; numeral 1 of 1799 is plain red in the copy FOTW describes, yellow-red-yellow in Perrin. Which half of Popham's telegraph flag was red: Popham names only "a diagonal red and white flag" (1803, p. 6); Perrin has white and red, Wilson red over white, Kent white over red, and Barraclough found both in contemporary books (all via FOTW). The hoists held along the chain until the Victory's answer ran back come from Popham (1803, p. 6) and the *Signal-Book* of 1796; the wear lights (two at the ensign staff, one at the bowsprit end), the form of No. 31 (three guns and two lights, or a square of four lanterns) and the one-light answer come from the 1799 *Night Signals* (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R16, R25). No log of 19–21 October records them, except that on the 20th the Agamemnon kept the signal up (NRS II 300). All ranges are estimates: flag colours readable at about 4–11 nm, lanterns at 1–3 nm, blue lights at 5–14 nm (`research/scene-02/a-h3.md`, R21–R22). The blue light's burn is inferred from the false fire's, the only one defined (Smyth 1867). The colour of the British fires, which French observers remarked on (the Cornélie, the Argus; Lucas), is read as bluish blue lights against their own yellow lanterns and gold rockets.
- **Dawn, 21 October.** The British lay west, slightly south of west, of Cape Trafalgar, 20–21 nm off (Collingwood's "E by S about seven leagues"; the logs; `research/dawn-setting.md`, 1.2), or about 21 miles north-west of it (Desbrière p. 179). The combined fleet lay about 8–12 nm off the coast by the British bearings, its northern end about 7–9 nm, or 18–24 nm off by the French (Dumanoir; the Scipion's "8 à 9 lieues" at noon); Villeneuve's own reckoning (Cape Trafalgar ESE about four leagues; Fraser 1906, pp. 110–111) puts his centre about 2½ nm north-west of the scene 1 position. At first light and at sunrise the British steered N by E by compass, about 350° true (the Victory's log); they bore up after signal No. 76 at 6.50 by her clock (about 06:40–07:00 in scene 1's notes) to ENE, about 047° true, then E by N (058°) from 8 a.m. Single British ships lay 3–4 nm from the Victory at 4 a.m. (the Mars, the Ajax); no source says how far apart the two British groups were. The Africa's position, Cape Trafalgar SE 8–9 leagues at 6 a.m., about 36°21′ N 6°30′ W, is low. The distance between the fleets at daylight runs from 6 to 12 nm in the sources: 10–12 from the Victory by her logs (9–10 at 8 a.m.), James and Browne, about 12 by Beatty, four leagues by the Neptune and the Britannia, 9 by the Belleisle and an officer quoted by Fraser, about 8 by Wheeler (1995), 6–7 by Collingwood, about 2½ leagues by Villeneuve, about three by the Algésiras and "dos leguas" by the Montañés, while Gravina's squadron, to windward and so nearer, had the British 4–5 miles off; the scene uses 9–12 nm from the Victory, as scene 1 does. The line ran NNE–SSW by compass by the British (the Victory's log; Corbett), south-east to north-west by Lucas of the Redoutable (Fraser 1906, p. 112) (`research/dawn-setting.md`, sections 1.1 and 7, doubts 1 and 7).
- **The small craft and frigates at dawn.** No log fixes any of them at first light, and every place drawn is an estimate. The Pickle is last logged in company with the Sirius at midnight; at 8 the Sirius lay a mile from the Victory, so by then the two had parted (NRS II 313, 317). The Pickle's distances run high: at 8 a.m. she had the enemy E, 4 or 5 leagues, and the Commander-in-Chief WSW, 2 or 3 leagues in Nicolas's printing of her log (NRS II 317; Nicolas VII 145 n.), when the Euryalus put the British van 8 or 9 miles from the enemy's centre (NRS II 149); a modern summary of her manuscript log gives a noon reckoning, Cádiz N 30° E 28 miles, that fits neither (maritimeviews.co.uk). The Entreprenante may have kept by the Victory as the admiral's tender or lain between the two British bodies; Collingwood's printed order of attack lists both small craft with the van column (Nicolas VII 215), Pocock's painting of the opening of the battle shows them attending Nelson's line (RMG BHC0548), and the Harbin plan draws them between the two columns (Corbett 1910, frontispiece), all of later in the morning. The Argus may have been anywhere along the allied line after carrying Gravina's word down it in the night, and her dawn bearing of the British, NNW to NE, fits no other report (annexes 269). The Rhin's place is not recorded.
- **A third small sail with the British.** The Orion's master logged "a brig" with the British fleet besides the schooner and the cutter (NRS II 278), and the French Hermione at first light and the Hortense at 6.45 counted three small craft among the British (annexes 189, 243); every other British list or log, and Prigny, the Aigle, the Redoutable and the French Swiftsure, give two or fewer (annexes 141, 198, 224, 247). One reading makes one of the Hermione's three a ship of the line misjudged at a distance, perhaps the Africa, since her total, 33, is the British naval total; it does not explain the Hortense, who counted all 27 of the line. The other makes the third sail the laden merchant brig the Agamemnon detained on the 19th and towed on the 20th, casting her off when the enemy was sighted (NRS II 147–148, 299–300; James III 387): with a prize crew she could have followed the fleet. Nothing places her on the 21st, and Desbrière's identification of her with the American ship the Héros took into Cádiz on the 20th is doubtful (pp. 162–163; NRS II 147, 313). She was no warship, and the chart does not draw her. Low–medium for a third sail; low for its identity.
- **Not consulted first-hand.** Tofiño's own plan of Cádiz (only Faden's English copy of 1797); the Victory's master's log for 1–21 October (ADM 52); the Spanish official report in full; the treaties of the Third Coalition and Britain's part in it; the Victory's logs for 15–28 September beyond Nicolas's extracts (her captain's log, ADM 51/4514, and the master's private log kept from 19 September, at Pembroke College, Oxford) and the modern biographies of Nelson (Sugden, Knight, Adkins, Goodwin) for that passage; Adkin's *Trafalgar Companion* (2005), known only as Wikipedia cites it; the *Précis des événements militaires*, known only as James quotes it (III 375); Faden's plate of the coast of Boulogne with the new works (1805), the likeliest English chart to letter Vimereux; the plates of Serres's *Little Sea Torch* (1801), known only by their catalogued titles; Winfield's registers of British and of French warships, known only as threedecks.org and Wikipedia report them; the Pickle's manuscript log (ADM 52/3669), known only from a modern summary.
- **The 1805 names.** Each disputed form, with who used it, is under "The names on the chart". Three of the beats' forms may be misread. Spire names Speyer, not a church: Spire on Wilkinson's two plates of 1802 and Cary's *Netherlands* (1804), and in Brookes (1807); Speyer on Laurie & Whittle's post roads (1802), and today; Spires in the English accounts of the crossing of the Rhine in 1805. Vimereux is Wimereux, which no English chart that was read letters: Vimereux in the *Naval Chronicle* of 1805; Wimereux once there, for a privateer, and today. Donawerth is Donauwörth, spelled four ways on six English plates of 1801–1806: Donawerth (Arrowsmith 1804; Cary 1804), Donauwert (Wilkinson 1802; Laurie & Whittle 1802), Donawert (Cary 1801) and Donauwerth (Wilkinson 1806); the usage of 1805 favours Donawerth, and Donauwerth is the fallback. The other medium rulings: C. Grisnez (Gris Nez on Knight's chart of 1806; Cape Griznez in Malham), Heilbronn (Heilbron on two plates of 1802; one n in nearly all the print of 1805) and St. Helens (St. Helens Road on Cary's plates of 1801 and 1807).

Winfield, *British Warships in the Age of Sail 1793–1817* (2008), and R. Winfield and S. S. Roberts, *French Warships in the Age of Sail 1786–1861* (2015), not read directly, as threedecks.org (https://threedecks.org/) and Wikipedia's articles on the vessels report them (the small craft's dimensions; the Weazle's class and commander); maritimeviews.co.uk, "The prelude" (https://www.maritimeviews.co.uk/trafalgar/the-prelude/), a summary of the Pickle's manuscript log (ADM 52/3669; her noon reckoning of 21 October); Wikipedia, "Arsenal de La Carraca" (its place and its dry docks; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_de_La_Carraca); Hispanopedia, "Batalla de la Poza de Santa Isabel" (the Flora at Cádiz in 1808; https://es.hispanopedia.com/wiki/Batalla_de_la_Poza_de_Santa_Isabel); the notes in `research/scene-02/session-17/` list them with what each gives.
- Data and tools: Natural Earth 10m land and minor islands (the chart's coastline, against which every position and route was checked): https://www.naturalearthdata.com/downloads/10m-physical-vectors/; EMODnet Bathymetry (modern depths): https://rest.emodnet-bathymetry.eu/; NOAA NCEI magnetic declination, model gufm1 at 1805: https://www.ngdc.noaa.gov/geomag/calculators/magcalc.shtml; OpenStreetMap, the Cádiz entrance marks (https://www.openstreetmap.org/#map=15/36.555/-6.29) and the rocks off San Martiño (https://www.openstreetmap.org/way/638538031, https://www.openstreetmap.org/way/292616904), read through Overpass: https://overpass-api.de/; NOAA, ETOPO 2022 global relief (the chart's heights and its 15″ tiles): https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/products/etopo-global-relief-model; OpenStreetMap Nominatim (the army's waypoints, present-day town centres): https://nominatim.openstreetmap.org/
